Every day, unnoticed by the world, men and women perform great deeds, speak words of wisdom, and endure sorrows with quiet nobility. Most of these hidden heroes will remain unknown until that final hour when the great are humbled and the humble exalted. Yet even those recorded in dusty annals sleep forgotten, their lives reduced to cold, lifeless lines that stir no hearts. For history, without flesh and breath, is but a skeleton—striking the reader’s mind only to glance away, unremembered.

Thus, the truths of the past remain sealed to common folk, for chroniclers leave too much to imagination—and imagination is a rare gift. Here, then, the storyteller may serve as a bridge, breathing life into the long-dead.

In a musty chronicle, written in stiff and grating Latin, lies a brief, harsh account of two souls who lived and died unsung four centuries ago. Their names, etched without pity into that stern page, lie as forgotten as fossils in stone. Unjust in life, Fate remains unjust in death. Yet if I can but draw back the veil of those dry words, perhaps you will grant them, for a day, a place in your heart.

It was the middle of the fifteenth century—a time when Louis XI ruled France with cunning, Edward IV sat uneasy on England’s contested throne, and Philip the Good, having crushed his cousin Jacqueline and broken her spirit, reigned unchallenged in Holland. Here, our tale begins.

In the small town of Tergou lived Elias, a cloth and leather merchant, and his wife Catherine. They were prosperous, their trade thriving, yet earthly cares weighed upon them—not for want of coin, but for the burden and blessing of nine children.

When the little ones came, one after another, each was welcomed with joy, each a gift from Heaven. But as the years passed and the family grew, so too did the parents’ fears. In Holland, where prudence was second nature, reckless fathers were as rare as disobedient sons. And so, as the table stretched with each new mouth, Elias and Catherine often exchanged uneasy glances, whispering, *Who will feed them when we are gone?*

The younger children, trusting in the eternal return of supper as surely as the sunrise, could scarce contain their laughter at such worries. But the older ones, marked by Dutch solemnity, took the words to heart—some with resolve, others with darker thoughts.

One day, Richart, the second eldest, stood before his mother, his face calm but his voice firm. *"Send me to Amsterdam,"* he said. *"Let me learn the merchant’s trade. I am quick with numbers, and I would not be a burden."*

Catherine’s hands flew to her cheeks. *"Leave Tergou?"*

*"What are streets and stones to me?"* he replied. *"If I can leave the people, I can leave the place."*

*"Mother,"* he said gravely, *"if I can leave you, I can leave anyone."*

She wept, but Richart’s will was iron. And so, with a heart of granite and a face of sorrow, he departed, costing them nothing more than the last of their small hoard.

Two years passed. Richart found a place for his brother Jacob, and Jacob too left, slipping away after dinner one day. That evening, Elias whispered, *"Sit wider,"* and Catherine, who had vowed not to weep, broke her promise. *"The best always leave us,"* she sobbed.

*"Nay,"* said Elias, *"all are dear alike. But men are ungrateful by nature—and women foolish."*

*"Yet Richart and Jacob were the flower of us all,"* she insisted.

Again the little coffer was drained, and again they saved like ants, denying themselves to fill it anew. In time, two more children were settled—a son as a tailor, a daughter as a seamstress—but the cost left them nearly bare.

Now only the unwilling and the unable remained: Giles, the dwarf, half-witted and sharp-clawed, clung to by his mother; Little Catherine, pale and sweet, who moved on crutches and never complained; Sybrandt, the idle youngest; and Cornelis, the eldest, who waited like a vulture for his inheritance.

*"What will become of them when we are gone?"* the couple often sighed—until, at last, their prayers seemed answered. *"Thanks be to St. Bavon,"* they said, *"there is Gerard."*

Gerard was different. A scholar, a dreamer, destined for the Church. His hands were made for pen and parchment, not for trade. He spent his days bent over books, his nights tracing letters by candlelight, even when the burgomaster himself slept. The monks admired his skill, the old painter Margaret Van Eyck became his friend, and though his family barely understood his passion, they indulged it—for the Church would provide.

When Duke Philip offered prizes for the finest craftsmanship in the land, Gerard’s heart leaped. He would compete—illuminate a manuscript, prove his worth. His mother, after much fretting, gave him the coins for vellum and colors. His sister Kate prayed to the Virgin for his success.

And so, with hope in his heart and his tools in his pack, Gerard set out for Rotterdam—unaware that Fate, which had been so cruel to his obscure kindred, had not yet finished with them.

Here is my rewritten version of Chapter II from *The Cloister and the Hearth*, tailored for a modern reader while preserving the historical tone and charm of the original:

---

“The soup is hot,” said Gerard.

“But how are we to get it to our mouths?” sighed the old scholar, his shoulders drooping.

Margaret’s eyes sparkled with quiet amusement. “Father, the young man has brought us straws.”

“Ah, yes,” the old man murmured, “but my bones are stiff, and the fire is too fierce to kneel beside with such short reeds. By St. John the Baptist, the lad is clever!”

For while he spoke, Gerard had already solved the problem. With swift fingers, he untied the knot at his breast, removed his hat, and placed a stone in each corner. Then, wrapping his hand in the hem of his jerkin, he deftly lifted the flask from the flames, nestled it between the stones, and presented the makeshift bowl beneath the old man’s nose with a cheerful grin. Trembling, the scholar bent forward and sipped through the straw.

His face, drawn and weary, transformed before their eyes—color returning, his gaze brightening. He drew a long breath and exhaled triumphantly.

“Hippocrates and Galen!” he declared. “A *soupe au vin*—the very elixir of life! Blessed be the nation that conceived it, the woman who brewed it, and the young man who brings it to starving souls. Here, child, take your turn while I enlighten our benefactor on the virtues of this sovereign remedy.”

Margaret pressed the hat into his hands. “Father, spare us the lecture—at least until the soup is drunk!”

The old man chuckled but obliged, and soon the meal was finished. As they prepared to resume their journey, Gerard found himself fumbling with the ribbon Catherine had tied. Margaret watched his struggles with quiet amusement before offering her help.

Her fingers, deft and graceful, worked the stubborn knot, her auburn hair glinting silver in the fading light. Gerard stood motionless, a strange warmth spreading through him as her touch lingered—a sensation entirely new, as if the world had shifted beneath his feet.

When at last she stepped back to admire her handiwork, she found his gaze fixed upon her with such intensity that she faltered, cheeks flushing, and retreated to her father’s side.

They entered Rotterdam through the Schiedamze Poort, but their paths soon diverged—Gerard bound for the Hooch Straet and the Stadthouse, while the scholar and his daughter turned toward the Ooster-Waagen Straet. Their farewells were swift, and Gerard was left adrift in the bustling city, his heart heavy with sudden loneliness.

*I shall never see her again,* he thought bitterly. *What cruel fate brings beauty, wit, and kindness across my path only to snatch them away?*

Distracted, he wandered until a crowd drew him toward a meadow by the Maas, where games and revelry unfolded. Jugglers, wrestlers, and trained animals vied for attention, but Gerard’s thoughts were elsewhere. Hearing that the Duke had retired to the Stadthouse, he hastened there—only to find the gates barred by a pompous official.

Then, to his astonishment, he spotted Margaret and her father pleading for entry.

“My cousin the alderman is within,” the old man insisted, offering a written plea.

The gatekeeper sneered. “I am no messenger. Stand aside.”

Margaret turned, her voice trembling. “Good people, we have traveled far. Must we beg at the door like strangers?”

The crowd jeered.

Before she could shrink further, Gerard seized her hand.

She gasped—a sound of relief, of startled joy—and clutched his fingers tightly.

A bystander laughed. “Ho! She’s found her sweetheart!”

Margaret whirled, eyes blazing. “I have no sweetheart! But I know a true friend when I see one—which is more than you louts understand!”

The crowd fell silent, chastened.

Gerard seized the moment. “I am a competitor,” he announced.

The gatekeeper scowled but consulted his list. “Gerard Eliassoen may enter.”

“Not without my friends.”

“They are not your company.”

“Then I stay.”

A stalemate—until Gerard, with a grin, bellowed at the top of his lungs:

The gatekeeper blanched. “Madman! The Duke is within earshot!”

“Then open the gate,” Gerard said sweetly.

With a frantic wave, the porter ushered them through, and they emerged into a scene of dazzling opulence—tables laden with delicacies, guests in silks and jewels, music weaving through the air.

The Duke himself, seated high above, acknowledged them with a nod.

As they feasted—on roasted boars with gleaming tusks, sugared castles, wines like liquid rubies—Gerard found himself lost in Margaret’s presence. The world around them faded; even the food held no taste compared to the pleasure of her company.

“What do you admire most here?” she asked softly.

He did not hesitate. “You.”

She flushed, but did not pull away.

And as the minstrels played and the fountain sparkled, Gerard knew one truth with absolute certainty:

This was no world of sin and misery.

Not while she was by his side.

---

This version maintains the original's charm while streamlining some of the dialogue and enhancing emotional beats for modern readability. Let me know if you'd like any further refinements!

A stately seneschal, his hair as white as frost, approached their table with measured steps. His eyes swept over the company before settling on Gerard.

“Young sir,” he said, with a courteous bow, “are you Gerard Eliassoen?”

Gerard, surprised, confirmed it.

“The Princess Marie requests your presence.”

A ripple of murmurs spread through those nearby. Faces turned, eyes sharp with envy, fixed upon the young man summoned by royalty. Gerard rose at once.

Margaret, though her fingers tightened slightly on the edge of the table, kept her voice steady. “I suppose we shan’t see you again.”

He bent low, whispering, “This is my good princess—but you are my queen.” Then, aloud: “Wait for me. I will return.”

Peter, roused from his doze, mumbled agreement.

Once Gerard was gone, the pair—plainly dressed yet suddenly the center of attention—found themselves scrutinized by curious onlookers. William Johnson, seizing the moment, stepped forward with exaggerated surprise.

“Cousins! And to think I was right behind you all this while!”

Margaret’s reply was cool. “I saw you long ago.”

“And yet you did not speak?”

“It was for you to welcome us to Rotterdam, as we would welcome you in Sevenbergen. Your servant barred your door to us.”

Johnson flushed. “The fool!”

Margaret’s gaze was steady. “I wished to see whether the master was like the man.”

Stung, Johnson acted swiftly. “Come home with me now, and judge for yourself whether you are welcome.”

Margaret hesitated. “We must wait for Gerard. He doesn’t know where we lodge.”

Johnson waved a dismissive hand. “My secretary will wait for him and bring him along. You needn’t worry.”

She wavered—then agreed.

---

The seneschal led Gerard through torch-lit corridors, past young nobles lounging at their posts, their cards and dice forgotten as they eyed the stranger with open disdain.

“The Princess has retired,” one drawled.

“Come back tomorrow,” said another, rolling the dice.

But the seneschal only smiled and pressed on.

In an antechamber, ladies in silk and jewels paused their chatter as Gerard entered. Their eyes pinned him like moths to a board. A duenna, stern-faced, rescued him, ushering him into the presence of the Countess of Charolois and her daughter, Princess Marie.

The Countess, seated with needlework in hand, greeted him warmly.

“Margaret Van Eyck is an old friend,” she said. “I am glad to receive her letter—and gladder still that you brought it safely.”

The young princess, bright-eyed, added eagerly, “And the miniature! I thank you, sir.”

The Countess’s smile deepened. “Now, how shall we repay you?”

Gerard, overwhelmed, could only murmur, “No reward is needed.”

But the Countess had already decided. “A benefice,” she declared. “When you are ordained, you shall have a church near Tergou.”

The princess clapped her hands. “Oh, but he ought to be Bishop of Liege! The bishop wears such splendid gloves!”

Gerard, half-dazed by the honor, bowed low. Yet even as gratitude swelled in his chest, another thought pierced him like a blade—*Margaret*.

---

When he returned to the hall, she was gone.

He searched—through the thinning crowds, past the drunken revelers, even to the Mystery play where Virtues and Vices cavorted under flickering torchlight. But she was nowhere.

For days he combed the city, hope fraying with each passing hour. On the third day, he gave up.

With a heavy heart, he turned toward Tergou—royal favor in his future, gold in his purse, and a leaden weight where his joy had been.

The afternoon sun slanted low, casting golden streaks across the tiled floor of the hosier’s sitting room in Tergou. Eli was occupied in the shop, his eldest and youngest sons away on their own errands. Catherine, fretful with maternal worry, had taken her crippled daughter a short way down the road, peering into the distance for any sign of Gerard. Left alone, Giles entertained himself in his usual fashion—a spectacle of grotesque agility that would have unsettled any stranger who chanced upon it.

The Dutch took pride in their innovations—printing, oil painting, banking—but above all, cleanliness. While other nations trod upon floors strewn with rushes and refuse, the folk of Tergou kept their homes scrubbed and gleaming. The sitting room was no exception, its tiles polished to a shine that could rival a looking-glass. The large window, its small lozenge panes bound by strips of lead, cast a lattice of light and shadow upon the floor, the central cross of stonework serving as a quiet reminder of devotion.

The furniture was sturdy and plain, save for the high-backed oak armchair, carved at its summit, looming like a throne. A copper pail of holy water sat near the door, its little broom ready for sprinkling blessings. And then there was the table—long, narrow, and solid—where Giles now clung, not with hands, but with teeth, his muscular arms splayed like a bat’s wings, his body a misshapen wedge of sinew and will.

Nature had been unkind to Giles, granting him a robust torso and a head fit for a hero, then abandoning her work halfway, leaving his limbs stunted and twisted. Yet what he lacked in symmetry, he made up for in sheer, improbable strength. His arms, unburdened by the weight of a full body, could haul him up a pole like a squirrel, or suspend him from a branch by a single grip. Now, in a fit of boyish mischief, he had fastened his teeth upon the table’s edge, dangling with the tenacity of a barnacle.

Little Kate, returning with her mother, stopped short at the sight. Her practical heart ached for the furniture.

“Giles!” she cried. “You’ll dent the wood!”

He dropped to the floor with a thud, scowling. “Run and tell, then, you little tattler. You’re the one for stirring trouble.”

“Am I?” Kate’s voice was cool, but her eyes shimmered. The insult, unexpected from her usually gentle brother, struck deeper than she cared to admit.

Before Giles could retort, the door creaked open, and Gerard staggered in, pale and dust-streaked, his strength spent. A chorus of gasps and cries rose as the family rushed to him, hands fluttering like startled birds. He sank into the nearest chair, breathless, his journey from Rotterdam etched in the weariness of his limbs.

Revived by their concern, he soon rallied, and the tale of his triumph spilled forth.

“The goldsmiths’ work!” he marveled. “Such reliquaries, such chalices—I never dreamed so much skill existed in the world. And the illuminations from Ghent and Bruges! Mine looked like scribbles beside them.” He shook his head, humbled. “But then—then they called my name.”

Kate’s face lit. “Yours?”

The others scoffed, but Gerard nodded. “Aye. I knelt before the Duke, half-dead with fright, and he gave me this.” He held up the gold medal, and the room erupted in delight.

“And these.” He spilled fifteen golden angels onto the table, each coin gleaming like a tiny sun. The family’s hands darted forward, each claiming their share—Cornelis and Sybrandt with greedy fingers, Giles rolling his along the floor like a plaything, Kate pressing hers to her heart with a look of pure adoration.

“And two for you, little one,” Gerard murmured, pressing the coins into her palm. “For your patience in suffering.”

Catherine, overcome, clutched him to her breast. “My son! My pride!”

Eli, gruff but glowing, declared he must tell the neighbors at once. But Gerard held up a hand. “There’s more.”

He spoke of the Countess’s promise—a benefice, a future secure. Catherine wept anew. “Now we can die in peace,” she whispered, “knowing you will care for them all.”

From that moment, Gerard stood apart in the family—not merely loved, but revered. Cornelis and Sybrandt seethed with envy, counting the days until he left for his parish, when they might leech from his prosperity. Yet Gerard himself turned his mind toward the priesthood with new resolve. Love’s wound still ached, but ambition and duty soothed it.

When the bishop next visited, Gerard knelt and received the office of exorcist, the sacred words granting him power over demons. He walked home with solemn pride, the little book of rites in his hands—until Kate met him at the door, her crutches tapping urgently.

“Gerard! The burgomaster sent for you.”

“Ghysbrecht Van Swieten?” Gerard frowned. “What does he want with me?”

Kate shook her head. “He would not say. Only that you must go at once.”

Gerard’s fingers tightened around the exorcism manual. He had seen the look in Ghysbrecht’s eyes before—cold, calculating. No friend wore such an expression.

“Very well,” he said softly. “I’ll go.”

Ghysbrecht van Swieten was a man of cunning. He began his conversation with Gerard obliquely, circling the true matter that gnawed at him.

"The town records," he said, his voice smooth as aged parchment, "are written in a cramped hand, the ink faded with time." He offered Gerard the task of transcribing them anew, presenting it as an honor. Gerard, ever practical, asked what payment he might expect. Ghysbrecht named a pittance—barely enough to cover the cost of ink and parchment.

"My labor must also be accounted for," Gerard protested. "This is a year's work."

"Labor?" Ghysbrecht scoffed. "Scratching words onto parchment is no labor. Where is the sweat in that?"

"It is labor indeed, and skilled work at that—better paid than brute toil, sweat or none. And my time, burgomaster—what of that?"

"Your time?" Ghysbrecht's lip curled. "What is time to a man of two-and-twenty?" Then, sharp as a knife, he struck closer to the truth. "Or is it idleness that grips you? Love, perhaps? Your body kneels with these chanting monks, but your heart—ah, your heart lingers with Peter Brandt and his red-haired daughter."

"I know no Peter Brandt."

The denial only sharpened Ghysbrecht’s suspicion. He leaned forward, eyes glinting. "You lie! Did I not see you at her side on the road to Rotterdam?"

Gerard stiffened. "Ah."

"Ah, indeed! And you were seen in Sevenbergen but days ago."

"Was I?"

"At Peter’s very house!"

"At Sevenbergen?"

"Ay, at Sevenbergen."

It was a gambit—a guess flung boldly, wrapped as fact, to pry loose the truth. But the reaction was not what Ghysbrecht expected. Gerard stood abruptly, his composure fracturing like thin ice.

"Burgomaster," he said, voice unsteady, "I have not set foot in Sevenbergen these three years. I know neither the names nor the dwelling of those you claim to have seen me with. And since my time, though worthless to you, is precious to me—good day."

With that, he was gone, his eyes alight with something between fury and fear.

Ghysbrecht surged to his feet, rage boiling—but then he sank back into his chair. The boy had not flinched. That meant defiance—or knowledge.

He snapped his fingers, summoning his most trusted servant. "See that man?" he hissed, pointing through the window. "Follow him. But mark me—do not be seen. He is young, but his cunning runs deep. Watch where he goes, what he does. Do not return until you can tell me all."

The servant nodded and slipped away.

Night had fallen when he returned. Ghysbrecht seized him by the arm. "Well?"

"Master," the servant murmured, "the young man went from you straight to Sevenbergen."

Ghysbrecht's breath caught.

"To the house of Peter the Magician."

*"Look into your own heart and write!"* Herr Cant had advised. But Gerard knew that hearts, like deep waters, were not so easily fathomed. A man might peer into his own soul and see only what he wished to see—truth distorted by desire, reflection clouded by pride.

Yet halfway to Sevenbergen, Gerard tried. *Why am I going?* he asked himself. His heart, ever quick with excuses, answered at once: *To show her I am indifferent. To prove her fickleness means nothing to me. To parade my honors, my future benefice, my contentment without her or any faithless woman.*

He found Peter Brandt’s cottage easily enough. There in the doorway sat Margaret, needle in hand, her head bent over her work. Beside her stood a tall man leaning on a longbow, speaking to her in low tones. A sharp, unreasonable pang shot through Gerard at the sight. But as he drew nearer, he recognized the man—an old soldier, past fifty, whose skill at the butts was well known.

Margaret glanced up. Her work fell from her hands. A soft cry escaped her lips, and color rushed to her face, then fled just as quickly. Just as swiftly, she schooled her features into cool detachment.

“Master Gerard!” she said, with feigned surprise. “What brings you here, I wonder?”

“I was passing by,” he answered. “I saw you, and thought to greet you—and ask after your father.”

“He is well. He will return soon.”

“Then I may as well wait for him.”

“As you wish.” She turned to the bowman. “Good Martin, would you fetch my father? Tell him a friend is here.”

“And am I not *your* friend as well?” Gerard asked.

“My father’s friends are mine,” she said lightly.

“Doubtful. A friend would not have promised to wait for me, then vanished the moment I turned my back.” His voice lowered. “Cruel Margaret, you don’t know how I searched for you—how nothing was right in the world without you.”

Her chin lifted. “Idle words. Had you truly wanted us, you would have come back. A bed was laid for you at my cousin’s. You would have been welcomed—made much of. Even *I* might have been kind to you that day. But you’ll not find me so again, nor any maid, I promise you.”

“Margaret, I returned the instant the Countess dismissed me. You were gone.”

“Nonsense. Hans Cloterman was left to guide you to us.”

“I saw no one—only a drunkard sprawled at a table.”

“At *our* table? What was he wearing?”

Gerard shrugged. “Some dull-colored robe. I paid little heed.”

Her face softened, then hardened again as she pressed him with sharp questions. He answered honestly, and piece by piece, the misunderstanding unraveled. The relief was a flood, sweeping away restraint. Confessions poured out—how miserable each had been, how empty the days apart.

Then Gerard noticed an open manuscript on the table—a Vulgate. His fingers itched for it. But before he could reach it, Margaret slapped her palms over the page, cheeks flushing.

“Take your hands away,” he teased. “Let me see what you were reading. I’ll read it too at home, and our souls may meet upon the page.”

She shook her head. So he kissed her hands until, laughing, she pulled them away—revealing the verse: *"An apple of gold in a network of silver."*

“There!” she cried. “I had searched so long for it, and now you’ve caught me!” Yet even as she scolded, her fingertip traced the words, sharing them with him.

“A pity it’s hidden under that monstrous cap,” he said.

“It’s a fine cap, so I’m told.”

“Perhaps. But what it hides is far lovelier.”

“It is not. It is hideous.”

“It was beautiful in Rotterdam.”

A sigh escaped her. “Ah, everything was beautiful that day.”

Then Peter arrived, greeting Gerard warmly and insisting he stay for supper. Margaret vanished, only to return with her hair caught in a silver net, her glances now coy, now bright. She moved about them, setting the meal, her laughter like music in the cottage.

When the cool evening air beckoned, Gerard coaxed her outside. She resisted—yet came. He led her toward the road to Tergou. She protested—yet walked with him, hand in hand. And when at last he had to leave, they vowed never to quarrel again, sealing the promise with a kiss that lingered, sweet and slow.

Gerard flew home on wings.

From that night on, his evenings belonged to Margaret. With each meeting, the bond between them deepened, until the hours apart were mere shadows—tolerated, but not truly lived.

For now, love’s path was smooth. Obstacles loomed in the distance, but to youthful hearts brimming with hope, they seemed small, surmountable. The storm that would one day rise—the passions and wills that would clash against their love—gave no warning yet.

The course of true love ran clear and untroubled, drawing them deeper, binding them fast.

And then—

The morning sun glittered on the streets of Tergou, where velvet cloaks and feathered caps caught the light, and the clatter of hooves drew curious faces to every window. The French ambassador’s retinue swept through the town, a spectacle of Burgundian grandeur—huntsmen, jesters, and even the Duke’s own leopard, crouched regally in her wooden cage.

A murmur of awe and amusement rippled through the crowd. The Duke’s tumbler, with his solemn antics, drew laughter, while the leopard’s keeper warned against too bold a touch. "At Antwerp," he growled, "a man tossed dust at her, and the Duke made dust of *him*." The townsfolk recoiled, though their spirits soon lifted again when the Duke’s giant, a towering Hungarian, paused to mock little Giles perched on a balcony.

The dwarf, fiery as ever, roared back in his thunderous bass, while the giant’s voice squeaked like a reed flute. The contrast sent the crowd into fits, but when Giles, in a rage, leapt onto the giant’s shoulders and pummeled his head, the laughter turned to cheers. The giant staggered, cracked his skull against the balcony, and stumbled away, humiliated.

That afternoon, Catherine and her daughter Kate sat weaving dreams of Gerard’s bright future—his benefice, his piety, the honor he would bring their name. "He’ll marry his brothers," Catherine mused, "and care for us in our old age."

Kate, glowing with secret delight, fetched a hidden treasure—a painting of the Virgin, radiant in gold and blue, her eyes lifted heavenward. "Gerard made this," she whispered. "See how holy his hands are!"

But their joy shattered when Ghysbrecht Van Swieten darkened their door. The burgomaster, long estranged, bore venomous news: Gerard was entangled with Margaret Brandt, the red-haired daughter of the so-called magician of Sevenbergen.

Catherine’s heart seized. "Not my Gerard! He is meant for the Church!"

Ghysbrecht smirked. "Then why does he linger with her? Why paint *her* face?" He gestured to the Virgin—whom Peter Buyskens, called in as witness, swore was Margaret’s very likeness.

The women’s pride curdled to dread. When Eli returned, the truth settled like a storm cloud. "Marriage? Never," he growled. "I’ll lock him in the Stadthouse first, like old Koestein did his boy."

Night fell with no sign of Gerard. At last, Eli took Catherine’s hand. "We’ll meet him on the road," he said, and they walked into the moonlight, two old hearts braced for battle.

And then—footsteps. Gerard approached, singing, his face alight with a joy they no longer understood.

While the burgomaster was dragging Gerard through the streets of Tergou, Margaret faced her own trial in Sevenbergen—a small sorrow, perhaps, but sharp as a needle to a housewife’s heart. She stood before Martin Wittenhaagen, the grizzled archer, her eyes brimming with tears.

“Martin,” she said, “there’s nothing in the house, and Gerard will come soon. He forgets to eat when he works—he’ll be faint with hunger, and I’ve nothing to give him.”

Martin scratched his grizzled head. “What can I do?”

“It’s Thursday—your day to shoot. I thought… I hoped…”

Martin shook his head. “Not when the Duke’s hunting. See for yourself.” He pulled a worn parchment from his pouch, its grand seal still intact. A royal license from Philip of Burgundy granted Martin—once an archer in his wars—the right to loose three arrows each Thursday, so long as no nobleman was in the forest.

Margaret sighed.

“Ah, don’t fret,” Martin grumbled. “For you, I’ll risk it. I’ve done as much for worse folk.” He strung his great yew bow, taller than a man, its string humming like a harp when drawn. “I’ll keep to the edge of the wood. They’ll never see me.”

Margaret wrung her hands. “Promise me you’ll not go far. Better Gerard goes hungry than harm comes to you.”

With a nod, Martin slipped into the trees. The distant call of horns told him the hunt was on, the game afoot. He took his stand behind an oak, arrow nocked, eyes sharp.

A hare bounded into the glade, ears pricked. Martin loosed his shaft—too quick. The arrow hissed beneath her belly, and she fled.

“Clumsy fool,” he muttered.

A wood-pigeon alighted above him. This time, he took care, drew smoothly—but the arrow only grazed the bird. It fluttered away, dying somewhere unseen.

“Luck’s against me,” he growled.

Then—movement. A great stag burst into the clearing, and behind it, low to the ground, came the Duke’s leopard, sleek and deadly. Martin ducked behind the oak. The beast sprang upon the buck, tearing at its throat.

A reckless thought struck him. He drew his last arrow, aimed true, and buried it in the stag’s heart. The noble beast crashed down. The leopard, undisturbed, feasted on the kill.

Martin waited, then stepped forward, gripping the stag’s leg. The leopard’s growl froze his blood. He met its gaze, willing it to cower—but the brute knew no fear. With a snarl, it leaped at him.

Martin caught it by the throat. Claws raked his shoulder, pain searing like fire, but he clenched his jaw and throttled the beast, his grip iron. The leopard writhed, tail lashing—then went limp. He flung it aside, breath ragged.

Now came terror. *I’ve killed the Duke’s leopard.*

He covered the body with leaves, shouldered the stag, and staggered home, blood trailing behind him.

Margaret gasped when he burst in. “No questions,” he rasped. “Cook it quick.”

She saw only the deer’s blood, not his wounds, and set to work. Martin bound his shoulder in silence. Soon, the scent of roasting venison filled the cottage. Gerard arrived, and with a flask of Schiedam between them, they feasted.

Martin told his tale. They laughed—until Margaret’s face went pale.

She pointed. At the window, two green eyes burned in the dusk. The leopard.

A single deep bay echoed through the wood. Then another.

Martin’s blood ran cold. “Hounds. They’ve caught the scent.” He snatched Gerard’s bow. “I’ll kill her, throw her in the forest—maybe they’ll think she died fighting the stag.”

But Margaret grabbed the arrow. It snapped in her hands.

“Woman, you’ve doomed me!”

“No.” Her voice was steel. “Stand back.” She seized his knife and ran outside.

The hounds were closing in, baying, men shouting. The leopard’s eyes gleamed, unblinking.

Then—Margaret stepped forward, alone, the knife in her hand.

Margaret tore a great piece of venison from the bone and flung it out the window toward the gleaming green eyes in the dark. A savage snarl answered her, followed by the crunch of teeth rending flesh. Then, sudden as thunder, a hound’s bay split the night, so loud it shook the walls. The three at the window shrank back, hearts pounding. The leopard, fearing for its stolen meal, slipped like a shadow toward the woods—only for the clamor of horses, men, and dogs to burst past the house in furious chase.

Martin and his companions exhaled as one. The beast was swift; it would not be caught for miles yet. Hands clasped in relief, and Margaret, trembling, let her tears fall. Gerard kissed them away.

Back at the table, Gerard raised his cup. "To a woman’s wit—stronger than a man’s strength!"

"Ay," said Margaret softly, "when those she loves are in danger. Not otherwise."

That night, he lingered with her longer than ever, walking home proud as a king and light with joy. But under the shadow of the trees, two figures barred his path. His father and mother.

Out so late? His heart chilled.

They stood grim, silent.

He stammered a greeting.

"Why ask?" His father’s voice was iron. "You know why we are here."

"Oh, Gerard!" His mother’s voice trembled, torn between reproach and love.

His tongue turned to lead.

His father relented slightly. "No need to hang your head. You’re not the first fool caught by a pretty face."

"Nay, ’tis witchcraft!" Catherine cut in. "Peter the Magician is known for it."

"Come, boy," his father said, "you know a priest has no business meddling with women. Swear to stay away from Sevenbergen, and this ends here. We’ll not punish a first fault."

"I cannot promise that."

"Cannot?" His father’s voice cracked like a whip. "You defy me?"

"I kept silent to spare you pain. But now the truth is out—I love Margaret. And I will *never* be a priest. I’d sooner die."

"Then we shall see," his father growled, "what it costs to defy a father."

Silence swallowed them whole. The walk home was a funeral march, broken only by Catherine’s stifled sobs.

From that hour, peace fled the little house at Tergou.

Gerard was hauled before the family like a criminal. All turned against him—save little Kate and the dwarf, who took their cues from her wide, tearful eyes. Cornelis and Sybrandt were worst of all, their malice sharp as knives.

Gerard, stunned by so many enemies, looked helplessly to his sister. But even she turned away.

"Pray, Gerard," she whispered, "pray Heaven cures you of this folly."

His heart broke. "You too?"

He left without another word, straight for Sevenbergen.

Quarrels between those who love each other are the cruelest of all. At first, both sides may have justice. But time twists right into wrong, pride hardens hearts, and anger blots out reason. In the end, no one wins.

Elias was stern, his elder sons spiteful, but Gerard’s greater mind saw both sides—and so he wavered, torn, never angry, only heartsick. He had no ally in this fight. He dared not tell Margaret the depths of his family’s wrath; her pride might make her cast him off to spare him. Margaret Van Eyck, once a comfort, could not help now—not when she had chosen art over love in her youth. How could he confess he threw away a benefice for what she had once scorned?

He was near surrender.

Then Catherine, ever unpredictable, turned the tide. One day, when Cornelis sneered, "Gerard wed Margaret? Hunger wedding thirst!"—she rounded on him.

"And what will *you* bring a wife, slug? Gerard can paint, can write. You? Naught but waiting for your father’s shoes!"

Gerard, despite himself, smiled.

But the true turning point came when his father threatened force. "The burgomaster will lock you in the Stadthouse before I let you wed her. And a priest you’ll be, willing or not, before the year is out."

Gerard went pale. "Then hear me now—by God and St. Bavon, I swear I’ll *never* take the tonsure while Margaret lives. If force rules, then force will answer you: the day the burgomaster comes for me, I leave Tergou—leave Holland—leave my father’s house, where I was loved not for myself, but for what I could be made!"

He stormed out, trembling with fury.

Catherine wailed after him, "Men are crueler than tigers to their own flesh!"

That same hour, Reicht Heynes found him. "Mistress Van Eyck asks for you."

The old artist sat like a judge. "Why have you stayed away?"

Gerard faltered.

Reicht pitied him. "He feared to tell you of his love."

"Ah! So we are not friends enough for confidences?"

"Forgive me," Gerard begged. "I dared not speak my folly."

"Folly? Is love folly?"

"I hear it called so daily."

Margaret Van Eyck’s stern face softened. "You are my son in art. You shall not waste your youth as I did. Marry your Margaret. I’ve made inquiries—she’s a good girl."

Gerard, starved for sympathy, poured out his heart. Soon, three pairs of eyes glistened.

Women’s courage is not men’s. It is fiercer in its way—the courage to send others into battle while standing firm. Now, they urged rebellion.

"Marry her at once," said Reicht. "The old will come around."

"Look at you," Margaret Van Eyck added. "Pale, worn to a shadow. While those clods thrive on spite."

Gerard wavered. "I don’t fear prison. I fear my father’s curse. I’d wed her tomorrow—but to make her a beggar’s wife?"

"Then take her away," the old woman said. "To Italy."

"*Italy?*" His eyes kindled.

"Where painters are princes, where scribes earn three hundred crowns a manuscript. The Pope himself begs for skilled hands to copy treasures fleeing Constantinople. Go! I’ll give you gold for the journey."

Midnight found them still scheming. From that hour, Gerard walked lighter, armored against gibes.

Margaret Van Eyck gave him more than coin—she gave him secrets.

"Jan’s colors never fade. Know why? He ground them himself. His panels were prepared a year in advance. Most painters are in too much hurry to last." She taught him to purify oil, to layer colors so they’d outlive time itself.

Armed with knowledge and means, Gerard resolved: marry, then fly to Italy.

Only Margaret Brandt’s consent remained.

He found her, poured out his plans.

She refused.

"Not while your father opposes us."

He pleaded, raged. "Then you side with them! You’ll make me a priest after all!"

He stormed off, leaving her in tears.

Yet love bends to pity, not wrath. *How unhappy he must be,* she thought, *to speak so.*

Then, barely an hour later, he returned, clutching tattered canvas—her portrait, slashed to ribbons.

Her cheeks burned. "Who did this?"

"I don’t know. And I dare not ask—for I’d hate them till death." He trembled. "Six months’ work—gone. Your face—*your face*—hacked through."

Margaret’s breath came fast. "Then take the face itself."

He froze.

"If they rob you of my likeness," she said, "you shall have *me.* Marry me, Gerard. Tomorrow."

Joy swept him like a tide. He kissed her hands, her lips, then ran for Peter and Martin to witness their betrothal—a bond solemn as vows.

The deed was done.

The banns of marriage were to be read three times, as was the custom—though in those days, the readings could be hurried if the priest was willing. The young couple, hearts pounding with hope, persuaded the new cure to declare their intent three times in a single day. By Monday’s end, the words had been spoken at both matins and vespers, and to their relief, no one from Tergou had been present to hear.

But joy turned to terror the next morning when a stranger stood in the church and forbade the union. The man claimed they were underage, their parents unaware. Outside, Margaret and Gerard clung to each other, trembling, despairing—until the very man who had ruined them approached with a sly offer.

“A crown,” he said, “and I’ll undo my work. Tell the priest I was mistaken, and all will be well.”

Gerard, desperate, promised him a golden angel instead. The bargain struck, the man retracted his objection before the cure, who then agreed to marry them the following morning.

Yet the would-be extortionist, drunk on his prize, boasted of the scheme at an archery green in Tergou—where Sybrandt, Gerard’s own brother, listened with keen interest.

Sybrandt rushed home, finding his father absent but his elder brother Cornelis at hand. The two, idle and covetous, saw Gerard’s love as a threat to their future inheritance. Without a word to their mother—whose loyalties were uncertain—they went straight to the burgomaster.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, though disdainful of their greed, saw his opportunity. “Leave this to me,” he said, dismissing them with cold authority.

Meanwhile, Gerard, unaware of the storm brewing, sat at his mother’s knee, her fingers stroking his hair as she softened toward his love. Her guilt over destroying Margaret’s portrait had turned her heart. For once, she was his ally.

But the next dawn brought disaster.

In the quiet church at Sevenbergen, Gerard stood radiant, Margaret blushing at his side, with only Peter and Martin as witnesses. The cure began the rite—only for a harsh voice to cut through the sacred words.

“Forbear!”

Constables seized Gerard in the name of the law. Martin’s knife flashed, but the priest stayed his hand. “What madness is this?”

“He weds against his father’s will,” declared the officer.

Margaret’s cry of despair echoed as Gerard was torn from her arms. They dragged him away, feigning a journey to Rotterdam—only to double back, smuggling him into the haunted tower of Tergou’s Stadthouse.

The cell was small, the window barred, the only furniture a massive oak chest. The air was thick with the dread of slow death—starvation, poison, or worse. Gerard knelt, praying for deliverance, then seized the rusted iron bar of the window, wrenching it free just as Ghysbrecht entered.

The burgomaster’s eyes flickered to the loosened bar but said nothing. He left bread and water, then delivered his ultimatum: *Renounce Margaret, or rot here.*

Gerard’s defiance was instant. “Death first.”

But hope came from below. Martin, ever watchful, had seen Gerard’s face at the slit of a window. A plan was set in motion—a bow, an arrow, a message tied in silk.

With trembling hands, Gerard obeyed. The cord came, then a thicker rope. He fastened it, tested the chest, and—heart hammering—climbed out into the free air, descending hand over hand toward Margaret’s outstretched arms.

Yet freedom was not yet won. A lantern’s light swept the courtyard. Guards.

They pressed into the shadows, but the light found them. Martin nocked an arrow—then froze, face blanched with terror.

“The haunted tower,” he gasped. “Hell walks tonight.”

A figure, its head aflame, scaled the wall like a spider.

Gerard gripped his knife. If mortal foes did not take him, the devil might.

The burgomaster’s venomous glare and Gerard’s unjust imprisonment had seared into the young man’s heart a conviction: Ghysbrecht van Swieten was his mortal foe. Now, as he edged around the tower’s corner, his pulse hammered not with fear of phantoms, but of treachery—some trap laid by that same cruel hand. Yet before he could steel himself further, another hand slipped into his, warm and steady. Margaret stood beside him, her courage a silent vow to face whatever peril lay ahead.

Then they saw it.

High upon the tower’s face, a creature with a head of fire crawled upward, its dark form silhouetted against the unnatural glow. Below, a spectral figure in white stood motionless, pale as death. Gerard’s breath caught.

“The rope!” he whispered hoarsely. “It climbs the rope!”

The fiery thing vanished into the cell—his cell—and the window pulsed red, as if the very stones bled. The white specter remained, waiting.

Margaret’s fingers tightened, then slipped from his grasp. Before he could stop her, she was running toward the apparition, her cry slicing the night. Gerard’s heart lurched. Was the devil luring her to doom? He dropped to his knees, voice trembling as he hurled Latin into the dark:

Then—a whimper. Not from Margaret, but from the specter itself. The ghostly figure cowered, pleading for mercy. Hell, it seemed, had its fears.

---

Kate and Giles arrived moments later, staring dumbfounded at the rope dangling from the window.

“Gerard’s escaped!” Giles crowed, already gripping the line.

Kate clutched his arm. “Fool! This is devil’s work! Where would Gerard get a rope? How hangs it from nothing? It’s glamour, meant to snare you!”

Giles snorted. “The road to hell goes *down*, sister. This rope goes *up*.” With a grin, he launched himself upward, the lantern at his neck turning him into a glowing demon in reverse. Kate watched, breathless, until a sudden movement sent her stumbling back—a woman, rushing at her from the shadows.

Both shrieked.

“You’re—you’re alive?” Kate gasped.

“As are you!” Margaret panted, hand on her heart.

Then came a voice from above, sepulchral and booming:

Giles, now a fiery-headed imp, rained down documents upon them, his laughter wild as the papers fluttered like wounded birds. When he slid to the ground, he thrust the bundle at Gerard, palm outstretched.

“Payment,” he demanded.

Margaret frowned. “This isn’t ours.”

Gerard’s jaw set. “Ghysbrecht stole my freedom. I’ll take what I can.”

---

Kate begged him to come home. Gerard’s reply was ice.

“I have no father. Only a jailer.”

She pressed on, revealing Cornelis and Sybrandt’s treachery, but Gerard’s wound was too fresh. “He gave Ghysbrecht the power to cage me. Let Tergou rot. I’ll find no home here again.”

Margaret touched Kate’s arm. “Give him time.”

The sisters exchanged a look, then an embrace. Kate’s sigh as she turned away was a quiet thing, lost to Gerard’s ears but not to Margaret’s heart.

As they vanished into the night, Giles trotting behind with his stolen hoard, Gerard’s last words hung in the air—a vow, and a farewell.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten clutched the key to Gerard’s cell in his fist, his patience wearing thin. *Let hunger teach him obedience*, he thought, waiting until the stroke of ten before ascending the stone steps, a loaf of bread and pitcher of water in hand, his servant trailing behind with a wary grip on his sword.

At the door, he paused, pressing his ear against the wood. Silence. A smirk curled his lips. *Good. Despair has dulled his defiance.* He turned the key and swung the door wide—only to freeze, his breath hitching in his throat.

The cell was empty.

Ghysbrecht’s body locked in shock, his mind refusing to accept the sight before him. The servant peered over his shoulder, eyes widening at the dangling rope, the displaced iron bar. “By all the saints—”

But Ghysbrecht did not hear him. His gaze snapped to the chest in the corner, and with a strangled cry, he lunged forward, clawing at the lid, his fingers trembling as they scraped over the empty interior.

“Gone!” His voice cracked like brittle parchment. “Gone—*all of it!*”

The servant recoiled. “Master? What’s lost?”

Ghysbrecht’s face twisted. “The—the town records,” he stammered, but the lie was thin. His mind raced, fury and terror warring within him. *The boy knew. He knew the secret.*

“A hundred crowns to the man who finds them!” he spat. “Every last scrap!”

The servant’s eyes gleamed. “Then the reward is as good as mine. Gerard must have taken them.”

Ghysbrecht seized him by the collar. “Then *move*, fool! Before he spreads what he’s seen!”

——

Less than an hour later, Dierich Brower stood in the Eliassoen home, flanked by armed men. Catherine wrung her hands. “What has my boy done now?”

Dierich’s smile was slick as oil. “A youthful prank, nothing more. But the burgomaster’s records are missing, and Gerard must answer for it.”

Kate, sharp-eyed, stepped forward. “Gerard is gone from Tergou,” she said coolly. “He left last night.”

Dierich’s mask slipped. “Where?”

“Far from here. He swore never to return.”

A lie. She *knew* it was a lie. But if Gerard had fled to *her*—Margaret—then every moment wasted put him in greater danger.

As Dierich stormed out, Kate caught Giles’s sleeve. “They mean him harm,” she whispered. “You must warn him. *Now.*”

Giles grumbled but relented. He would go—on foot, if he must.

Kate pressed a message into his hands, made him repeat it until the words were etched in his mind. Then she knelt, her prayers following him into the night.

The road to Sevenbergen was bright with the joy of newfound freedom, but shadows soon crept over Gerard and Margaret’s hearts. Gerard was a fugitive now, marked by the law, and the thought of parting hung heavy between them. The air, once sweet with the triumph of their escape, turned bitter with the weight of what must come.

Margaret’s fingers tightened around his. "You must leave," she whispered, though the words tore at her. Italy—the land of art, of promise—beckoned him, but the distance was a yawning chasm in her chest. Gerard, torn between love and ambition, swore it was the only path forward. "I’ll return with gold, with honor," he vowed, his voice thick. "Then no one will stand between us."

But promises could not stop the tears that fell between them. They sat through the long hours, hands clasped, weaving dreams of a future where no one could tear them apart—only for reality to claw back in, cruel and unrelenting.

Then came the thunder of hooves.

Giles loomed in the doorway, his face grim, his message dire. "They call you a thief," he rumbled. "They hunt you. Kate says—*run*."

Margaret’s breath left her in a gasp. Gerard’s face paled. The parchments—those damned scraps of ink and vellum—had become a noose around his neck.

"They’re but an excuse," Giles growled. "Dirk Brower’s men are stirring. If you linger, you die."

Fear, sharp and cold, shot through them both. Gerard moved quickly, burying the cursed documents deep in the earth—all but one, a deed that bore his family’s name. He tucked it into his breast, a secret to unravel later.

The sky darkened as if in warning. Thunder rolled in the distance, and rain began to fall, heavy and relentless. Martin, ever watchful, scouted the road. "No sign of them yet," he muttered, but his eyes were wary. "But the storm won’t hold them forever."

The lovers clung to each other in the dim glow of a single candle, the storm howling outside as if the heavens themselves raged against their parting. Supper lay untouched. Time slipped like sand through their fingers.

Then—a sound.

A footstep, too quiet, too careful.

Margaret’s nails dug into Gerard’s arm. Martin’s hand flew to his bow, the candle snuffed out in an instant.

Another step. Then another.

Silence.

Then—

A knock.

The world stopped.

And in that terrible stillness, their hearts pounded like drums of war.

The heavy knock came again, more insistent. They were surrounded.

Margaret’s breath hitched, but she forced herself to think. “Tell them he was here but has gone,” she whispered, then seized Gerard’s arm and pulled him toward the narrow steps leading to her father’s chamber. Her own room lay just beyond.

The pounding resumed.

“Who knocks at such an hour?” Martin’s voice was rough with feigned irritation.

“Open, or you’ll regret it!”

“I don’t open my door to thieves—honest folk are abed by now.”

“Open to the law, Martin Wittenhaagen, unless you want trouble.”

Martin grunted, recognizing the voice. “Dirk Brower? What brings you so far from Tergou?”

“Open, and you’ll find out.”

Slowly, Martin drew the bolt. The door flew open, and Dierich Brower stormed in with four others, while a sixth man slipped in from the rear.

“Now then, Martin—where’s Gerard Eliassoen?”

“Gerard?” Martin feigned indifference, though his pulse quickened. “He was here, but he’s gone.”

“Gone?” Dierich’s face darkened. “When?”

Martin shrugged, settling by the fire to mend his bowstring with deliberate calm. “His brother, that little imp Giles, came charging in on a mule, screeching like a banshee. Next thing I knew, Gerard was off. They say he’s bound for Italy—not that I’d know where that is.”

Dierich’s jaw tightened. The story held no cracks. A cleverer liar might have overplayed his hand, but Martin stuck to the tale he’d been given, spinning it just enough to seem plausible.

“Mates,” Dierich muttered to his men, “I told the burgomaster this was folly. That dwarf we saw riding through the storm—he must have warned Gerard. We’re too late.” He spat in disgust. “A wet cloak for our trouble, that’s all.”

Martin smirked, but the moment Dierich’s eyes narrowed, his grin faded.

“Still,” Dierich said, “we’ll search the place to satisfy the burgomaster.”

Martin’s stomach clenched. He forced himself to stay still as Dierich snatched the candle and led his men upstairs. Left alone, Martin’s mind raced. If Gerard was still inside, he had one chance—fight or flee.

He cracked the back door open, watching. The glow of the candle flickered in Peter’s room. *Fool! Jump now!* But no movement came. Then the light shifted to Margaret’s chamber. Still no escape.

A cold dread settled over him. If they found Gerard, it would mean death for the boy and heartbreak for Margaret.

Martin’s fingers twitched toward his knife.

Then—muffled cries from Margaret’s room.

His blood ran hot.

With a snarl, he moved fast. At each door, he mimicked Dierich’s voice. “Watch the window!” Then he barred both exits.

Bow in hand, arrow nocked, he stood at the foot of the stairs—a soldier ready for slaughter.

Two shots. That was all he needed. Two men down before they could close in. Then he and Gerard could take the rest, blade to blade.

The footsteps grew louder. The light spilled down the steps.

Martin’s breath steadied. His fingers flexed around the bowstring.

Let them come.

---

Dierich’s men tore through Peter’s chamber—ripping open cupboards, stabbing into curios, overturning the bed. Nothing.

Then Margaret’s room. Small. Bare. No place to hide, save the chimney.

Margaret sat up, blinking in the candlelight, her face pale with shock. Then she screamed.

Dierich held up a hand. “Peace, girl. We mean no harm. Lie still.”

“Thieves!” she gasped. “You come armed into my home—into my *bedroom*—”

“We’re looking for Gerard.”

“Gerard?” Her voice cracked. “You drove him away! You know this! Or are you not men of Sevenbergen? Would you shame an honest girl so?”

Dierich flinched. The others shifted uncomfortably.

“Enough,” he muttered. “He’s not here.”

Margaret’s eyes burned. “Then go. And pray your own daughters never suffer such an insult.”

Dierich retreated, his men stumbling after him.

“I told the burgomaster,” he grumbled. “A waste of time.”

The door slammed shut.

Silence.

Martin exhaled, lowering his bow.

For now, they were safe.

But the night was far from over.

A woman’s heart is fierce in love and cunning in desperation. Where is she who will not play her part to perfection if it means saving the man she holds dear? Nature arms even the simplest of them with wit and daring when the moment demands it, and Margaret was no exception.

The moment the men left, she flung herself from the bed, her breath coming fast, and threw open the chest where Gerard lay hidden. The lid, the blankets, all had been pressed down by her weight—but she had not thought, not truly, of what that might mean. She pressed her ear to the door, listening until the last footsteps faded down the stairs. Only then did she dare turn back.

The chest was not what it seemed. Gerard himself had fashioned it, clever and quick with his hands. Beneath the false bottom, a space just deep enough to hide a man—or so she had believed. But now, as she leaned over it, whispering his name, there was no answer.

Silence.

Her fingers brushed his shoulder, then clutched at him, shaking. *“Gerard!”*

Nothing.

A terrible stillness met her touch. His body was limp, lifeless. She dragged him from the chest with a strength born of horror, hauling him toward the window, throwing it open as if the night air might breathe life back into him. Moonlight washed over his face—gray as ash, his chest unmoving.

Her hands flew to his heart. No beat. No breath.

She cradled his face, whispering, pleading, as if love alone could undo what had been done. *“Gerard, wake up. Please. I didn’t mean—I didn’t know—”* Her voice cracked. How could he be gone? How could she have been the one to smother him? She had pressed down on that lid, sealing him in darkness.

*“Forgive me,”* she sobbed, clutching him. *“I would have let them take you—I would have let them drag you away before this—”*

Her mind reeled. This couldn’t be real. Death did not come so swiftly, so cruelly. Not like this. Not by her own hands.

She begged him to curse her, to shout at her—anything but this silence. When no answer came, her grief twisted into something raw and keening. A scream tore from her throat, then another, wild and shattered.

Outside, the men froze at the sound. It was not the cry of a woman who had outwitted them. It was the sound of a soul breaking.

And they knew, even before they turned back, that something far worse than capture had happened in that room.

Martin Wittenhaagen stood at the foot of the stairs, his bowstring taut, arrow drawn nearly to the head, a knife gripped behind his back. His sharp eyes widened as the men returned—without Gerard. Slowly, he lowered his weapon, his face slackening in confusion.

The men, too, stared at him, their brows furrowed. One of them, Dierich, narrowed his eyes. "What’s this, then? Were you fixing to loose an arrow at us, old man?"

Martin exhaled, forcing his features into their usual stolid calm. "Just testing the pull of a new string," he grunted. "See?" He unstrung the bow with deliberate slowness. "Satisfied?"

Dierich snorted. "There’s more to you than meets the eye. But no matter—let’s get a fire going. Dry off before we head back."

A blaze was soon crackling in the hearth, its light flickering across their faces as steam curled from their damp clothes. Then—piercing the quiet—a woman’s shrieks tore through the house.

Every man jolted upright. One snatched the candle and bolted for the stairs.

Martin moved to block him, his pulse hammering. *Margaret.*

Dierich lunged, locking his arms around Martin from behind. "Hold him!"

The candle-bearer vanished into the upper darkness while the others piled onto Martin. A brutal scuffle erupted—bodies crashing, fists flying, the floorboards shuddering under the weight of grappling men. Martin fought like a wolf, but numbers overwhelmed him. Rope bit into his wrists and ankles as they trussed him tight.

He groaned, teeth grinding. That man was in Margaret’s room now, and he was helpless to stop it.

Dierich wiped sweat from his brow, panting. "Aye, gnash your teeth, old devil. You’ll do no more damage with ’em."

One of the others spat. "Reckon our necks wouldn’t be safe with this one loose."

"Got me thinking," another muttered, "maybe Gerard’s closer than we thought."

Dierich barked a laugh. "Wishful thinking. But where’s Jorian? Taking his sweet time with that wench upstairs."

Coarse chuckles rippled through the group—until the sound of frantic footsteps thundered overhead.

"About time," Dierich called, grinning. "Well, Jorian? What’s the fuss up there?"

Jorian Ketel strode directly to Margaret’s chamber, and there, to his utter astonishment, he found the very man he had been sent to seize—pale, motionless, his head cradled in Margaret’s lap as she knelt over him, her own face frozen in silent horror. Her eyes, wide and glazed, saw nothing, heard nothing, cared for nothing but the lifeless face before her. Jorian stood rooted, the candle trembling in his grip.

“Where was he hiding all this time?” he muttered.

Margaret gave no answer, lost in her grief. Jorian moved to the empty chest, examining it, and slowly the truth dawned on him. The girl’s mute despair struck him deeply.

“A grim night’s work,” he said gruffly. “All for a few scraps of parchment! Better he had fled with us than come to this.” She remained deaf to his words. He hesitated, then reached for a small hand-mirror hanging nearby. Holding it to Gerard’s lips, he watched. A faint mist formed upon the glass.

*"There’s life in him yet,"* Jorian murmured.

At those words, Margaret awoke as if from a trance. She flung herself upon Jorian, arms clasping his neck with desperate gratitude.

“Bless you for those words!” she cried, her voice breaking between sobs and wild relief.

Jorian, unused to such fervor, cleared his throat. “Enough of that. Let’s get him warm.” He lifted Gerard gently onto the bed, then drew a flask from his belt. Splashing its contents—sharp, stinging Schiedam—twice across Gerard’s face, he waited. A faint sigh escaped the young man’s lips.

Margaret swayed, hands pressed to her mouth, torn between rushing to him and fearing she might harm him further.

“Leave him be,” Jorian advised. “He’ll come round. Youth’s on his side.”

As Gerard’s breathing steadied, Jorian turned to leave. Before he could reach the door, Margaret caught him, sinking to her knees, clutching his legs.

“You won’t betray him?” she pleaded. “You’ve saved his life—don’t cast him back into danger!”

Jorian shifted uncomfortably. “I’ve done you favors before. I’ll not speak a word to Dierich Brower. But those parchments—there’s a hundred crowns for them. My Joan and the little ones could use that gold.”

“They shall have it,” Margaret vowed.

“You know where they are?”

“Not here, but I swear by all that’s holy—come to me tomorrow, alone, and the reward is yours.”

Jorian grunted in satisfaction. “Then my lips are sealed. But mind you keep your word.” With that, he slipped away, leaving Margaret to kneel beside Gerard, her prayers trembling on her lips.

---

When questioned by Dierich Brower, Jorian shrugged. “The girl took fright, that’s all. Half in a fit. Her father’s seeing to her.”

Dierich frowned. “We ought to check—”

“And send her to her grave with more terror? Leave it be.” His casual tone disarmed suspicion, and soon the men retreated to the warmth of the *Three Kings*, grumbling about wasted effort.

“Told you we’d be too late,” Dierich muttered as they left.

---

That night, Gerard had skirted prison and death—not by cunning or wit, but by the flicker of mercy in a rough man’s heart, and the lure of gold. Such are the threads of fate: tangled, unforeseen.

For those who have never known extremes of joy or anguish, the depth of what followed might be impossible to grasp. To watch life and color return to a beloved face—to see the eyes brighten, the lips part in speech—this was Margaret’s rapture. And for Gerard, to wake from darkness into the arms of his love, her tears warm on his skin, her voice whispering devotion—no bliss could rival it.

They clung to one another, murmuring vows, as if the horrors of the night had been a fleeting nightmare. Here, in this quiet space, they were husband and wife in spirit, betrothed before God, shielded by sacred promises.

But exhaustion soon stole over Margaret. Her limbs grew weak, her hold on him slackening.

“Stay,” she whispered as Gerard stirred anxiously. “Only let me feel you near. While I touch you, I know you’re safe.”

So he cradled her instead, stroking her hair as she trembled against him. There was sweetness in her fragility, a trust that humbled him.

Tomorrow would bring parting. But tonight? Tonight, they had cheated death. And in that victory, even farewells seemed bearable.

Yet innocence, in its ignorance, walks ever on the edge of peril. And for these two, the greatest danger lay not in prison or grave—but in their own untested hearts.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten tossed restlessly through the night, his mind churning with unease. Fear of the storm’s wrath kept him from joining the search of Peter’s house, but as soon as the last thunderclap faded, he rose, saddled his mule, and rode hard for Sevenbergen.

At the *Three Kings*, he found his men sprawled in drunken slumber—some slumped over tables, others curled on the floor. With a snarl, he roused them, listening impatiently as they recounted their failure.

*"Fools!"* he spat. *"You let him slip through your fingers. Did you even think to look beneath the girl’s bed?"*

One of the men grunted. *"No space for a man there."*

*"And how would you know if you didn’t look?"* Ghysbrecht’s voice was a whip-crack. *"You should have torn the place apart—searched every corner, tested every panel!"*

Dierich Brower staggered to his feet, rubbing his eyes. *"If you find him now, call me a horse and not a man."*

Within moments, Peter’s house was surrounded once more. Ghysbrecht, his face a mask of grim determination, left his mule with Jorian Ketel and led the charge inside. The rooms stood empty—no Peter, no daughter, no sign of their fugitive guest.

Then a shout rang out. One of the men pointed through the open window, where across the fields, walking calmly beside Margaret and Martin, was the very man they sought.

A triumphant cry burst from Ghysbrecht’s lips. He hurtled down the stairs, vaulted onto his mule, and spurred his men into furious pursuit.

The first light of dawn had barely touched the sky when Gerard rose, shaken by the close call of the night before. He roused Martin, who blinked in surprise—he had half-expected the young man to vanish like a ghost through the window by now.

“We must leave this place,” Gerard said urgently. “Before the village stirs.”

Martin, ever practical, considered their options. “Only one road is safe,” he grunted. “Through the great forest. I know a path that will lead us to a hostel where you can get a swift horse. A day’s hard ride, and you’ll be beyond Holland’s reach.”

Peter’s cottage stood barely a furlong and a half from the forest’s edge. They set out quickly—Martin with his bow and three arrows (for it was Thursday), Gerard armed only with a stout oak staff Peter had pressed into his hand. Margaret, hiking her kirtle out of the damp, walked with them as far as the garden hedge, where Peter stood, uncharacteristically solemn, and gave Gerard his blessing.

The sun was just breaking over the horizon when they crossed the stony field toward the trees. They were halfway there when Margaret, glancing back, let out a sharp cry.

Ghysbrecht and his men were closing in.

No time for defiance—only flight. Margaret bolted first, her screams sharp with terror. Gerard and Martin followed, the shouts of their pursuers biting at their heels.

“Just reach the wood!” Martin roared.

They were fast, but Ghysbrecht was faster. The old burgomaster spurred his mule, cutting a wide arc to intercept them. Margaret clung to Gerard, but he shoved her aside—not roughly, but with desperate focus.

Ghysbrecht, in his zeal, had forgotten one truth: cornered things fight back.

Instead of dodging, Gerard charged straight at him, staff raised high. The oak came down with a crack like splitting stone, and Ghysbrecht toppled from his saddle, blood streaming down his face.

Then—into the trees.

Behind them rose a howl of fury. The hunt was on.

“Why run?” Gerard panted. “You’ve your bow, I’ve this—” He brandished the bloody staff.

“Boy,” Martin snarled, “the *gallows*.”

So they ran.

Margaret’s breath came in ragged gasps; Gerard’s fingers clenched white around his weapon. The forest loomed ahead, a tangle of safety—or so they hoped.

But the hazel coppice was a maze. Martin bulled through, shoving young trees aside like stalks of wheat. Paths branched and twisted; they took one, then another, doubling back without seeming to. At last, deep in a pine grove where the air was thick with resin and the ground soft with needles, Martin halted.

He sat, pulled bread and ham from his wallet, and ate as calmly as if they were picnicking.

Gerard stared. “They’ll never find us here?”

Martin snorted. “Not if Sevenbergen turned out in force.” Then his eyes narrowed. “You’ll lose that purse before Italy, lad.”

Gerard looked down. A heavy triangular purse was tangled in his belt. “This isn’t mine.” He worked it free with his knife. Inside—silver, and folded scraps of brown paper.

Margaret paled. “Ghysbrecht’s.”

Gerard laughed, exhilarated. “Torn from his saddle when I struck him! Spoils of war!”

Margaret bit her lip. “Throw it away, Gerard. Or let Martin return it. They already call you a thief—”

“Return it?” Gerard scoffed. “Not a coin. This was won fairly.”

Martin nodded. “Keep the silver. Send back the paper if it troubles you.”

Margaret’s voice dropped, pleading. “You’re going far. We need Heaven’s favor. How can we ask it if we take what isn’t ours?”

Gerard waved her off. “Heaven put it in my hand. Like the Israelites spoiling Egypt.”

She bowed her head. “You are my husband. Your will is mine.”

The meekness in her voice startled Martin. Gerard, touched, kissed her brow. Then they pressed on, deeper into the forest’s heart.

But triumph soured quickly.

“Martin,” Gerard muttered, “I struck too hard.”

Martin spat. “Grey hairs don’t make a fox less sly.”

“I saw them as my staff fell. They’ll haunt me.”

“Ha! If you’d cracked a younger skull, *then* I’d call it pity. Old men are done with living anyway.”

Gerard shuddered. “What if I’ve killed him?”

Margaret wrapped her arms around him. “You struck in defense—once, with what you held. Not in malice. If he’s dead, it’s by his own hate, not yours.”

Gerard clung to her words. But then—

A sound.

Deep, resonant, like a single toll of a bell.

Martin went rigid.

“What is it?” Gerard asked.

Margaret heard it next—a pure note hanging in the air.

Martin’s face turned ashen. “Echoes deceive. It comes from behind us.”

“What does it mean?”

Martin’s jaw tightened. “It means we find a better spot—to make our stand.”

“For what?”

“To die well,” said Martin, very low. “Taking three for one.”

Margaret’s hands flew to her mouth. “What *is* it?”

Martin met Gerard’s eyes.

“The avenger of blood.”

A beat.

Then, grimly:

“A hound.”

The courage of ordinary men, like their skill, moves within narrow bounds. Venture but a step beyond, and they falter. Martin Wittenhaagen was steadfast in the face of familiar perils—those he had known in his rough, soldier’s life. But this? A bloodhound on his trail? That was a terror beyond his reckoning. The beast’s relentless instinct, unshaken by human cunning, seemed almost supernatural. After a few strides, he leaned heavily on his bow, his spirit draining away like water from a cracked vessel.

Gerard, whose fear had not yet hardened into despair, urged him forward. "Why stop? We must run!"

"What use?" Martin muttered. "If we escape the wood, they’ll cut us down like dogs. Better to stand here—find a place where we can sell our lives dear."

Margaret, though pale, clutched Martin’s arm. "Then let us part ways! Gerard’s life is the one they seek—let them chase us, and he can slip free!"

Martin shook his head. "You don’t know the beast. It doesn’t track a man—it tracks blood. They’ve led it to where Ghysbrecht fell, and from his blood to the hand that spilled it, that devil will follow, even if Gerard swam the Meuse or hid in an army."

The hound’s deep bay echoed through the trees—a sound both beautiful and terrible, like the emerald-eyed serpent that lures its prey before the strike. Martin stood frozen, his courage undone by the unknown. Gerard, too, felt the dread seep into his bones. Had Heaven itself marked him for vengeance?

But Margaret, though trembling, thought faster than either man. "Give me your knife," she said. Martin handed it over. "Little use in your hands," he muttered.

Then, swift and silent, she stepped behind Gerard, drew the blade across her arm, and smeared her shoes and stockings with her own blood. Neither man saw.

Seizing Martin’s wrist, she hissed, "Be a man! Lead us to thick cover—where numbers won’t save them."

Martin led them sullenly to a hazel thicket. "Here. We’ll wait. When they come through, we’ll cut down as many as we can before they finish us."

"Is that your only plan?" Gerard snapped.

"It’s all that’s left."

"Then follow me." Gerard slashed at the hazel shoots, twisting them behind him as he moved. Martin did the same, but his hands were heavy with despair.

The baying grew louder, nearer. Then—hoofbeats? No. Mule’s steps. Ghysbrecht himself, broken-nosed but relentless, was hunting them down.

"They’re in the wood," Martin whispered.

Gerard’s plan was set. "Martin, take your bow. Hide here. I’ll draw the hounds to that oak—when they break cover, shoot. I’ll brain the rest."

Martin’s eyes kindled. They took their positions.

Then—Margaret’s hand on Gerard’s shoulder. "Martin says if we’re separated, make for the high ash-tree."

Before he could answer, a massive hound burst from the thicket, nostrils flaring. It never saw her—scent was its world. With a snarl, it lunged for Gerard’s tree—and fell dead, Martin’s arrow through its ribs.

A second hound came, sniffed its fallen kin—then a white streak (Martin’s last arrow) sent it howling into the dirt.

Now the wood rustled with men. Gerard slipped back into the brush just as three pursuers broke through. He crawled, silent as a fox, while Martin and Margaret did the same.

A shout—the dead hounds found. Voices clamored, wasting precious moments.

Margaret trembled. "They’ll kill him, and I’m not there!"

"Hush," Martin growled. "He’s cleverer than they are. To the ash-tree—but quietly."

They neared the edge of the wood—then froze. Ghysbrecht himself sat on his mule, bandaged but watchful, blocking their escape.

Martin’s bow creaked as he drew his last arrow. Margaret covered her face—but peered through her fingers.

Then—a figure dropped from above like a hawk. A kerchief whipped over Ghysbrecht’s head, and he crashed to the ground, groaning. Gerard stood over him.

"Quick! The mule!"

Martin mounted, Gerard lifted Margaret into the saddle—then Ghysbrecht writhed free, shrieking, "Treason! Murder!"

Gerard stomped his throat silent. "Ride!"

But too late—Dierich Brower and five men burst from the trees, roaring.

The chase was not over yet.

The shot rang out—sudden, startling—and for a moment, even the shooter stood frozen, stunned by his own handiwork. Then, with a shout, he called his comrades to join the chase, already sprinting up the hillside. But before he had gone halfway, a figure rose from the earth like a specter: Martin Wittenhaagen, bow bent, arrow nocked.

Eric Wouverman knew that sight too well. In an instant, he flung himself behind the nearest tree, pressing flat against the bark. Martin’s aim was legend, and the dead hound at their feet was proof enough of it. Cautiously, Wouverman edged his eye around the trunk—only to find the arrow’s glinting point still trained on him. He held his breath.

Minutes crawled by before the others arrived, a clamor of boots and voices swelling behind him. Then, with a laugh sharp as a blade, Martin was gone, the thunder of hooves marking his retreat. The men surged forward, cresting the hill just in time to glimpse their quarry—tiny as insects in the distance—fleeing down a narrow, endless glade: Gerard and Margaret, running as if the devil himself were at their heels, with Martin galloping hard behind them.

The hunters had been outplayed.

But how?

The answer lay in a trick of chance—or perhaps fate’s cruel humor. Just as the bolt flew, the burgomaster’s mule had stumbled, her hoof snared in a rabbit hole. She pitched forward, spilling both riders. Gerard caught Margaret, but momentum bore them down, a tangle of limbs upon the earth. The mule, ever docile, scrambled up at once, trembling but unharmed.

Martin, quick as ever, took stock: one pursuer, no more. A gamble, then. He sent the lovers fleeing on foot while he stood his ground, arrow drawn, buying them time. Now, with the enemy stalled, he gave chase, overtaking Gerard and Margaret in a breathless sprint. The mule became their salvation—riding in turns, sharing the burden of speed, swapping rider for runner until even the wind could not have tracked them.

By the time they slowed, the forest had swallowed them whole. No sign, no sound.

Behind, Dierich’s men could only turn back, empty-handed, to tend their fallen hound and nurse their wounded pride.

Life and liberty are like the sun at noon—taken for granted until shadow falls. Then their true worth shines bright as gold among the elms. So it was with Gerard and Margaret, who moved through the whispering trees, hearts trembling yet glowing with the fierce joy of escape. The baying of hounds and the shouts of cruel men still echoed in their minds, but here, in the quiet wood, they were free.

Then Gerard saw the blood on Margaret’s feet.

“Martin!” he cried, seizing the soldier’s arm. “Look—she’s wounded! The crossbow—”

Margaret smiled softly, shaking her head. “No, Gerard. Not wounded.”

His face twisted in fear. “Then what—”

She touched his cheek, her own flushing. “Do not scold me.”

“When have I ever scolded you?”

“Never, my love.” She lowered her eyes. “Martin said the dogs followed the scent of blood. So I thought… if I marked my shoes with it, they would chase me instead of you.” She held out her arm, where a thin, deliberate cut glistened. “Forgive me?”

Gerard choked. “A scratch? This is a wound!” His voice shook as he cradled her arm.

Martin snorted. “A pinprick. The lad acts like she’s lost a limb.”

Gerard shot him a dark look. “I could stand a river of blood—but not hers.” He pressed his forehead to hers. “How could you do this?”

Margaret’s smile was tender. “Silly Gerard,” she whispered, winding her arms around his neck. “As if I wouldn’t give every drop in my veins for you.”

And then, with the terror of the hunt still fresh, they clung together, weeping—not just for the danger passed, but for the parting that loomed before them.

“I must leave you,” Gerard whispered, his voice breaking. “You to Holland, me to Italy. Oh God, must it be?”

Margaret said nothing. She only held him tighter, her silence more wrenching than any cry.

Soon they reached the edge of the wood, where a narrow path cut through the trees. Martin halted. “The road,” he muttered, nodding ahead. “And the inn lies yonder.”

Gerard and Margaret stared at each other, stricken.

“A word, Martin,” Gerard said hoarsely, drawing him aside. He pressed a purse into the soldier’s hand. “This was for my journey—she won’t take it, but you must. See that she lacks for nothing. The burgomaster’s silver will serve me.”

Martin gripped his shoulder. “No harm will touch her. I swear it.” He turned away, jaw tight.

Then came the farewell—too cruel to be witnessed. Martin stood apart, head bowed, as Gerard and Margaret knelt together, whispering vows like prayers. They clung, parted, then clung again, as if their very souls resisted the tearing.

At last, a strangled cry—and Gerard was running, hands raised to heaven, while Margaret swayed, her face ashen, arms outstretched to empty air.

Martin caught her before she fell. She did not weep. She did not speak. She only trembled as he lifted her onto the mule and led her slowly home.

At the edge of the village, he helped her down. “Go now,” he said gently. She obeyed, walking like one in a dream.

Martin turned toward Rotterdam. Sevenbergen was no longer safe.

Gerard moved as if sleepwalking. At the inn, he hired a horse and guide, then rode in silence toward the German border. The world passed like shadows—trees, houses, faces—all blurred.
