“Shall we never be free of this cursed land?” he burst out once, savagely.

At dusk, they reached a hill overlooking a slender brook. “There,” said the guide, pointing. “Germany lies beyond.”

Gerard dismounted, paid the boy without a word, and descended alone. The water sparkled over smooth stones. He drank, bathed his face, then stared at the far bank.

With a sudden rush, he leaped across.

The moment his feet touched foreign soil, he whirled back. “Farewell, ungrateful country!” he cried. “Were it not for her, I’d leave you without a sigh—my kin, my home, my mother’s arms—all of it! Farewell, fatherland. The wide world is mine now.”

But the brave words crumbled. His knees buckled, and he sank to the ground, sobbing.

Then, after a time, he rose. Wiped his eyes. And without another glance behind, he turned his face toward Rome, stepping firmly into the unknown.

The road forked ahead, winding like serpents through the countryside. Gerard took out the little dial Peter had given him, held it to the sun, and chose his path—as sure, as solitary, as a swallow flying south.

Only this swallow flew alone.

Gerard trudged wearily along the muddy road, the weight of his journey pressing upon him. The afternoon sun slanted through the trees, casting long shadows that seemed to stretch endlessly before him. As he rounded a bend, he came upon an odd sight: two men in sober attire lounged lazily against either side of a horse, while its rider, clad in a gaudy green jerkin and hose, lay sprawled face-down in the dirt like a sunbathing lizard. His flaming yellow velvet cloak had been draped carefully over the horse’s back, as though the beast were the one in need of warmth.

Gerard paused. “Is something amiss?” he asked.

One of the servants glanced up, unperturbed. “Naught that I know of.”

“But your master—he lies like a dead man. Do you not think to lift him from the ground?”

The man chuckled. “The earth’s the best cure for his ailment. A hard bed sobers a man quicker than a soft one. Isn’t that so, Ulric?”

“Aye,” the other agreed, eyeing Gerard with sudden suspicion. “Who are you, stranger, that you don’t know the Count is ever drunk by this hour?”

Gerard flushed. “I am a traveler, and new to these parts.”

Ulric smirked. “Then pay us for the lesson—trinkgeld for our wisdom.”

Gerard hesitated but fished out a few coins, if only to escape further scrutiny. As he handed them over, he gestured to the cloak. “Why lay the man’s mantle on the horse?”

The servants, now emboldened by his coin, launched into competing theories—one claiming the Count would rage if his steed caught cold, the other insisting a horse, being a milksop water-drinker, needed warmth more than its ale-soaked master. Their debate grew heated, then violent, until cuffs were exchanged over the horse’s mane. The beast, startled, reared back—and planted a hoof squarely on the prone nobleman.

The Count awoke with a howl, sword flashing from its sheath. The servants fled, the horse bolted, and the nobleman staggered after them, cursing wildly, his blade glinting as he caromed off the hedgerows in drunken pursuit.

Gerard watched the chaos fade down the lane, then turned southward, his purse lighter but his spirits no brighter.

---

Dusk fell swiftly, and with it came rain—a deluge that soaked Gerard to the bone. The road ahead vanished into a wall of darkness, and soon he found himself groping through a dense forest, the trees looming like specters overhead. Wolves howled in the distance, their voices rising in a chorus that set his teeth on edge. He gripped his cudgel, heart pounding, until at last a flicker of light pierced the gloom—a candle in a window.

Relief flooded him. He stumbled toward the glow and pushed open the door of a low, crowded room, only to reel back as a wave of heat and stench struck him. The air was thick with the reek of wet wool, unwashed bodies, and the pungent tang of garlic. A great stove dominated the space, surrounded by travelers drying their sodden clothes, while in one corner, a brood of children added their own sour note to the mix.

Gerard hesitated, torn between the horrors within and the wolves without. Hunger decided him. He crept inside.

“Is this an inn?” he asked a man near the door.

The fellow snorted. “Where else? ‘The Star of the Forest,’ known to all.”

Gerard’s stomach growled. “Might I have supper?”

The landlady, plucking feathers from a heap beside her, barely glanced up. “Supper’s long past. Come by six next time.”

His heart sank. “Then might I at least sleep?”

“When the beds come.”

Baffled, Gerard leaned against the wall, watching as straw was strewn across the floor. Soon, the guests sprawled upon it like felled logs, their snores rising in discord. He claimed a corner, as far from the stove’s stifling heat as possible, and curled up, nose buried in straw to blunt the odors.

Sleep came fitfully, broken by the hind’s prodding as he delivered a foaming mug of sour beer. Gerard choked it down, too weary to protest.

Dawn brought relief. Most of the company had vanished, and Gerard paid his meager fee, eager to escape. The kind woman who had shared her pudding with him the night before stood at the door with her husband.

“Godspeed,” she said warmly as he passed.

He kissed her cheek in thanks, then stepped into the morning light, the road stretching before him once more.

---

By afternoon, he reached a grand monastery, its arched gateway welcoming. Within, he found charity—a clean bed, a meal, and the soaring voices of monks at vespers. Yet even their celestial harmonies could not ease the ache in his heart. *Margaret should be here,* he thought, and sighed.

The prior, noting his youth and purpose, gifted him a silver coin and sage advice: “Find company on the road. The way is perilous for one alone.”

Perilous? Gerard scoffed inwardly—until that evening, when he stood before another inn, its door barred, its windows dark. A face appeared at a hatch, eyed him dumbly, then pointed wordlessly to a side entrance.

Inside, the common room was a den of filth—mud-caked travelers scraping boots with knives, mothers comparing ailments, the reek of unwashed bodies thickening the air. The landlord, a grizzled tyrant, measured guests before feeding them, and the tablecloths were sacks fit only for scarecrows.

Gerard despaired.

A Burgundian soldier, laughing at his disgust, clapped him on the back. “Courage! The devil is dead!”

Gerard managed a weak smile. But as the room grew hotter, the company ranker, he wondered if the devil had merely moved—into Germany.

When straw was scattered for beds, he lay down, too exhausted to care. Tomorrow, he vowed, he would find better company. Or die trying.

Denys caught Gerard as he fell, lessening the impact, but it was the dying bear beneath them that truly broke his descent. Gerard’s head and shoulders struck the thick fur, and Denys wrenched him free before the beast could stir. She was past harming them now—her breath came in ragged gasps, her limbs twitched weakly, and soon she lay still. Denys propped Gerard against her soft bulk, fanning him until his senses returned.

Groggy, Gerard recoiled at the touch of fur and rolled away with a cry.

"Courage," said Denys, grinning. *"Le diable est mort."*

"Is it dead? Truly dead?" Gerard peered from behind a tree, his earlier bravery now eclipsed by a cold, trembling dread.

Denys laughed, tugging the bear’s ear, prying open her jaws, even thrusting his head inside in mocking bravado. Gerard turned away, sickened.

"What now?" Denys teased. "You climb like a squirrel, then tumble like a drunkard just when the fight is won?"

"I swooned, I think."

"Why?"

Gerard wiped his face, grimacing. "Her blood—the stench of it. I cannot abide blood."

Denys smirked. "A fine soldier you’d make. Yet here you stand, unharmed, while your enemy lies gutted."

"You mock, but I saw you pale as a ghost beneath that tree."

Denys flushed. "A man may tremble for his friend."

Gerard, moved, embraced him without a word.

Denys, uncharacteristically softened, ruffled Gerard’s hair. "Women faint, not men—yet here you kneel like a monk at vespers."

Gerard dropped to his knees. "Should we not thank Him who saved us?"

Denys hesitated, then knelt beside him, hands folded in the manner of his homeland, face solemn. When they rose, Gerard’s eyes shone with quiet joy.

"God will reward your piety, Denys."

*"Bah!"* Denys waved a hand. "I did it to please you. Still, ’twas well-prayed—fit for a bishop. Now, with the saints on our side, *marchons!"*

Two steps forward, and Denys halted. "The cub—"

Gerard shuddered. "Leave it."

"You’re right. The hour is late, and the beast weighs heavy. Besides, its sire may yet seek vengeance." He paused, eyes widening. "You’re wounded!"

"I am not."

"You *are!*" Denys dropped to inspect Gerard’s leg, fingers tracing the stiffening limb. "Quick, before it locks!"

Gerard hobbled, wincing. "Who frets now?"

Denys muttered, "I mocked you for fainting—yet you bore a wound without complaint. I’ve a black tongue and a blacker heart."

"Denys—"

"You lie."

Denys chuckled ruefully. "Your kindness shames me."

The pain worsened until Gerard could barely walk, then not at all. He sank to the ground. "Leave me. I cannot go on."

Denys hauled him into the woods, pressed a crossbow into his hands. "If rogues come, shoot first. Honest men keep to the road; fools parley." Then he snatched Gerard’s axe and sprinted back the way they’d come.

Alone, Gerard wavered between pain and dreams of Margaret—how sweet to one day tell her of these trials! His thoughts were shattered by shouts, the *thunk* of an arrow striking a tree.

Battle cries filled the air, though from where, he couldn’t tell. Hooves pounded, steel clashed, and strange thunderous cracks split the night, followed by drifting smoke. Then—silence.

When Denys returned, hauling the bear’s pelt, Gerard cried out in relief.

"Were you in the battle?"

Denys blinked. "What battle?"

Gerard described the phantom clash.

Denys chuckled. "Fever breeds visions. Once, with a cracked skull, I saw armies clash in a cart-shed."

Gerard pointed to an arrow embedded in a tree.

Denys sobered. "English. Drawn to the ear, not the breast. A skirmish, then—but no affair of mine." He fashioned a bed of leaves, draped the bear-skin over them, and they slept.

At dawn, Gerard woke, ravenous.

Denys yawned. "Sleep dines."

"I *cannot* sleep for hunger!"

They trudged on, Gerard leaning on his axe and Denys. The moon revealed the wood’s edge—and a gibbet, its grim cargo swaying in the breeze. Beneath it, a fire burned, tended by a slumbering figure.

Denys stoked the flames, ignoring Gerard’s unease. The watcher woke, startled, then offered wine—Rhenish, rich and smooth. Gerard drank, the warmth steadying him, while Denys feasted, jesting macabrely with the stranger.

The man spun a tale of outlaws betrayed, hanged by soldiers disguised as merchants. He toasted each dangling corpse, his words slurred with wine and spite.

At Denys’s urging, the man helped Gerard walk, though Gerard recoiled at touching a thief.

Near Dusseldorf, the man balked. "Go further, and you deliver me to the noose."

Denys tossed him coins. "For your trouble—and a prayer, since you lack one."

Gerard obliged—in Latin, a swift, sharp plea for divine justice. The thief, satisfied, vanished into the dark.

As they walked, Gerard murmured, "That she-bear saved us both—by God’s will."

Denys grunted. "Aye. And may He guide us to softer beds next time."

The afternoon sun slanted through the window as Gerard lay restless in his bed, his fevered mind clinging to the thought of a lemon—something tart and cool to cut through the dryness in his throat. Denys, ever the faithful guardian, had set out to find one, though lemons were scarce in these northern lands. Left alone, Gerard drifted in irritation, his body aching, his thoughts frayed.

Then the door creaked open, not with Denys’ boisterous return, but with the measured step of an elderly physician. The man was a portrait of learned dignity: a long fur-trimmed gown, cherry-colored hose, and shoes so pointed they might have been weapons. His white beard flowed like a scholar’s scroll, and behind him trailed a boy bearing a basket of vials and instruments, the tools of his trade.

“How fares my patient?” the doctor murmured, his voice soft as if afraid to disturb the air.

Gerard, wary but polite, admitted his head was heavy, his throat parched. The doctor’s eyes sharpened at the mention of a wound.

“A bite?” he mused, inspecting the mark. “A dog, surely. No bear would leave so neat a puncture.”

“It was the paw,” Gerard corrected.

“Ah! A grave matter indeed,” the doctor intoned, shaking his head. “Claws carry a venom worse than teeth. We must act swiftly—cautery and bloodletting.”

Gerard flinched. “Must it burn?”

“Science demands it,” the physician declared, launching into a lecture on the humors, the evils of corrupted blood, and the infallibility of his methods. He spoke of Greek anatomists and Arabian quacks, of veins and vital essences, his voice swelling with the pride of Montpellier-trained wisdom.

Then Denys returned, lemons in hand, and halted at the sight.

“What’s this?” he asked, eyeing the physician’s tools.

“He means to bleed and burn me,” Gerard said weakly.

Denys’ face darkened. “I’ve seen enough battlefields to know what follows when fevered men are drained. The strong grow weak, the weak die.”

The doctor bristled. “You would trust a soldier’s superstition over centuries of learning?”

“I trust my eyes,” Denys shot back. “And they’ve seen more men saved by rest and clean air than by leeches and hot irons.”

Gerard, caught between them, groaned. “Must I choose between two tyrants? One would boil my blood with words, the other with arguments.”

The debate raged—Denys scoffing at the physician’s pretensions, the doctor dismissing Denys as an ignorant brute. Voices rose, tempers flared, until Gerard, driven to desperation, flung his pillow at the old man’s head.

Chaos followed. The doctor toppled, his basket of phials scattering, his dignity shattered. The boy yelped. And then—the final humiliation—the physician sat upon the hot coals meant for Gerard’s wound.

A hiss. A yowl. The scent of singed wool.

Denys roared with laughter as the doctor scrambled up, his robes stained, his pride in tatters. Gerard, despite himself, let out a weak chuckle.

“Perhaps,” he gasped, “the cure is worse than the disease.”

The physician, red-faced and sputtering, stormed out, hurling dire prophecies of convulsions, black vomit, and death. The door slammed.

Silence.

Denys wiped his eyes. “Well, little monk, it seems we’ve made an enemy of medicine.”

Gerard sighed, sinking back. “At least we still have lemons.”

And with that, he closed his eyes, exhausted but—for the moment—unburned.

The physician returned home, groaning about a "bad burn"—for while we are quick to cauterize another’s wound, our own pain is ever a burning misery. His housekeeper, with the gentle confidence of old remedies, applied a salve as mild as milk. He endured it meekly, his only aim being relief. Yet even as he suffered, his mind turned to vengeance: he drew up a bill for his shattered phials and swore before a magistrate that two strangers, indebted to him, plotted to flee the town.

Fate, however, was unkind. For once, the truth spilled from his lips—*in an affidavit*, no less!—and when the officers reached *The Silver Lion*, the birds had flown. Down to the river they rushed, gathering word of the fugitives’ path, and gave chase.

Denys, ever sharp-eyed, had seen the danger the moment the physician left in silence. "That old fool’s quiet was unnatural," he muttered. "We are strangers here; the law favors its own. Five minutes more, and we’ll be clapped in irons for striking a town worthy. Can you walk to the river? Once there, a boat is as good as a bed—better, for it moves."

Gerard’s eyes lit with feverish excitement. "The boat! The Rhine! The very breeze will cool this trifling heat in my blood. To Rome—to Sevenbergen—to home! Let us fly!"

Swiftly, Denys settled their dues, and they hurried to the water’s edge—only to learn the public boat had long since departed. But where questions gather, a crowd follows, and soon an old boatman with his two sons offered passage. A bystander scoffed. "The current’s too strong for three oars."

"Then my comrade and I will row," said Gerard, though he swayed where he stood.

"No need," laughed the old man. "The fool owns the other boat."

With a stout wind at their backs and oars in hand, they set off at a gallant pace. Gerard, breathing deep, sighed with relief. "The river air is healing already. Though, in truth, the doctor did me some good."

Denys chuckled. "The doctor? You refused his cures."

"Aye, but—" Gerard hesitated, then grinned. "Knocking him down soothed me."

Denys roared. "Amiable dove! What a fierce little soul unfolds! I misjudged you at first."

"Nay," Gerard protested. "I’d have borne his threats, but he was near an infidel—denying the hand of God in our shaping! Such men I was taught to abhor. Had it been a thunderbolt, it would have been justice—yet all he got was a pillow from this feeble arm."

"*Feeble?*" Denys scoffed. "I’ve seen arms in my day, but never one that struck like a catapult. The bolster wrapped his nose, the ends met behind his head, and his skull rang like a bell. Had he been Goliath, down he’d have gone. Saint Denys spare me from such *feeble* foes!"

The river twisted, and the wind shifted, tilting the boat. All but one small figure—a child of five, the boatman’s grandson—scrambled to the high side. The boy, who had slipped aboard unseen, sat unconcerned, too light to heed the laws of balance.

As they sailed, Gerard suddenly recalled: this path led to Rome—*not* Burgundy. "Denys," he said, troubled, "this is not your road."

"I know," said Denys quietly. "But I’ll not leave you while fever grips you. You shift from red to white even now. I must go on to Cologne, then strike across."

Gerard brightened. "Then thank Heaven! For how could we pass so near and not see it? A city ancient and vast—founded by a Roman lady, sacked by Attila, scarred by Lothaire! A church for every week, chapels beyond count, and the great minster, unfinished yet glorious! There lie the Three Kings—Melchior, Gaspar, Balthazar—in their shrine of shining brass, jeweled and radiant. And St. Ursel’s, where rest eleven thousand virgins, martyred by Moors! Their bones fill the crypts; their skulls gaze from the church. And the Maccabees, boiled for refusing swine’s flesh—"

Denys laughed. "Peremptory king! I’d have eaten the bacon and kept my skin."

Gerard scowled. "It was their *faith*."

"Peace! Are there no knights in this city of priests?"

"Aye! Herman Gryn, who slew a lion with cloak and sword—his statue stands there, lion and all. Few have done the like—Samson, Lysimachus, Benaiah—"

Denys grinned. "A supper with those four! What a night that would be."

Gerard shuddered. "Not I. But tell me, when did *you* see Cologne?"

"Never. But the monks paint it bright in words."

Denys shook his head. "No dead saint draws me there—only you, my living friend."

Gerard fell silent.

Then the youngest boatman cried, "What ails the brat?"

The child, who had sat mute, now burst into tears. Oh! Oh! Oh! No injury, no fright—just sudden wailing.

"Who can fathom children?" snapped the boatman. Then his eye caught movement ashore. "Look yonder—who are those men?"

Four figures strode along the eastern bank. The boatmen huddled, whispering, glancing between passengers and pursuers.

But before they could act—*disaster*. A squall struck. The boat heeled; the men lunged to balance it—only to see water rushing across the deck. A heartbeat later, the Rhine swallowed them whole.

The officers fumbled for their ropes. Gerard, a strong swimmer, surfaced gasping, shaking water from his eyes. He saw the capsized boat, figures clinging—then heard a voice, low and despairing:

"Adieu, comrade. Adieu."

Denys was sinking, dragged down by his arbalest. His pale face, wide-eyed, turned once to Gerard—then vanished beneath the dark water.

With a cry of terror, Gerard dove after him.

The officers hurled a rope into the churning river.

Good and evil have a curious way of balancing themselves, often with unexpected symmetry. The steel bow strapped to the arbalestrier’s back, rising above his head, had dragged him under. Yet that same bow, positioned just so, could not escape Gerard’s grasp—one hand seized it, the other slipped between the bowstring and the frame, securing a firm hold. The next instant, Denys was wrenched upward with such force that half his body burst from the water.

“Don’t grab me! For pity’s sake, don’t grab me!” Gerard cried, terrified of the fatal mistake.

*“Pas si bête,”* Denys sputtered, water choking his words.

Relief washed over Gerard. Here was a man who understood. “On your back!” he commanded, seizing the crossbow and pulling forward to guide the motion. “Hand on my shoulder—slap the water with the other. No, downward, like this. Do nothing more than I say.”

Twisting his fingers into Denys’s long hair, Gerard clenched the ends between his teeth, using the strength of his neck to keep the soldier’s head above the surface. Then he struck out fiercely against the current. For a heartbeat, he hesitated—which bank to aim for? The western shore lay slightly closer. A simple choice, yet one with fateful weight. Had he chosen the other, they might have swum straight into the arms of the law.

The river fought them, dragging them sideways as they struggled forward. When at last their feet touched gravel, they staggered onto solid ground, breathless and dripping. For a moment, they simply stared at one another, as if to confirm they were both whole. Then, without a word, they flung their arms around each other’s necks, hearts too full for speech.

To save a life—to save a friend—what joy could surpass it? No poet’s flight could reach such heights. For Gerard, the exiled wanderer, this moment was sweeter than any relic or shrine.

Calmer now, they clasped hands and walked along the bank like sweethearts, careless of direction. Behind them, the boat’s survivors clung to the overturned hull, the turnip-faced man still wailing as though nothing had changed.

“Shall we wait for them to right the boat?” Denys asked.

Gerard shook his head. “Our fare is paid. We owe them nothing. Let’s move on—swiftly.”

Denys agreed. “We can walk all the way to Cologne from here.”

“I’m not going to Cologne.”

Denys stopped short. “Where, then?”

“Burgundy.”

“Burgundy?” Denys’s voice cracked. “You can’t mean it.”

“I do. What does it matter how I reach Rome?”

“But—the churches, the relics! You were eager to see them.”

“Foolish Denys,” Gerard said softly. “That was when we were to see them together. The world is full of churches, full of bones. But a friend like you? There’s only one. I won’t drag you farther from home, nor will I lose you now. Since I fished you from the Rhine, I find I love you too well to part.”

Denys let out a joyous whoop. *“Courage! Va pour la Bourgogne!”* He clapped Gerard’s shoulder. *“Oh, soyez tranquille! Cette fois, il est bien décidément mort, ce coquin-là.”*

And so they turned their backs on the Rhine.

But across the water, the law had not forgotten them. A voice rang out: *“HALT!”*

Gerard turned. One of the men from the boat brandished an official badge and a parchment. His stomach twisted—a law-abiding man at heart, he had always obeyed authority.

Denys, however, was French. And a soldier. He assessed the situation: the river between them, the boat still adrift, no ferry in sight.

“Quick,” he muttered. “To that hill—out of sight, then double time.”

They walked on, deliberately unhurried.

Denys turned, snapped his fingers insolently, and kept walking.

Another finger-snap for his grace.

Denys dismissed his majesty with the same contempt.

Gerard winced at the theatrics. “Now we must *run* to Burgundy,” he said grimly. And run they did, Gerard outpacing Denys with a stamina that left the soldier gasping.

“Where in hell’s name is your fever?” Denys panted.

“Drowned in the Rhine, I suppose,” Gerard shot back.

At a village, Denys bought bread and a bottle of Rhenish wine. “No inns,” he warned. “We’ll sleep rough—else we’ll be taken in our beds.”

As night fell, they found a barn, its floor heaped with fresh straw. Denys buried Gerard in it, leaving only a breathing hole. “Water may kill fevered men,” he joked, “but I’ll make warm water of you yet.”

Gerard laughed. “A few drops from the Rhine won’t chill me. I’ve heat enough to boil a cloud.”

Sleep took him instantly. Denys lay awake longer, listening to the distant drum of hoofbeats and the eerie howls of pursuit. He held his breath until the sounds faded, then slept like the dead.

Morning came. Gerard sat up, straw steaming around him. “Lazy loon,” he teased Denys, who only grinned.

They set out, Denys scouting ahead from a treetop. “Clear to the next village,” he reported.

As they neared the settlement, Denys stopped. “How do you feel?”

“As if Rome were just past those houses.”

“And your body?”

“Neither hot nor cold. Only this cursed leg—itches like the devil.”

Denys sighed in mock solemnity. “Unhappy youth. Your fever is gone, and your wound is healing. Which means I must now tell you: *the hue and cry is out for us, and they’re on fast horses.*”

Gerard’s face fell. *“Oh.”*

Gerard staggered at the sudden news, his face flushing pale then red with shock. Then his jaw tightened in anger, for even the gentlest soul, when pressed too hard, will turn and fight like a cornered boar. His fingers curled into fists, ready to strike—but the next instant, his resolve faltered. “My axe is in the Rhine,” he muttered bitterly.

They debated their next move. Prudence whispered *avoid the village*; Hunger growled *find food*. Hunger won.

They cut across the fields, stopping at a haystack to steal two bundles, then burrowed into a dry ditch—hidden, but nettles stung their skin. Taking turns, they ventured out and returned with stolen turnips, gnawing on them in furtive silence as dusk bled into night.

When darkness swallowed the land, they crept out, shivering in the lashing rain. The road beyond the village was a black void, the wind howling like a wounded beast. They moved blindly, unseen and unheard, ghosts slipping past unseen foes. The storm itself seemed to claw at them, the night so thick they might as well have been carving their way through solid stone.

By the time the moon rose, they were leagues from Dusseldorf—yet still they trudged on. Then, looming ahead, a vast building materialized from the gloom.

“Courage!” Denys cried. “I know this place—a convent! We’ve crossed into Juliers. Cologne holds no sway here.”

A moment later, the walls closed around them, and they were safe.

Gerard found himself in the company of a monk whose hands were as skilled as his soul was humble. The man had crafted the great sundial in the prior’s garden, a wheel for drawing water, and even a winnowing machine for grain—ingenious works that spoke of a mind ever busy with creation. Now he labored over a set of *regalles*, a small organ meant for the choir. Gerard, who knew only the rudiments of the psaltery, listened in awe as the monk played with a grace that seemed divine, gently exposing his lack of skill. Yet when it came to illumination, Gerard’s own hand was the finer.

There was an earnest simplicity in their exchange, the kind that bridges youth and age. The monk pressed Gerard to stay the night, and after a glance at Denys—who agreed with a resigned shrug—Gerard accepted. He spoke of their journey, their destination, and the perils they had faced, though he softened the tale of the bolster.

“Ah,” sighed the old monk, shaking his head, “I too was once a traveler, yet no rogue ever troubled me.” He urged Gerard to avoid inns—haunts of sin and danger—and instead seek refuge each night in the quiet safety of monasteries. Then, with sudden sharpness, he asked, “How long since you were shriven?”

Gerard flushed. “More than a fortnight.”

“And you an exorcist!” The monk clicked his tongue. “No wonder misfortune found you. You must be absolved at once.”

Gerard knelt eagerly, but the monk waved him off. “Not to me! I am as worldly as any man alive. My soul clings to these wooden pipes and leather stops—vanities that perish. Go to Brother Anselm.”

He directed Gerard down the corridor but then hissed in alarm when the young man nearly knocked at the wrong door. “Not there! Not there!” he whispered urgently, flapping his hands as if shooing away demons. Only when Gerard moved to the next cell did the monk relax, vanishing back into his own with a satisfied nod.

Brother Anselm was a figure of quiet dignity, his face a blend of wisdom and kindness. As Gerard confessed, his artist’s eye could not help studying the monk’s noble features, wondering if he might later sketch them. The penance was light: three kisses upon the lowest altar step, three paternosters, and a credo. When Gerard returned, Anselm tended to his wound with a cooling plaster, speaking of medicine and faith.

“We Dominicans seldom bleed now,” he mused. “The Vulgate calls blood the life of a man. Simples and herbs serve us better—God’s own remedies. And when earthly means falter, we call upon the saints.”

Gerard ventured a question. “Do the relics themselves hold power, or is it faith that works the cure?”

Anselm smiled. “Who can say? The woman who touched Christ’s robe was healed by faith—yet had she not touched it, would she have been cured? These mysteries are not for us to unravel. Only believe.”

With a final blessing, the monk sent Gerard on his way, leaving him strangely mournful. *How sad,* he thought, *to meet a kindred spirit only to part at once, never to cross paths again.*

The refectory brought practical aid: the friars mapped their route, marking each monastery along the way. That night, the old monk chuckled as he revealed why he had been so frantic in the corridor. “You nearly fell to Brother Jerome—a saint, but stern. He’d have sent you barefoot to Loretto—or worse, Compostella!”

One last strain of the psaltery, sweet and solemn, lulled Gerard to sleep, his heart eased.

The days that followed were quiet, marked only by steady progress. No brigands barred their path, no storms waylaid them—just the wear of miles and the ache of tired feet. Monastery after monastery offered shelter, their doors ever open, their welcomes warm. The road stretched long, but step by step, Gerard and Denys pressed on, their faces bronzed, their shoes wearing thin, until at last the border of Burgundy drew near.

Gerard found himself nearly as eager for Burgundy as Denys, for the soldier never ceased singing its praises. At every minor inconvenience along their journey, Denys would scoff and declare, *"In Burgundy, they do things better!"*—and above all, he played upon Gerard’s fastidious nature by promising clean linens at the inns.

*"If I can but sleep in a naked bed once more,"* Gerard sighed, *"I ask no greater luxury. To think I’ve not lain in one since leaving home! Each time I see their foul sheets, I wish I could pluck out my eyes and nose rather than endure them."*

Denys’s patriotism ran so deep that he had marched twenty leagues in shoes split at the seams rather than buy from a German cobbler, whose incivility was matched only by his stubbornness. As evening fell, they stood at a crossroads—a small town lay one way, a monastery the other, though the latter lay off the road. Denys favored the inn; Gerard, the convent. After some debate, the soldier relented—on one condition: once they reached Burgundy, they would lodge only at inns. Gerard agreed readily, for his chart of monastic hospices ended here.

As they turned toward the monastery, Gerard could not help but ask why Denys, who had endured convents without complaint until now, suddenly recoiled from them. The soldier hesitated, then burst forth with pent-up grievances.

*"I held my tongue for your sake,"* Denys admitted. *"I saw they had you under their spell—feeding you their honeyed words like sacramental wine. But now, since this is the last, let me speak plainly. These stone dungeons have been my purgatory!"*

Gerard, taken aback, murmured an apology.

*"Ah, no matter,"* Denys waved him off. *"You’re not the first fool led astray by a monk’s robes. But mark me—these places are built to crush a man’s spirit. Every one the same: great gates, echoing cloisters, cold refectories where a man must choke down his words along with his supper. And that cursed bell! Tinkle, tinkle—summoning you to meals you’ve no stomach for, prayers you’ve no heart in, sleep that’s broken before you’ve sunk into it!"*

Gerard sniffed. *"The only bell you love is the jingle of a wench’s laughter."*

*"At least there’s music in that,"* Denys shot back, *"even when it scolds. But these monks? They fret over every grain of salt! ‘Use a knife, not your fingers!’ ‘Wipe your blade on bread, not the cloth!’ Small souls, pinched by their own rules. And if a man speaks freely—"*

*"If he blasphemes, you mean,"* Gerard interjected.

Denys scowled. *"At the last house, I tried to cheer the company—called out ‘Courage, all! The devil—’ And before I could finish, some sour-faced brother hissed, ‘Blaspheme not!’ I told him it was but a soldier’s rallying cry. And what does the fool say? ‘A habit is worse than a slip of the tongue.’ Then he warns me that shouting the devil’s name summons him!"*

Gerard stifled a laugh. *"And was he wrong?"*

Gerard opened his mouth to argue, but Denys barreled on, denouncing monks as hypocrites. *"They skulk about, eyes downcast—not from piety, but guilt! Strip off the cowl, and you’ll find a thief; clothe a rogue in black, and lo—a monk!"*

Gerard’s face darkened, but before he could retort, a bell tolled. Denys perked up at once. *"Ah! The one sweet chime—the call to dinner. My stomach roars ‘Amen!’"* And with that, he strode toward the convent, triumphant in his blasphemies, unaware of the silent disgust festering in his companion’s heart.

The refectory hummed with quiet conversation when Gerard took his seat among the monks. At his table, the talk turned to the sudden passing of Brother Werter, who had been tasked with transcribing the monastery’s charter and rule. Gerard hesitated, then ventured timidly, "My lords, if it please you, I might take up the work—not for payment, but in gratitude for the shelter your order has often given a weary traveler."

A flicker of doubt crossed the monks' faces. One smiled kindly but murmured, "Brother Werter was a master scribe. His hand cannot be matched by any but the most skilled."

Gerard, undeterred, drew from his pouch a scrap of vellum—a deed he had cleaned and inscribed as a sample. The monk who took it stiffened in surprise, then hurried to the high table, genuflecting at the fifth and tenth steps before presenting it to the prior. A murmur rose among the elders; fingers pointed, voices buzzed. Soon the monk returned, beaming, bearing a silver cup of spiced pimentum and a savory game pie for Gerard.

The young scribe bowed deeply, first to the prior, then to his tablemates, and drank. The monks exchanged knowing glances. "You were convent-bred," one declared. "Deny it not!"

Gerard admitted it gladly. "Yet how did you guess?"

"You drank with both hands on the cup," two answered in unison.

Further down the hall, raucous laughter erupted—a burst so loud the prior sent the sub-prior to quell it. Gerard’s cheeks burned, for amid the noise he had caught Denys’s booming voice and the word *courage*.

Soon, Gerard was installed in the late Werter’s cell, surrounded by wax lights, inks, and a scribe’s frame. His work stretched beyond a single night, and he feared Denys—ever disdainful of monks—would refuse to linger. But to his relief, the soldier shrugged. "A day’s rest won’t kill us. Write, and I’ll amuse myself."

When Gerard’s calligraphy won praise, the monks declared their records improved by Werter’s death. The sub-prior pressed a rix-dollar into his hand, along with brushes and pigments from the abbey’s stores. Heartened, Gerard resumed his journey, hopeful his skill might yet win back his lost love.

"Come, Denys," he said cheerfully, displaying his gifts. "See what kindness the brothers have shown me? Now, in fairness, you must admit they are not all hypocrites."

Denys grinned. "I recant! I was a scurrilous fool. These men are sinners, perhaps—but hypocrites? No. They own their vices like honest rogues."

Gerard frowned. "Then you’ve seen nothing to repent of?"

Denys launched into a tale of midnight revels—of monks feasting in secret, of a rope-ladder tryst upon the wall, of raucous songs and flagons disguised as breviaries. Gerard recoiled in disgust. "Wretched men! Corrupters of innocence! Would that the church had crushed them all!"

Denys blinked. "Hypocrites? Nay, they never pretended to virtue. But—" He lowered his voice at Gerard’s warning. The crypt they’d defiled, it was said, was haunted. Voices of the dead whispered there; ghostly music echoed through the stones.

Denys paled. "Had I known, not even mules could have dragged me down!" Then, with a wistful sigh: "And what a merry night I’d have missed."

Their debate was cut short by a lay brother galloping up on a mule, waving a vellum deed—the contract between Ghysbrecht and Floris Brandt. Gerard, overcome, kissed the mule’s nose in gratitude before carefully stitching the parchment into his doublet, his thoughts flying to Sevenbergen.

At last, they reached the promised Burgundian inn—*Les Trois Poissons*. Denys frowned at a sprawling new inscription:

Gerard stared. Denys, after a laborious deciphering, puffed up with pride. "Ah! ‘Here they lodge man and horse.’ A fine welcome indeed!"

And with that, they stepped inside.

They met the landlord in the narrow passage, his cap doffed in a low bow.

“Welcome, messieurs.”

Gerard laughed. “Come, we are not in Germany!”

In the common room, the mistress—a buxom woman of forty—greeted them with a curtsy and a warm smile. “Trouble yourselves to sit, fair sir,” she said, dusting the chairs with her apron, though they scarcely needed it.

“Thank you, dame,” Gerard replied, amused. *The trouble of sitting?* he mused. *Soon, the labor of eating, the toil of digestion, and by Hercules’ aid, the great struggle of sleep.*

Denys ordered supper for two.

“Only two?” Gerard exclaimed. “What, do we not wait for forty more?”

Denys grinned. “Courage, *camarade*! *Le diable—*”

“*Est mort*,” Gerard finished dryly.

The inn’s hierarchy was clear—women ruled here. The landlord drifted aimlessly, bowing to every passerby, while the women brushed past him like leaves in a breeze, unheeding.

A maid brought their meal, followed by the mistress, hands empty but words plentiful.

“Eat well, my masters,” she urged. “Your only enemy here lies beneath your knife.”

They obeyed. The mistress settled nearby, chatting as though they were old friends. Soon, she bustled off, replaced by her daughter—a gentle, dark-eyed girl of twenty, who took a seat beside them. She lingered, asking after Gerard’s travels, her kindness softening the rough edges of the road.

When she left, Gerard sighed. “An inn? It feels like home.”

Denys puffed with pride. “*Qui fit Français, il fit courtois.*”

“Courteous? Nay, Christian. To welcome strangers as kin—that is grace.”

Their third visitor was Marion, the chambermaid—a sharp-tongued, quick-witted woman with laughing eyes and a bare, sturdy arm. The moment she entered, a traveler flung a jest at her. Marion riposted, and the room roared at *his* expense. One by one, men tried their wit against hers; one by one, they fell.

Gerard gaped. “Denys, now I see why they say a woman’s tongue is her sword.”

Denys, ever the chivalrous fool, cheered her on. “Courage, *ma mie*! *Le diable est mort!*”

She wheeled on him. “How can he be dead while archers still draw breath?”

Laughter erupted. Marion turned next to Gerard, eyeing his sparse beard. “*Monsieur* might like his whiskers starched.”

Gerard, unruffled, gestured to Denys’s bristling chin. “Mine is unworthy. Try this fine growth.”

Marion sniffed. “I’ll tend it when I starch the broomsticks.”

The mistress returned, and Marion’s fire dimmed to meekness in an instant—a performance wasted, for the dame had heard all and was amused.

“Marion,” she chided, “if you laid an egg each time you cackled, we’d never lack omelets.”

When Gerard asked for their reckoning, the mistress waved him off. “Pay when you leave. ‘Lose the guest, find the money’—that’s our rule.”

Gerard pointed outside. “But your sign says—’”

“Bah! Let it stick to the wall.” She gestured to the ceiling, black with smoke and scribbles—accounts kept by knife-scratches in the soot. The sign, she explained, was merely to deter beggars.

“How do you know *I* can pay?”

She smiled. “Yours is the best face to enter here this autumn.”

Denys feigned offense. “And mine?”

“Honest, but I’d not meet it on a dark road.”

Marion giggled. “Said I not you could charm them still, mistress?”

“Reading faces is no marvel,” the dame told Gerard. “’Tis the first thing a girl learns in an inn.”

“And frying eggs the second,” Marion added. “No—lying. Frying’s third.”

“And holding her tongue last,” the mistress shot back.

Marion sighed, flopping dramatically onto Gerard. “Alas, I am but a chambermaid—spurned, discarded—”

Gerard shoved her off. “Enough! Must I endure jests while speaking sense?”

Marion pirouetted. “Ah, mistress, you reign supreme—at least with babes.”

The dame shook her head. “Girl, we excel in two arts: reading faces and frying eggs. ’Tis all we know.”

Gerard frowned. “But how, by Denys’s face, did you guess his folly—his *mulierosity*?”

“His *what*?”

“A word I coined. It means he is wrapped in women like a vine.”

The dame chuckled. “Eyes never lie. His follow every skirt. Yours turn to the speaker—man or woman—like sense.”

Denys guffawed. “You misread him! This smooth-face is a Turk at heart—smitten with one Margaret, a Dutch lass.”

The dame nodded sagely. “Then cling to her, boy. Better one true love than a hundred follies.”

Marion smirked. “Aye, and his purse will thank him.”

The dame shooed her. “Enough clack!”

The landlord piped up. “Must the cock never crow?”

“Not indoors,” his wife retorted. “The hen rules here.”

Gerard quoted: *“Femme veut en toute saison, être dame en sa maison.”*

The dame clapped. “A true saying! Give me an old saw over new lies any day.”

“And me a young husband,” Marion sighed. “Ah, had one spoken then—”

Denys grinned. “Some poor soul’s spared.”

As night fell, the household gathered—travelers, host, servants—around the fire, trading tales. Gerard told one of martyred saints, thrilling them all before drowsing off.

Marion lit a rush and led him to a room with two clean beds.

“Paradise,” he murmured. “I’ve not lain in bare linen since Holland.”

She smirked. “Then let flax meet down—so, *allons!*” She offered her cheek.

“What?”

“’Tis custom—goodnight’s toll.”

“Not in my land.”

She huffed. “Here, even greybeards pay it. Must I beg?”

“Ask Denys. He’s *mulierose*; I am not.”

She sighed. “To bed unpaid, then. Rare youth, who resists brazen maids!” At the door, she turned, solemn. “The Virgin guard thee, wanderer. Amen.”

Then—footsteps, laughter downstairs.

*What a creature,* Gerard thought, yawning. *Half imp, half saint.*

Sleep took him to Sevenbergen.

Morning found him trapped—his clothes gone. Marion entered, arms laden with fresh-washed linen.

“Oh, bless you!”

She winked. “Found me good at last?”

“Was this another custom?”

“Nay, but we ask travelers at night if they’ll have washing. You slept so sound, I spared you.”

Denys, ever the commentator, interjected. “*She* spared *you*—your skin’s like cream, she says.”

Marion rolled her eyes. “Guess again.”

Gerard smiled. “I’m sorry I refused you—anything.”

“Ah! Now he’d pay. Too late. *Quits.*” She vanished, victorious.

At breakfast, they settled their score and prepared to leave. Then—custom struck again. The women kissed them farewell, the landlord too, with hearty cries of “Return soon!”

On the road, they marveled at the shoemaker’s courtesy, the town’s pebbled walk (“For dry feet on Sundays,” said Denys), and then—a gibbet. Two skeletons swung.

Gerard shuddered. “Must our welcome end so?”

Denys shrugged. “Each rogue aloft means one less below.”

Farther on, a wheel studded with iron prongs held tattered bones.

Gerard hid his face. “What’s left of a man—once like us!”

Denys scoffed. “A thief.”

“How know you? Have no innocents suffered?”

“None of my kin swung.”

“Luck, not virtue. The saints died hard—even in Burgundy’s shadow.”

Denys reddened. “Blasphemy! The gibbet proves guilt here.”

Gerard sighed. “Then on Calvary, you’d have cheered the crosses—Roman gibbets all.”

Denys sputtered. “Ancient lies! No matter now.”

“Does time wash blood from hands?” Gerard turned away. “Our hearts stand far apart. Yet I love thee.”

By afternoon, wailing filled the air. A grim procession approached: pikemen herding a hundred souls—men silent, women weeping, children clutched in arms.

Gerard halted. “What crime can these all share?”

Denys nudged him. “Ask.”

The officer, though wary, answered courteously at Gerard’s plea.

“They are vagrants—cleared from the roads by order.”

“But the babes—”

“The law spares none. Idleness breeds thieves.”

Gerard stared as they passed, his joy in Burgundy now shadowed.

Denys clapped his shoulder. “Come. The world is as it is.”

They walked on, the sun sinking, the road stretching—a little wiser, a little heavier.

A rush of footsteps echoed through the inn as half a dozen rough but welcoming hands stretched out, seizing Denys and Gerard in firm grips.

**"You've saved our lives tonight, lads,"** Denys declared, his voice thick with relief. **"You've saved us."**

The scene before them was one of stark contrasts—torchlight flickering against breastplates, bronzed faces of archers beside the pallid, bound thieves, and the fallen giant sprawled in his own blood, abandoned without ceremony. Gerard moved among the soldiers, clasping each man’s hand with shining eyes. In return, they embraced him, pressing rough kisses to his cheeks. He returned the gesture, then turned to a young archer, his own age, with a weary smile.

**"Good soldier, do me a kindness—keep watch over me. A strange drowsiness weighs me down. Let no one slit my throat while I sleep, for pity’s sake."**

The archer laughed, thinking it a jest, but Gerard had already slumped into a deep slumber. Denys watched, puzzled, but held his tongue—it suited his purpose.

Two soldiers poked at the Abbot’s corpse with their boots, marveling.

Denys scoffed at first, but hesitated. *If Gerard wakes and babbles about divine intervention, I’ll look the fool.* But with the boy unconscious, he grinned.

**"Well, lads, I ran him through, but others swarmed me. Needed my blade back, so—foot to his gut, a good yank, and sent him flying! Died midair, I’d wager. Made quite the entrance, eh?"**

The archers chuckled, but one grumbled, **"Easy to say ‘let’s drink’ when there’s no drink to be had."**

Denys smirked. **"Then follow me. My nose sniffs out wine like a hound on a hare."**

Torch in hand, they descended into a dank cellar, the air thick with mold and saltpetre crusting the walls.

**"Ah! That’s the smell of good vintage,"** Denys declared. Then his eyes narrowed. **"What’s that in the corner? A heap of rags? No—a man!"**

The torchlight revealed a trembling figure—the innkeeper, pale as death.

**"Up, coward!"** barked an archer. **"The thieves are bound, and we’re thirsty for your wine."**

**"Th-they’re caught?"** the man stammered, knees wobbling. **"Wine? At once, sirs!"**

Denys stepped forward, blocking his path. **"Hold. He’s one of them."**

The landlord’s face twisted. **"That lying wench! Curse her!"**

Denys’s eyes gleamed. **"Now why curse her, unless her words struck true?"**

The man faltered, but the archers shrugged. **"Let the burghers judge him. For now, let’s drink."**

Denys wasn’t done. **"Then hold him. A prisoner can pour wine, if his hands aren’t tied."**

The innkeeper, desperate, babbled about unpaid rent, but the soldiers only laughed. As they moved to leave, Denys halted them.

He prodded a heap of debris—bones clattered to the floor.

**"Nothing but kitchen scraps,"** the landlord insisted, sweat beading on his brow.

Denys lifted one, examining it in the torchlight. **"A man’s shinbone."**

The innkeeper’s face turned ashen. **"S-surely oxen—"**

**"Then why shake like a leaf?"** Denys’s voice turned icy. He dug deeper, uncovering more—skulls, finger bones, then, with a choked cry, a golden tress of woman’s hair.

**"By God,"** he whispered, clutching it. **"Had you as many lives as hairs here, I’d take them all."**

Seizing the landlord, he forced him to his knees, grinding his face into the bones. **"Smell your work, butcher!"**

The man shrieked, then whimpered like a beaten cur.

With a bowstring, Denys threaded a skull and hung it around the wretch’s neck. **"Wear your trophy, carrion."**

Kicking him into the kitchen, they found the alderman taking statements. The burgher recoiled at the ghastly sight—the landlord, bloodied and collared with death.

**"Bind him,"** the alderman ordered coldly. **"He’s the blackest of the lot."**

The man wailed, begging for the skull’s removal.

**"It suits you,"** the alderman spat. **"A fitting crest for your foul heart."**

As constables hauled the prisoners away, the clerk, a fussy little man, chirped, **"Now, the hand! Where’s the hand?"**

**"Above, nailed to the door,"** Denys replied grimly.

The clerk scurried off, only to be bowled over by fleeing constables—terrified by the glowing corpse upstairs.

By dawn, the inn stood silent, justice served. Only a lone constable remained, watching over the sleeping Gerard, while Denys kept vigil, his hand resting on the golden lock of hair—a promise of vengeance yet to come.

Gerard stirred from his slumber to find Denys gazing down at him, concern etched across his weathered face.

“You sleep like the dead,” Denys remarked, nudging him. “The sun’s already climbed past noon.”

Gerard sat up, blinking as if emerging from a dream. “A blessed sleep indeed,” he murmured. “It has drawn a veil between me and last night’s horrors. To think we sit here now, whole and breathing—it feels like some dark vision, not a thing that truly happened.”

Denys clapped him on the shoulder. “A wise way to see it, lad. What’s past is but a shadow. Now, break your fast, and let it fade.” He hesitated, then added, “I promised to bring you before the magistrate by midday.”

Gerard tore a piece of rye bread, soaking it in wine before eating. The taste grounded him, pulling him back to the present. Still, when they retraced their steps to the scene of the ambush, his stomach twisted. The trampled earth, the splashes of dried blood—it was real. He shuddered, quickening his pace until they reached the safety of the town.

Denys led him to *The White Hart*, a sturdy inn with whitewashed walls and a swinging sign. “No cutthroats here,” he assured Gerard. “The landlord’s a burgess, an honest man. This was where I meant us to lodge last night, but—” He shrugged. “Nightfall had other plans.”

Gerard’s jaw tightened. “And the woman at the door—the one Jeanneton warned us against.”

Denys gave a dry chuckle. “Aye, and yet it was a woman who saved our necks, risked her own to do it.”

Gerard’s chest tightened. “She’s worth more than gold. I’d give every coin in my purse to thank her.”

“*Parbleu*, so would I.”

“Then where is she?”

“The alderman may know. But first, we must face his questions.”

The magistrate received them with an odd expression—first a flicker of surprise, then a slow, knowing smirk. He listened as Gerard recounted the night’s events, scribbling notes with a quill. When he finished, he delivered his verdict.

“You’ll remain in town until the trial,” he said sternly. “To leave would mean fines—or worse, a cell.”

The two exchanged uneasy glances.

The alderman softened slightly. “It won’t be long. The thieves were caught red-handed.” Then, with a cryptic note, he added, “Besides, you couldn’t leave this week even if you wished.”

Denys frowned at that, but Gerard barely registered the words. His mind was elsewhere.

“Your worship,” he pressed, “we’ve searched the town for the girl who saved us—the one who came to you in the night. Do you know where she might be?”

The alderman raised a brow. “And who might this benefactress be?”

Gerard leaned forward, urgency sharpening his voice. “The brave soul who risked her life for ours. Where is she? We must thank her—*please*.”

The question hung in the air, unanswered.

**“Messire Bailiff,”** interjected Denys, **“do you lock up every witness, then?”**

Flattered by the title, the alderman grew talkative. **“In cases of blood, we hold all who might flee justice. Women are fickle creatures—today they swear truth, tomorrow they recant. This one wept for betraying five men to the gallows. Had they been women, we might have trusted her to return. But no—she stays.”** He adjusted his spectacles. **“Besides, she was one of them.”**

Denys and Gerard protested in unison.

**“Spare your outrage,”** said the alderman dryly. **“Here it is, plain as day.”** He tapped a parchment. **“‘Jean Hardy confessed that the girl Manon lured victims for Georges Vipont, her lover—hanged last month. Since his death, she has raged against the band, blaming them for his capture. It is his belief she betrayed them out of spite.’”**

**“His belief?”** Gerard’s voice sharpened. **“What weight has the word of a thief, desperate for revenge? Is a criminal not a liar by trade? And here, with motive to damn her?”** He stepped forward, eyes burning. **“Sir, can you not see the difference between those hardened men and this broken girl? Would you hang her beside them? She risked her life for ours!”**

The alderman shifted, discomfort flickering across his face. Gerard dropped to his knees. **“I beg you—spare her! Denys and I owe her our lives. Must we watch her die and then cut our own throats at her feet?”**

The alderman huffed, torn between duty and pity. **“Enough! Daniel—fetch the town clerk.”** When the clerk arrived, the alderman muttered, **“This boy’s heart is set on saving her. Can we bend the law?”**

The clerk, a stickler, shook his head. **“She was no accomplice. Had she been, we might have pardoned her testimony.”**

**“She did better than an accomplice!”** Gerard cried. **“She stopped the crime and ran to expose it!”**

The alderman waved him off. **“Law is law.”** Yet after a heated debate—and spurred by the clerk’s stubbornness—he relented. Manon was freed on bond, with Denys depositing gold as surety.

As they left the courthouse, two processions clogged the street: prisoners bound for the gallows, and a silent train bearing bones to consecrated ground. The crowds jeered as the cart of condemned men jostled against the mourners’ bier—a grim collision of crime and consequence.

Gerard, solemn, followed the dead to their burial. After the rites, he sought a mass for their souls, haggling with the priest over the fee—until they discovered a shared cunning in piety and thrift. Over wine, the priest shared a tale of his own: how he’d once outwitted his flock by charging burial fees in advance, lest they cheat the Church by dying on the gallows. **“Greed against greed,”** he chuckled. **“They hanged fewer men after that.”**

As dusk fell, Gerard returned to the inn, where Manon wept in her chamber. The landlord, sensing profit in her notoriety, kept her safe. Outside, the town buzzed with the day’s drama—justice served, mercy granted, and the dead at last laid to rest.

But in the churchyard, a skull rolled loose from a disturbed pillar—the defiant bones of a nobleman who’d demanded an upright burial. **“No base man shall pass over my stomach!”** his descendant laughed, kicking the relic aside. The priest sighed. **“Pride survives even death.”**

And so the day ended—with the living weighed by their deeds, the dead by their dust, and the threads of fate tangled yet unbroken.

The prisoners stood bound in the marketplace, exposed for two hours so that any who recognized them as perpetrators of other crimes might come forward. Yet no one did—unsurprising, for they were creatures of the night, preying on weary travelers, most of whom were strangers passing through.

Just as the guards prepared to lead them away, a piercing scream cut through the crowd. A woman, her eyes wide with horror, pointed at one of the accused before collapsing in a faint. The bystanders surged around her, torn between aiding her and satisfying their curiosity.

“She pointed at *him*,” one insisted.
“No, at *this* one,” argued another.
“Fools,” snapped a third, “it was the hangdog with the hair around his neck!”

The woman revived with a gasp, then flew at the landlord like a fury. “My child!” she shrieked. “Give me back my child!” She clawed at the golden locks hanging around his neck, tearing them free and pressing them to her lips. Then, the truth crashing upon her, she wailed, “Dead! My girl is dead!” and crumpled, sobbing, over the stolen tresses.

The crowd roared like a beast, surging toward the murderer. In those days, every man carried death at his belt, and the prisoner’s life would have ended then and there—had Denys not drawn his sword and planted himself between the mob and their prey.

“Back!” he bellowed. “The first man to touch him dies!” His fellow archers rallied to him, steel flashing, holding the furious townsfolk at bay.

“What madness is this?” Denys shouted. “Would you rob the executioner of his due? Let the law grind him slow!”

The mob growled but yielded to the threat of blades. The officers, sensing the danger, formed a guard and hurried the prisoners back to the gaol, the crowd hurling curses after them.

Denys, satisfied they were secure, strode toward *The White Hart*, expecting to find Gerard. On the way, he passed two girls leaning from an upper window, their cheeks rosy, their laughter easy. He tipped his hat. They smiled. He spoke; they answered. Soon, he invited them to dine with him.

They hesitated.

He shrugged.

They agreed.

And so, in charming company, he forgot Gerard entirely—while Gerard, bewildered and afraid, was hauled toward the gaol.

Only as they neared the prison did Gerard’s wits return. “By whose order am I arrested?” he demanded.

“The vice-baillie’s,” said the constable.

“The vice-baillie? What quarrel has he with a stranger? This charge of sorcery is a lie! I am no magician—only a poor traveler far from home.”

The officer scowled. “Show him the warrant, Jacques.”

Jacques held the writ at arm’s length, while the larger constable gripped Gerard, wary lest he snatch the document. But Gerard had no such thought. He craned his neck and read—then gaped in disbelief.

The vice-baillie who had signed the order was the *same alderman* who had been so friendly earlier.

“There’s some mistake,” Gerard insisted. “Take me to him!”

The officers exchanged glances.

“We’ve no orders for that,” Jacques muttered.

“A silver écu each if you do,” Gerard offered.

A pause. A nod.

“Very well,” said the constable. “But mark—this is a favor.”

They led him to the alderman’s house. Gerard, heart in his throat, stammered, “What offense have I given since I left your presence with Denys and Manon?”

The alderman frowned. “None that I know.”

Gerard pointed to the writ.

“Ah!” The alderman rubbed his temple. “My memory fails me. I signed this at dawn—ah, yes. The charge is sorcery. You stand accused of setting a corpse alight with hellfire.”

Gerard paled. “If that were true, I’d burn for it.”

“Precisely.”

“Then for mercy’s sake, let me speak to the curé!”

The alderman grimaced. “The Church is harsher on sorcery than we are.”

“But I am *innocent*!”

“So you say. Very well—take him to the curé, but guard him well.”

They found the curé in his workshop, mending a wheelbarrow. Gerard spilled his tale, desperate. “They would hang me for using phosphorus—a trick of chemistry, not magic!”

The curé’s eyes gleamed. “Show me.”

Gerard hesitated. “The room must be dark.”

The curé shuttered the windows. “Now.”

“But I need a face—a dead one. That was what made it terrible.”

The curé groped about and produced a wooden saint. “Here.”

Gerard recoiled. “A *saint*? That’s blasphemy!”

“Pah! It’ll wash off.”

Reluctantly, Gerard daubed the eyes and nostrils with phosphorus, then the hair. The saint’s face glowed eerily.

The curé gasped. “By the Virgin! No wonder they feared you.”

He seized Gerard’s arm. “Come.”

They returned to the alderman, where a figure in red hose and a black gown stood whispering—Mangis, the accuser, a self-proclaimed *white* sorcerer.

The curé scoffed. “This charlatan cries ‘magic’ at honest men to hide his own frauds. The phosphorus is a harmless trick, known to scholars.”

Mangis bristled. “I am no fraud! I battle Satan’s minions—unlike *him*!” He jabbed a finger at Gerard.

The curé turned on him. “You babbling fool! Your lies burned innocents at Dol—and when the truth came out, who paid? Not you. Shall I tell the bishop how you wear the Devil’s colors?” He pointed at Mangis’ red and black garb.

Mangis blanched. “I—I withdraw the charge.”

“Then begone.”

With the accuser dismissed, the curé turned to the alderman. “The boy’s no sorcerer. Release him.”

The alderman sighed. “So be it.”

Outside, Gerard clasped the curé’s hand. “You saved me! How can I repay you?”

The curé waved him off. “Keep your coin. But come dine with me tomorrow.”

Grateful, Gerard hurried to the inn—only to find Denys dicing with two brazen girls.

Gerard scowled. “*Forget not Jeanneton!*”

Denys laughed. “What of her?”

“‘*How little women are worth!*’”

The girls sneered. “None slander women but those too ugly or dull to please them.”

Gerard flushed. “Brazen toads! My comrade shames himself with you.”

Denys chuckled. “He scolds like a monk. Pay him no heed.”

Gerard slammed the door, then turned to the landlord for wine. The man served him a short measure in a deceitful bottle.

Gerard glared. “This is no pint.”

The landlord shrugged. “I don’t make the bottles.”

“Then I’ll take it to the vice-baillie.”

The landlord paled. “Wait! A mistake—let me correct it.”

As they haggled, Denys stormed in, roaring.

“Robbed! Those vipers took my purse—fifteen gold crowns!”

Gerard urged him to seek the provost-marshal, but Denys refused.

“The law? Useless! They’re long gone.”

Then, grudgingly, he confessed: he’d offered *marriage* to Manon.

Gerard gaped. “You *what*?”

Denys shrugged. “A ruse. I told her, ‘Mademoiselle, though marriage is not my habit, your virtues compel me. Be my wife.’”

Gerard snorted. “And she replied—?”

“With a slap.”

Denys grinned.

Gerard groaned.

The road outside the town was trampled with fresh wolf tracks, a grim reminder of the night’s unseen dangers.

“We did well to heed the old man’s warning,” Gerard murmured.

Denys chuckled. “Aye, and I heard them last night—scurrying under our window, howling for a taste of some plump burgher. But no kind soul tossed them a morsel.”

Gerard smiled absently, lost in thought.

“What weighs so heavy on that mind of yours?” Denys prodded.

“Your kindness.”

Denys stiffened, his good humor vanishing. “A civil question deserves a civil answer.”

Gerard blinked. “I meant no offense. But if another claimed you lacked kindness, I’d call him liar. You see only women’s charms, not men’s virtues.” He shook his head. “Last night, you would have wed me to the ‘Golden Head’ and left yourself alone. That was kindness.”

Denys snorted, his temper easing. “Call it friendship, not kindness. Though, truth be told, my conscience pricks me now for letting you turn your back on fortune. A truer friend would’ve hamstrung you—left you nursing at the inn till that lass softened your stubborn heart.” He grinned. “I had the blade ready, but you whimper at a scratch, so I lost my nerve.”

Gerard gaped. Before he could reply, hoofbeats thundered behind them. A rider on a mule galloped wildly toward them, skidding to a halt.

“You’re from the ‘Golden Head’?” the man demanded.

They nodded.

“Which is the younger?”

Denys smirked. “He who was born later.”

The rider squinted. “Your beard’s ripe, his is green. Here, boy.” He thrust out a packet. “Our mistress says you left this.”

Gerard frowned. “I left nothing.”

“Call me liar, will you?” The man scowled. “And where’s my pourboire?”

Denys tossed him a coin, and the rider sped off as fast as he’d come. Gerard unwrapped the packet—layer after layer of silk—until at last, a gold ring with a gleaming amethyst tumbled into his palm. A note fluttered free:

Denys whistled. “The little dove! Though she’d have done better to aim her affections at me.”

Gerard flushed. “Spare me your jests. I’ll not wear it—Margaret’s ring is all I need.” He kissed the simple band on his finger.

Denys rolled his eyes but relented, helping Gerard secure the amethyst inside his bonnet for luck.

The road stretched before them, silent save for the crunch of snow underfoot. Then, cresting a hill, a troop of Burgundian soldiers appeared—fifty strong, banners fluttering.

Denys’s face lit. “The Bastard of Burgundy himself! A leader who fights like a common soldier—my kind of man.”

But their admiration turned to dread as the troop halted before them.

“Halt!” The Bastard’s voice was steel. “Why do you flee south when Burgundy calls north?”

Denys stood firm. “I go on leave to see my kin at Remiremont.”

“Not now. Mount that spare mule—you ride with us.”

Denys tightened his grip on Gerard’s hand. “I cannot. I’ve sworn to see this lad safe.”

The Bastard’s eyes turned icy. “Jarnac! Shoot this dog where he stands.”

Six archers dismounted, leveling their weapons.

Gerard’s heart stopped. “No! He’ll go—I swear it! Only let us say farewell!”

The Bastard gave a curt nod. “Make it swift.”

Gerard clutched Denys, their words tumbling out in desperate whispers.

“Tell Margaret everything,” Gerard begged.

“Strasbourg—then down the Rhine to Rotterdam,” Denys urged. “We’ll meet again.”

But time was a thief. Rough hands tore them apart, forcing Denys onto the mule.

Gerard ran after them, lungs burning, until the troop vanished around a bend. Then his legs gave way. He crumpled to the snow, hollow with loss.

A cavalcade of nobles passed, velvet-clad and laughing. Then—cruel irony—three of their servants circled back, leveling a petronel.

“Your purse, boy.”

Gerard laughed bitterly. “Take it. And tell your master may it freeze his heart and damn his soul.”

Penniless, he staggered on until exhaustion dragged him down at the edge of a wood. The cold seeped into his bones.

The innkeeper’s rhyme whispered in his mind. But home was Margaret, not gold.

Yet how to reach her now?

The snow beckoned, soft as a shroud. His eyelids grew heavy.

With a sigh, he closed his eyes.

Rough travel has a charm for us English—and so do its tales, even when the wanderer is no lovelorn pilgrim. And ancient friendship holds at least the fascination of a relic. Yet the heart of this story lies in Holland, and it is time to return there, to the simple lives and quiet dramas that have filled the days of all ages.

Jorian Ketel arrived at Peter’s house to claim Margaret’s promise, but she lay ill in bed. Peter, upon hearing his demand, turned him away with harsh words, and a few bystanders even threatened to duck him in the nearest ditch. The town of Sevenbergen was aflame with sympathy for father and daughter, and in such moods, justice is swift and indiscriminate. Jorian left in bitter anger, regretting his kindness—a remorse not uncommon, and at least sincere.

Dierich Brower, caught at *The Three Kings* plying a drunkard with drink to pry loose Martin Wittenhaagen’s whereabouts, was seized and flung into a horsepond, with warnings of worse should he ever show his face in the town again. At last, roused by civic pride, the burgomaster of Sevenbergen sent a stern message to Ghysbrecht of Tergou, reminding him he had overstepped his authority and must in future address complaints through proper channels.

Ghysbrecht, swallowing his rage, replied smoothly that the man he pursued was a Tergovian, one Gerard, accused of stealing town records. Since Gerard had fled abroad—likely with the documents—the matter was closed. Yet this calm was but a mask. Foiled in Sevenbergen, he turned his spies elsewhere, seeking to learn from Gerard’s family where he had gone. To his dismay, they did not know. This gnawed at him. If Gerard lurked nearby, he might yet uncover the truth and return for vengeance. From that day, those near Ghysbrecht marked a change in him—a brooding unease, a furtive watchfulness, as if he expected a blow from the dark.

The little household at Tergou, once so harmonious, now wore the same faces but not the same spirit. Kate, knowing her brothers’ part in Gerard’s exile, shuddered at their sight, though she kept silent for fear of deepening the strife. Catherine, with a woman’s sharp eye, noted this but said nothing, watching and waiting. Cornelis and Sybrandt played their parts well, feigning sorrow to deceive their father and Giles, but their hidden satisfaction could not escape the women’s gaze—

Mistrust now sat at the table, a poor substitute for Gerard’s bright face, whose absence was felt only when he was gone.

The old hosier’s pride had been wounded by his son’s defiance, and he bore himself stiffly, refusing to speak Gerard’s name. Yet beneath the stern front, nature tugged at his heart. "If I but knew where the boy is, and that he is safe," he would sigh, "small would be my care." One could not help thinking that had Gerard walked in then, there would have been embraces, not reproaches.

The family was taken aback when Gerard’s tale spread through the town within a week. Worse still, the people’s hearts sided with the lovers, blaming the harsh parents and the meddling burgomaster.

"Mother," Kate ventured one day, "they say Margaret is down with fever—a burning fever. Her father fears for her."

"Margaret? What Margaret?" Catherine asked, with forced calm.

"Gerard’s Margaret."

Catherine’s voice rose sharp. "How dare you name that girl in this house? She is the ruin of my boy—the flower of my flock! Because of her, he is not a priest among us but a wanderer in the wide world, and I a broken-hearted mother." Then, softening: "Don’t weep, child. I do wrong to speak harshly. But oh, Kate, you cannot know a mother’s fears. I bear up by day, but at night I see him in dreams—torn by beasts, or in robbers’ hands, their knives raised over him. And all for that girl! Her very name is poison to me."

"I’ll not grieve you more, mother," Kate murmured, though her heart ached.

Yet while Margaret’s name was forbidden in one house, it was often spoken in pity elsewhere. Sevenbergen whispered of the village beauty "dying for love," though this did her little justice. Gerard was not dead, and she never doubted his faith. Her father needed her, and but for her failing strength, love would not have kept her from her duties—though she might have moved through them with a heavy heart.

The fever left her at last, but in such weakness that life seemed a burden. Love and friendship might have been healing hands, but she had to mend alone. Many days she lay weeping, listless, until one afternoon she woke to find two women in her room. One was a servant; the other, by the rich fur at her collar and the silver streak in her hair, was a lady of standing. Their faces were kind.

Margaret tried to rise, but the elder woman gently pressed her back. "Lie still, child. We come not to trouble you, but to cheer you, God willing. Now, guess who we are?"

Margaret’s eyes brightened. "You are the demoiselle Van Eyck, and this is Reicht Heynes. Gerard spoke often of you—of your goodness to him. Madam, he has no truer friend now." The thought brought fresh tears.

Reicht, tender-hearted, wept in sympathy, but her mistress chided her. "A fine nurse you make!" Then, with gentle art, she drew Margaret from her sorrow, coaxing her head onto her shoulder, stroking her hair.

"Ah, such hair!" the old lady admired.

Reicht joined in praising it, and Margaret, though frail, was not past the reach of such kindness. "Once I thought it plain," she confessed, "but he praised it, and since then I’ve been vain of it—saints forgive me."

"The greater fools are those who do not love," said the old lady briskly.

Margaret looked up, wondering.

This was but the first of many visits. Either Margaret Van Eyck or Reicht came nearly every day, and under their care, the girl grew stronger—Reicht crediting her broths, Margaret the kind words that gave her heart a reason to heal.

Meanwhile, Martin Wittenhaagen had ridden to Rotterdam, seeking the bull by the horns—the bull being Philip the Good, Duke of Burgundy. Learning the court was at Ghent, he pressed on, but found himself thwarted by lackeys and pages. At last, he waylaid the duke on a hunt, roaring for mercy.

"Why, what danger threatens?" laughed the duke.

"Grace for an old soldier hunted by burghers!"

Kings, for all their pride, often show kindness to lowly men, who neither rival nor flatter them. Philip reined in, listening as Martin recounted Gerard’s imprisonment, the escape from the tower, and the bloody chase through the woods. The tale so gripped the duke that when courtiers interrupted, he swore and threatened to lop off the next head that broke his pleasure.

"By St. Luke!" he cried when Martin finished. "Here’s sport in my own lands, and I miss it! A man-hunt! I’ve never seen one."

"My luck was poor," said Martin dryly. "I was on the wrong side of the dogs."

The duke chuckled. "What do you ask?"

"A pardon—for Gerard and me."

"For prison-breaking? Birds will fly. For striking the burgomaster? A boar turns at bay. For killing the hounds?" Here the duke frowned.

"They were not yours, sire—the ranger’s."

Philip relented. "I’ll not sacrifice an old soldier to a ranger’s dogs. You shall have your pardon—and Gerard’s. And tell that burgomaster his high-handed ways displease me. I’ll have no petty kings in Holland."

The next day, the pardon was signed. Martin returned in triumph to find Margaret sitting pale by the fire. Waving the parchment, he cried, "A free pardon! Send for Gerard when you will—no burgomaster dare touch him now!"

She trembled, reading it, kissing it, then flung her arms around Martin’s neck. When her joy had settled, she spoke of her new friend, the demoiselle Van Eyck. "I long to show her this—but I’ve not strength to walk so far."

Martin laughed. "Take my mule."

"*Your* mule?"

He admitted he had grown fond of the beast, forgetting it was Ghysbrecht’s. "Tomorrow I’ll return her—but tonight she shall bear you to Tergou."

At dusk, Margaret rode to her friend’s house, her cheeks flushed with unaccustomed color, the pardon safe in her bosom.

Some hearts grow old at forty; others stay forever young at eighty. Margaret Van Eyck’s was of the latter kind—an evergreen, vibrant with life. Her affection for young Margaret Brandt burned with the ardor of youth, yet it was no fleeting fancy. The elder Margaret possessed a keen eye for character, and in the younger she recognized something rare in their sex: genuineness. But beyond the girl’s own virtues, another force worked in the old artist’s heart—human nature itself.

Strange as it may seem to the unobservant, the heart warms more readily to those we have helped than to those who help us. The Greeks noted it; poets have sung of it.

> *"I heard, and thought how side by side
> We two had stemmed the battle’s tide..."*

The verse spoke of bonds forged not by favors received, but by favors given.

And Margaret Van Eyck had given much. She had broken her own habits to visit the ailing girl, nursed her, soothed her, petted her, and done more for her recovery than all the medicines in the world. Now her heart swelled with tenderness for the recipient of her kindness, and she loved her more deeply than she had ever loved Gerard—though it was for Gerard’s sake she had first sought her out.

When she saw the roses return to Margaret’s cheeks and read the parchment that had brought them there, she yielded without protest.

"Sweetheart," she said, "I had hoped he might stay in Italy five or six years, return wealthy, and above all, a master of his craft. But your happiness comes first. If you cannot live without him, then we must bring him home as swiftly as may be."

"Ah, madam! You see my very thoughts." The young woman blushed and lowered her eyes. "But how to reach him? That puzzles me. He is gone to Italy—but where? He spoke of Florence, of Rome..."

"Come to the right place at the right time," the elder Margaret said with a smile. "Hans Memling was here today. He leaves for Italy next week—'to improve his hand,' he says. Though Heaven knows it needs more than improvement."

"But how will he find Gerard?"

"He knows Gerard well enough. They supped here together more than once, thick as thieves. His path will cross Gerard’s sooner or later—artists haunt the same cities. Write your letter, copy the pardon into it, and I’ll see it safely delivered. In six months at most, Gerard will hold it in his hands. And when he does—" She chuckled. "He’ll kiss it, tuck it in his bosom, and come flying home. What are you smiling at? And why so rosy all of a sudden? Ah, happy days are coming, my little pearl."

Meanwhile, Martin sat in the kitchen with a black-jack of ale before him, Reicht Heynes spinning nearby. And how she plied him with questions that night!

Hans Memling had been a pupil of Jan Van Eyck and his sister. A painter of skill, if not of fame, he was a good-natured soul with one flaw: a fondness for the bottle that kept him ever lean of purse. It also ensured he remained a frequent guest at Margaret Van Eyck’s table—and thus bound to her service.

The letter was written, the pardon transcribed, and all entrusted to the elder Margaret. The following week, true to his word, Hans returned from Flanders. She gave him the letter and a gold piece for his journey. He seemed in haste to depart.

"All the better," she remarked. "He’ll reach Italy the sooner."

But like a horse that strains to gallop only to flag after the first strides, Hans’s zeal cooled the moment he reached Tergou’s principal inn and spied two boon companions in the bay window. He stopped for a parting drink—one that his friends insisted on paying, then another, and another, until his tongue loosened like a wine-skin’s stopper. He boasted of teaching the Italians to paint, of his exploits in battle, of conquests amorous and martial, until at last, brimming with loose words, he let slip his mission: a letter to one Gerard, a townsman of theirs.

"A good fellow!" he proclaimed, slapping Sybrandt so heartily on the back that the wind left him.

Sybrandt, wheezing, edged around the table but listened keenly. Here was news: Gerard had gone to Italy. Affecting doubt, he challenged it.

"My brother Gerard? Never."

"Liar!" roared Hans, his mood shifting with the drink. "He’s in Italy, ten times your brother! See here—read it yourself!" He flung the letter onto the table.

Sybrandt took it up, pretending to study it before admitting he could not read. But another drinker, proud of his rare skill, took it and read aloud:
