"Fine writing," the man observed, tracing the letters.

"Ay," Hans declared grandly, "and small wonder—’tis the hand of Margaret Van Eyck herself! A dear friend of mine, Margaret Van Eyck."

Sybrandt slipped away to find Cornelis.

The brothers put their heads together. Italy was far—could they keep him there?

"Keep him? Nothing would hold him long from his Margaret."

"Curse her!" Sybrandt spat. "Why didn’t she die when she had the chance?"

"She’d outlive the plague just to spite us."

Thus festering, their hearts conceived a plan: keep Gerard in Italy forever, and claim his share of their father’s wealth.

But execution required wit, and here they faltered—until, as if the Devil whispered in both ears at once, they cried as one:

"The burgomaster!"

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten received them at once. Suspense gnawed at him; certainty, even bitter, was better than doubt.

"You have news of Gerard?"

They told of the letter and Hans Memling. His eyes gleamed. "Who wrote it?"

"Margaret Van Eyck."

"Are you certain?" He fetched a sample of her writing. "Like this?"

"Ay, the same."

"Good. And what would you have of me?" His tone was careless, but his pulse raced.

They stammered, circling their intent before blurting it: a false letter, one to replace the true in Hans’s wallet, with words to keep Gerard in Italy.

Ghysbrecht’s mind warred—vengeance urged Gerard’s return to face the law; prudence whispered to keep him an ocean away. But darker still was his fear: Gerard knew a secret that could undo him.

"My duty is to aid citizens who cannot write," he said at last. "But the matter is yours, not mine. What shall I pen?"

"Tell him Margaret is dead."

"Dead? At her age?"

"She nearly was. It’s no outright lie."

"And you think this will keep him abroad?"

"Ay," Cornelis said bitterly. "Italy was his dream. He’d return for her, but not for us. He cares naught for his own kin."

Ghysbrecht’s lips curled. "A bitter draught for him."

"For his own good," Sybrandt said smoothly.

"A lie breaks no bones," Cornelis added with a smirk.

Sybrandt’s gaze flicked to Ghysbrecht’s broken nose. "Nor leaves marks like a cudgel."

The burgomaster’s face darkened. That venomous glance struck home. Hate flared.

"Very well. Dictate, and I shall write. But remember—the guilt lies not with the hand that writes, but the tongue that guides it."

The brothers assented, sneering inwardly. Ghysbrecht drew his inkwell close, studying Margaret Van Eyck’s script, when an interruption came—Jorian Ketel burst in, urgency in his face.

"I bring news—not for all ears."

Ghysbrecht drew him aside. A whispered exchange, then Jorian was sent to saddle his mule. The burgomaster turned back, suddenly cold.

"The peace of households is precious. This must not be rushed. We shall see."

"But the man will be gone!"

"Where is he?"

"At the inn, drinking."

"Keep him there, then."

And with that, he dismissed them, baffled.

Earlier that morning, Margaret Brandt had met Jorian near her door. He scowled at her, but she stopped him.

"You saved him once. Why have you stayed away? Did you not believe in the hundred crowns?"

Jorian, disarmed by her candor, confessed his earlier rebuff. She led him to the garden, showing where the parchments lay buried.

"None have touched them but you may take them now."

Jorian, exultant, raced to the Stadthouse.

Now, as Ghysbrecht rode toward Sevenbergen, Jorian strode beside him, boasting that within the hour the missing deeds would be in hand.

"Lucky it was no thief took them," Jorian mused.

"No thief? What then?"

"A jackdaw. Mischief, not malice. Like my old chough—stealing what he least needed and hiding it. This Gerard’s the same. The parchments are no use to him; he hid them out of whim."

Ghysbrecht’s tension eased. "Perhaps you’re right."

At Peter’s gate, hesitation struck him.

"I wish it were anywhere but here."

Jorian reassured him. "The girl is honest. She had no part in it."

In the garden, loose soil marked the spot. Jorian dug, uncovering a parchment. Ghysbrecht snatched it up, trembling, tossing out one after another—until his face fell.

"Where is the other? The one worth all these?"

Jorian, suspicious, accused him of cheating.

A calm voice cut through.

"There was another—not a town record, but a private deed between you and my grandfather."

"Where is it?"

"Gerard took it with him."

"Whither?"

She turned away. "Ask me no more. The sight of you is more than I can bear."

Ghysbrecht stood stricken—remorse, fear, greed warring within. Then Martin Wittenhaagen’s mocking laugh rang out.

"Ho! Arrest him, thief!" Ghysbrecht snarled.

Martin thrust the duke’s pardon under his nose.

"The duke sends a message," he said coolly. "He’ll not have burghers playing kings. Be humble—or he’ll hang you at your own door, as he did the last one."

The insult, the impotence, drove Ghysbrecht to fury. He spat on Martin’s coat.

Martin laughed. "A venomous old toad! Not worth my boot."

Ghysbrecht mounted his mule, shaking with rage. "I’ll make their hearts ache. All of them!"

Back in Tergou, the black sheep brooded over Hans’s wallet. If only they could swap the letter! But they could not write.

Then Dierich Brower summoned them to the burgomaster.

Ghysbrecht, pale and tense, had forged a letter. "Now, the superscription—what were the words?"

Sybrandt recalled: *"To Gerard Eliassoen, these by the hand of the trusty Hans Memling, with all speed."*

"Good. Now, the size? The fold?"

They described it.

"Go. Give him this in place of the true one."

But when they reached the inn, Hans was gone.

Dierich hurried them back.

Ghysbrecht’s eyes gleamed. "Is he mounted?"

"No."

"Then catch him. Must I spell it out? Must every move be made with wine and trickery?"

A dark glance passed between Ghysbrecht and Dierich. The brothers paled, their zeal evaporating.

But Ghysbrecht was not done.

"Return the true letter to me—by any means."

And so the trap was set.

Denys, flanked by his captors to prevent any desperate bid for freedom, was led away in a storm of grief and bitter remorse. Gerard had abandoned the safer German road to Rome for his sake—and what had it earned him? Abandoned, alone, in the heart of Burgundy. The thought gnawed at Denys like a fanged beast, driving him now to wild curses at the heavens, now to a brooding silence so dark and dangerous that his guards, eyeing him warily, stripped him of his weapons.

They rejoined their leader just beyond the town, and the company halted at the *Tête d'Or*. The young landlady, though preoccupied with the count and even more so with the bastard, caught sight of Denys and asked, with a flicker of concern, what had become of his young companion. The question tore open the wound. Denys, his voice breaking, told her everything—and begged her to send someone after Gerard.

"Now that he’s parted from me, perhaps he’ll listen to sense," Denys pleaded. "The lad was never one for solitude."

The landlady tossed her head, cheeks flushing. "I think I’ve been over-kind already," she snapped.

"You refuse?"

"Most certainly."

Then—he unleashed a torrent of curses so vile that she recoiled, stammering that she was not accustomed to such language, and hurried away, wiping at her eyes. Yet within the hour, despite her wounded pride, she had two mules saddled.

Denys, unaware of this, rode north with his captors, slumped in his saddle, his spirit as heavy as his chains. Behind him, the landlady—swift for once in her life—spurred southward with a servant at her heels.

At dusk, after a hard march, the bastard’s band reached *The White Hart*. Their arrival sent the inn into an uproar, and it was some time before Manon, now a woman of standing, recognized her old acquaintance amidst the throng. She said nothing, but her cheeks burned. The past was a thing to be buried, not revisited.

Denys scarcely noticed. The inn itself was a ghost of Gerard, and the weight of his loss pressed harder with each passing league.

Then, as night deepened, the wind shifted. Manon beckoned him from the doorway. He rose, sullen, his guards shadowing him.

"Nay," she whispered, "a word in private."

She drew him aside, and in a hushed voice asked if he would do her a kindness.

His answer was a bark of laughter. "No. I’ve no kindness left in me for man, woman, or beast. Not even for a dog—if I could help it."

She winced but pressed on. "Good archer, I showed you and your comrade mercy once. For his sake, then—grant me this."

Denys exhaled sharply. "For his sake, then. What is it?"

Her voice dropped lower. "You know my history. I was... unfortunate. Now I am respectable. But today, a woman threw *him* in my face. It will always be so—so long as he hangs there. I would have him taken down."

Denys snorted. "Not my trade."

Manon wrung her hands. "There is no one else I dare ask!"

He shrugged. "Then pay someone. Two angels would buy you a pair of soldiers willing to do the deed. They’re not particular."

The suggestion stung her pride, but necessity won. "Send them to me," she murmured. "But tell them it’s for a friend—not for me. I would die of shame if they thought otherwise. Times have changed."

Denys found two men eager for easy coin and sent them to her. Then he returned to his brooding, drowning in thoughts of Gerard.

Manon and the soldiers struck their bargain swiftly—save for the matter of payment. The men wanted it upfront. She balked. Did they doubt her word? Let them do the deed first—or let her watch from afar.

"*Me?*" Her horror was genuine. "I’d sooner die than see it done!"

"Yet you’d have it done."

"Aye," she admitted, "for my need is great. Times *have* changed."

An hour later, the grim relic of her past was gone—no longer a specter to haunt her newfound respectability. The soldiers returned, expecting whispers of divine wrath for their meddling with the dead. Instead, they found the marketplace seething with murmurs.

Conscience prickling, the bolder of the two edged forward and melted into the crowd. When he returned, his face was pale.

"Fool," he hissed to his companion, "it’s not vengeance they’re talking of—it’s a *miracle*."

The church wall bore letters of fire—a celestial demand questioning why no masses were said for the duke in these troubled times. Many witnessed the apparition, which lingered before fading, leaving the townsfolk gaping in awe. The town fathers, unwilling to be outdone in loyalty by heavenly messengers, promptly funded a flood of masses. Our old acquaintance, the cure, lined his pockets handsomely—for he had a fine appreciation of such windfalls. Were it not for this profitable miracle, I would not trouble you with so common a spectacle.

Denys pleaded for the return of his weapons. *"Must you shame him as well as break his heart?"* he argued. The answer came: *"Swear upon the cross of your sword never to abandon the bastard’s service until this rebellion is crushed."* Reluctantly, he yielded, loosing a volley of oaths before reclaiming his arms and his freedom.

The company halted at *The Three Fish*, where Marion, spotting Denys, exclaimed, *"Bad luck for me! Who’d have thought to see you again?"* Noting his wounded expression, she softened. *"What’s amiss?"* He told her of Gerard’s flight. Ever practical, Marion brightened. *"Gerard’s too handsome and well-mannered to come to harm. Women will see him safe. If fortune favors him, he’ll join some decent company bound for Rome. If not, he’ll turn home—perhaps even beat you there, given the fighting ahead in Flanders."* She then returned his gold, steadfastly refusing a single coin despite his insistence. Her honesty lifted his spirits somewhat, offering a glimmer of hope in a world where his friend now wandered alone.

Scattered bands of foot soldiers, some penniless, trudged toward Flanders, sustained by town bailiffs who ordered innkeepers to feed and lodge them. Anthony of Burgundy swept them up as he passed, swelling his ranks until he crossed the frontier at the head of six hundred men. There, his brother Baldwyn met him with reinforcements—arms, provisions, and fresh recruits. They marched in battle array through loyal towns, greeted not with resistance but cheers.

*"This rebellion’s a farce,"* grumbled Simon, a soldier who had latched onto Denys. Denys said nothing but silently vowed to Saint Mars that if this rising—which had cost him Gerard—proved hollow, he would put a bolt through Anthony of Burgundy himself.

That afternoon, they sighted a fortified town—rumored disloyal. Yet its gates stood open, the flanking towers manned by lone sentinels. The army relaxed, breaking formation as they neared. Then, a furlong away, the drawbridge rose with a groan, the portcullis crashed down, and the walls bristled with lances. A low, fierce murmur ran through the ranks.

*"Halt!"* roared Anthony.

A herald spurred forward, sounding his bugle. A tall figure in gleaming brass armor appeared above the gate. A brief, heated exchange—inaudible but unmistakable—ended with the herald sounding a defiant note before galloping back, face dark with war.

*"Pioneers, to the front!"* Anthony commanded.

Hedges fell, the army fanned out, and the siege began.

For Denys, the clang of the portcullis was a tonic. His eyes gleamed; his heart quickened. As the herald returned, battle in his gaze, Denys laughed aloud for the first time in days. *"Courage, all! The devil’s dead!"*

The besiegers encamped just beyond arrow range. Sappers, pioneers, and crossbowmen advanced, laboring under a hail of missiles to raise a stockade at the moat’s edge. Denys, behind his mantelet, traded shots with an English archer whose arrows thudded perilously close. *"You shoot well, friend!"* he called. *"Step out and take your praise!"*

Among the defenders, one figure stood out—a tall knight in brass armor, directing the resistance with a long lance. Arrows clattered harmlessly off his mail. He strode the battlements, impervious, as if the shafts were mere rain. The besiegers, irked, cursed his invulnerability.

Gunpowder would later rob war of its artistry. But here, in the age of visible valor, the siege unfolded like a grim ballet. Catapults hurled stones; trebuchets flung fire. A *cat*—a covered ramp—crept toward the wall, filling the moat with fascines until the knight, with a flaming tar-barrel, forced it back.

Then came the wooden tower—rolling forward on hidden wheels, a monstrous siege engine bristling with armored men. The knight fired the mine beneath it. The tower lurched, tilted, and collapsed, spilling soldiers like ants from a crushed hill.

Amid the chaos, Denys spotted the knight balancing on a beam, thrusting fire-bags into the wreckage with his lance. Seizing his chance, Denys darted from cover, loosed a bolt—and missed the knight but struck the pikeman steadying him. The knight toppled, armor flashing, and plunged into the moat.

A moment later, Denys hissed as an English arrow pinned his arm. He retreated, clutching the wound, just as the knight—miraculously alive—was dragged from the water and hauled before Anthony.

*"A rebel knight?"* Anthony sneered.

Anthony scoffed. *"Then you’ll die at a stake, riddled with arrows."*

The hosier stammered, *"H-hang your b-b-brother Baldwyn first."*

A letter changed everything—a royal summons for Anthony to escort an English princess to Burgundy. The hosier, sensing advantage, offered a bribe: *"A th-th-thousand angels to end this."*

By dinner, under a flag of truce, the two dined and struck a deal.

*"But why rebel?"* Anthony asked.

Denys, nursing his wound, received a purse from the Comte de Jarnac for his service.

*"Your grief has passed?"* the comte asked.

The end came swifter than he expected. Released from duty the next day, Denys set out, wounded but resolute, to find his friend.

A shadow fell upon Margaret Brandt. She moved through her days as if walking in a dream, her spirit weighed down by some invisible burden. Even the gentlest word from Peter now made her startle, her wide eyes fixing upon him with something like fear. She withdrew from the warmth of Margaret Van Eyck’s company, no longer seeking solace in the old woman’s affection. Where once she had leaned into the embrace of friendship, she now stood stiff and trembling, sometimes even flinching away.

Most strange of all, she refused to step beyond her door while the sun still hung in the sky. If she went to Tergou, it was under the cover of darkness, slipping back before dawn could betray her. She avoided matins, shunned the daylight, until at last even Peter—slow as he was to notice such things—pressed her for an answer.

“The people,” she murmured. “They look at me.”

Margaret Van Eyck, too, saw the change and questioned her gently. “What troubles you, child? Surely you need not fear to confide in me?”

But Margaret could only shake her head, tears spilling down her cheeks. “I have no sorrows but those of my own making. I am treated more kindly than I deserve—especially here.”

“Then why stay away from me, dear heart?”

“I come more often than I should,” she whispered, her voice breaking.

Margaret Van Eyck sighed, puzzled. “There now, Reicht calls for you. Go, child. But I will find the truth of this.”

Turning the matter over in her mind, the old woman concluded that Gerard’s family must be the cause—their scorn driving the girl into this sorrow. Resolved to confront them, she set out the next day, Reicht at her heels, and marched to the hosier’s house.

Catherine received her with stiff respect, thanking her through tears for her kindness to Gerard. But when the conversation turned to Margaret Brandt, the warmth vanished. Catherine’s lips thinned, her eyes hardened, and she stood as immovable as stone. Margaret Van Eyck argued, cajoled, but met only silence and resistance. At last, her patience frayed, she left in a flurry of indignation, her curtsey clipped and cold.

Outside, she found Reicht speaking with a pale girl on crutches—little Kate. The old woman would have swept past, pride still stinging, but then a small, trembling hand slipped into hers. She looked down into Kate’s eyes, brimming with silent pleading and gratitude. The anger melted from her heart. She bent and kissed the girl’s forehead.

“I see,” she murmured. “Very well. I leave it to you.”

Back at home, she mused aloud. “Today I saw two things—one common, one rare. A stubborn, foolish woman… and an angel walking the earth. If only I could paint that face.”

Kate did not disappoint. She waited for the right moment, then told her mother what Reicht had shared—how Margaret had shed her own blood for Gerard in the wood.

“See how she loves him, Mother.”

“Who would not love him?” Catherine muttered.

“Think of it, though. Poor thing.”

“Aye,” Catherine admitted grudgingly. “She has her own troubles, no doubt. I can’t bear the sight of blood, let alone my own.”

It was a small concession, but when Kate pressed further, she was swiftly silenced.

A month later, a soldier returning from Burgundy brought a letter to the hosier’s house. The family sat at supper, the father absent, when the man laid the folded parchment on the table and left without reward.

Though none could read, they knew at once it was Gerard’s hand.

“And your father must be away!” Catherine cried. “Shame on you all—not one who can read your own brother’s words!”

Yet there was something in the letter they *could* understand. Between the lines, Gerard had drawn two clasped hands—a silent farewell. The sight struck them like a blow. The women wept openly; even Giles murmured, “Poor Gerard,” his voice uncharacteristically soft. Cornelis and Sybrandt sat in uneasy silence.

But how to hear the words? They did not wish to bare their ignorance or their grief before a stranger.

“Dame Van Eyck?” Kate suggested timidly.

“Aye, I’ll go to her,” Catherine decided. “She loves Gerard too. She’ll not refuse me.”

She found Margaret Van Eyck at prayer, waiting in a room where a young woman sat pensively by the stove. The stranger rose politely, and Catherine, warming her hands, studied her discreetly. The girl’s gown was trimmed with fur—a mark of wealth—but her face was gentle, unassuming.

“What brings me here, my dear? A letter—from my poor boy, far away in some wild place. And none of us can read it. Can you?”

“Yes.”

“Ah! That’s to your credit. She won’t be long, but every minute weighs on a mother’s heart.”

“I will read it to you.”

“Bless you, child.”

Catherine did not notice the tremor in the hand that took the letter, nor the way the girl’s breath caught.

“The first words are: *‘To my honoured parents.’*”

“Aye, he always did honour us,” Catherine whispered.

*“‘God and the saints have you in His holy keeping, and bless you by night and by day. Your one harsh deed is forgotten; your years of love remembered.’*”

Catherine pressed a hand to her heart, sinking back with a sob.

“Then comes this,” the girl went on softly. *“‘A long farewell.’*”

“Go on, dear. Even sorrow is comfort now.”

*“‘To my brothers Cornelis and Sybrandt—Be content; you will see me no more!’*”

“What does that mean? Ah!”

*“‘To my sister Kate. Little angel of my father’s house. Be kind to her—’*”

“That’s Margaret Brandt, his sweetheart,” Catherine murmured. “I’ve not been kind to her. Forgive me, Gerard.”

*“‘—for poor Gerard’s sake: since grief to her is death to me—’*”

The girl’s voice broke. A gasp, a shudder—then she slid from the chair, insensible, the letter slipping from her fingers as her head came to rest upon Catherine’s knees.

Catherine knew better than to panic when a woman fainted. She had seen it enough times to recognize the ordinary causes—tight lacing, stifling air, or a sudden shock. Without hesitation, she laid Margaret flat, loosened her gown, and flung open the window to let in the cool evening breeze. Only when she had done all this did she call out, firm and clear, “Come here, if you please.”

Margaret Van Eyck and Reicht hurried in, finding the girl pale and motionless, Catherine briskly chafing her hands.

“What have you done to her?” Margaret Van Eyck demanded, her voice sharp with accusation.

“Me?” Catherine shot back, indignant. “I’ve done nothing but what any woman would. This is no mystery—just nature taking its course.”

The elder Margaret’s cheeks flushed with anger. “You speak too lightly,” she said, “when you and yours are the reason she’s in this state.”

Catherine’s eyes narrowed. “That’s a lie, and you know it. No woman alive is to blame for this.”

“Then who is? You and your husband drove them apart! Now he’s gone to Italy, and her heart’s broken. ‘Nature,’ you call it?”

Catherine stiffened. “What are you saying? You can’t mean this is my Gerard’s lass. Look at her—that fur alone costs more than a craftsman earns in months. And if she isn’t a wife, she ought to be.”

Margaret Van Eyck’s voice dropped, heavy with meaning. “And who stopped her from being one? Who stood in their way at the very altar?”

Catherine crossed herself. “God forgive them, whoever it was. But it wasn’t me, nor my man.”

The old woman softened slightly. “Now that you’ve seen her, perhaps you’ll think better of her. She’s coming around, thank Heaven.”

“Think better of her?” Catherine scoffed. “That’s long past. Poor child—trouble behind her and trouble ahead.” She shook her head. “And to think I made her read Gerard’s letter to me. No wonder she fainted.”

As color slowly returned to the girl’s cheeks, Catherine drew Margaret Van Eyck aside. “Does she lodge with you?”

“No.”

“Then don’t let her go back to Sevenbergen tonight.”

“That’s her choice.”

“You’re the elder—make her stay.” Catherine leaned in, lips nearly touching the other woman’s ear, and in the space of a breath, conveyed what no man would have dared speak aloud.

Margaret Van Eyck recoiled. “That’s a vile slander! Look at her face—how could you even think it?”

Catherine snorted. “I know what I know. And I’m telling you—it’s so.”

Before the old woman could argue further, Catherine turned back to the girl, took her face in her hands, and kissed her fiercely. “You suffered for Gerard,” she murmured. “And I’ve been a fool to hold myself apart. From now on, you’re my daughter.”

Another embrace, and then she was gone, leaving silence in her wake.

Margaret—pale, trembling—managed a faint smile. Gerard’s mother had kissed her. Called her daughter.

But the joy was short-lived. Margaret Van Eyck’s expression darkened. “I don’t know how you could let that woman touch you.”

“His mother,” Margaret whispered, as if that explained everything.

“Mother or not, you wouldn’t let her near you if you knew what she said.”

Margaret’s breath caught. “About me?”

Before the old woman could elaborate, Margaret slid from the chair to her knees, clutching at her skirts. “Forgive me,” she pleaded, voice breaking. “Forgive me!”

And in that moment, it was clear—whatever secret lay between them, the weight of it was too much to bear.

The family sat gathered around the table in the hosier’s house, the air thick with unease, when their mother returned. Her sharp eyes swept over them, lingering longest on Cornelis and Sybrandt, before she placed Gerard’s letter flat upon the wood. Though she could not read, she traced the lines with her finger, reciting each word from memory, as if to prove—if only to herself—that she knew its contents well.

Then she lifted her head, and her gaze hardened. The storm that had simmered within her now broke.

Catherine’s voice was a torrent, swift and relentless, a flood of anger and grief that no man could hope to match. Her words lashed like a whip, each syllable striking with precision.

*"I’ve long suspected you of poisoning your father against Gerard, of whispering in Ghysbrecht’s ear like serpents. And now—here it is in his own hand! You’ve driven your own brother from his home, sent him into exile, and torn the heart from my breast. Do you think I’ve forgotten what you were as boys? The rest were a comfort—but you? Mischief trailed behind you like shadows. A woman’s labor could scarce keep your clothes whole, for where honest work wears cloth, wicked play slashes it to ribbons!"*

Her breath came sharp, her chest heaving.

*"And now—where does this sudden wealth come from? Since that night you slunk home late, Cornelis, your pockets heavy, your head swollen with secrets. Sloth and greed make poor bedfellows. If you didn’t earn it, then you stole it—and from whom? Some poor soul weaker than you, no doubt, for a man would have broken you with his bare hands!"*

She scoffed, shaking her head.

*"But your coin has shown me one truth—how you’d squander our name if it ever fell to you. I’ve watched you these weeks, spending like lords while I scrape and save to keep you fed. No more. This house, this trade—they will never be yours. Gerard is our heir. My Gerard, whom you cast out and left for dead!"*

Her voice cracked, but her resolve did not waver.

*"You thought to cut him from our lives, but you failed. You severed the stalk, yet left the seed. Margaret Brandt carries his child—my grandchild—and I pray it is a son. He will be our heir. He will outlive you both, for a rotten heart turns the body to dust before its time."*

She turned to Kate, her tone softening only slightly.

*"Fetch Gerard’s things from the drawer. We’ll take them to Sevenbergen soon—Peter Buyskens’ cart will serve. Margaret is his wife, no matter what lies Ghysbrecht spins. She is my daughter now, as much as you are, Kate."*

Then her gaze returned to Cornelis and Sybrandt, cold as winter.

The brothers shrank beneath her words, their faces dark with shame and resentment. They slunk away, avoiding her stare, and for many days after, she spoke not a word to them—neither kindness nor rebuke.

*Liberaverat animum suum.* She had freed her soul.

Here is the rewritten chapter in a more engaging and fluid style while preserving the original content and tone:

---

Catherine was a practical housewife who rarely left home, for whenever she did, something always went amiss. She was acutely aware of this, so she waited for a lull in household duties before making her trip to Sevenbergen—only to find Peter Buyskens’ mule unavailable on the one afternoon she could have gone.

At last, Eli confronted her in front of the family. "Is it true you meant to visit Margaret Brandt?"

"Aye, husband."

"Then I forbid it."

"Oh, do you?"

"I do."

"Well, then I suppose that settles it," she said, flushing.

"Not another word," Eli replied sternly.

Alone with Kate later, Catherine turned her frustration inward.

"I should’ve gone like a cat after a robin—quick and quiet. But no, I must cluck about it like a foolish hen and bring the whole house down on my plans. Next time we mean to do anything the least bit questionable, let’s do it then and there, without a word. Then, if they scold us, we can plead ignorance—‘Oh, we never thought! Who would’ve guessed?’ For two pins, I’d march straight there now, defiance in my teeth!"

Kate was taken aback by such boldness. "Nay, mother, patience. Father will come around."

"Aye, and so will Michaelmas—but when? I wanted you to see the girl for yourself. Then we could have judged her fairly. No matter what they say, you must lay eyes on a creature to know its worth. If I had fifty sons, I’d never thwart one’s fancy without seeing the lass first. But there—life’s a school, and the lesson’s never done. We mend one fault only to take up another, blundering on till we blunder into our graves."

Kate hesitated. "Mother..."

"Well? Out with it. That look bodes no good news."

"I have a confession," she murmured.

Catherine’s face lit with pride. "A fine word! Our Gerard taught you that, I’ll warrant. Speak, then."

"The truth is... I’ve seen her."

"And?"

"Spoken with her, too."

"And never told me? Wonders never cease!"

"You were so set against her. I waited for a time when it wouldn’t anger you further."

Catherine sighed, half bitter, half sad. "Like mother, like daughter. Cowardice is our curse. The others I scold, else the house would fall apart—but you, Kate? Have I ever been harsh with you?" She softened. "No tears, child. Likely you had your reasons. Now tell me everything."

Kate obliged. She explained how she and Giles had gone to the Stadthouse tower at night to comfort Gerard—only to find a stranger already there.

"At first we frightened each other half to death—what with the tower’s ill name and our own superstitions. But then I said, ‘You’re Margaret,’ and she answered, ‘And you’re Kate.’ Imagine!"

Catherine clapped her hands. "Of course! Gerard must have spoken of you to her, and her to you."

Kate responded with a kiss—a single gesture that conveyed understanding, admiration, and reconciliation all at once.

"Now speak plainly," Catherine urged. "Is she comely?"

"Very, and little different from her portrait."

"Pah! Looks are nothing. Does she love my boy as he deserves?"

"Sevenbergen is farther from the Stadthouse than we are, yet she got there before me. More than that—she had already freed him by the time I arrived."

Catherine scoffed. "The highest tower for miles? Impossible."

"But true. She and an old man sent up a rope. When I first saw it dangling, I thought it witchcraft. But when she embraced me—her heart pounding, her cheeks wet—I knew it was no spell. It was love. She’s strong, not frail like me, but even I feel sometimes as if I could move the world for those I love. And she loves Gerard."

"God bless her for it!"

"But—"

"But what, child?"

"Is her love truly honest? Strange as it sounds, the very thing that warms your heart chills mine. She’s no wife, when all’s said and done."

"They stood at the altar."

"Aye, but left as they came—maid and bachelor."

"The priest—does he say so?"

"I don’t know."

"Then I’ll take no man’s word but his in such a tangle. Still, you’re right. I’ll go to Sevenbergen alone. I’m a wife, not a slave. We’re in the dark here, and she holds the key. I must question her without ears about—least of all yours. I’ll bring no lily with a blight into this house, not even for a palace of gold."

---

The rewritten version tightens the dialogue, enhances emotional depth, and improves narrative flow while staying faithful to the original. Let me know if you'd like any refinements!

Denys waited until the next day to speak privately with Catherine and Kate, carefully omitting any mention of Cornelis and Sybrandt. “It’s not my place to darken their names,” he said. “They come from good stock. But Gerard sees them as no friends in this matter—and I’m Gerard’s comrade. A soldier keeps his secrets from the enemy—save for lies.”

Catherine sighed but held her tongue. His tale of Gerard’s trials wrung their hearts, and tears fell freely at the parting of the two friends—a moment Denys himself could not recount without a tremor in his voice. Yet grief soon gave way to hope, for surely Gerard would return to them soon.

Still, Denys insisted, whether Gerard came back tomorrow or next year, his last wishes must be honored. “If he rides home to find no Margaret, where would I stand then?”

Catherine, her heart now softened toward the girl, was as eager as Denys to find her before Gerard’s return. But she disagreed with his plan to scour the countryside. “She must have told someone where she was bound. They’re not fugitives—they owe no man a penny in Sevenbergen. And, Denys, you can’t hunt all Holland for her.”

“Can’t I?” he muttered. “We’ll see.” After a pause, he proposed dividing their efforts: she would stay, ears open for any whisper, while he combed every town if need be. “Three don’t flee as fast or as far as one,” he reasoned.

“That’s sense,” Catherine conceded. Yet she urged him first to seek the demoiselle Van Eyck. “She and Margaret were close as sisters. If anyone knows where the lass has gone, ’tis she—if she’ll tell.”

Denys would have gone that instant, but Catherine tidied herself swiftly, and together they sought the painter’s house.

Margaret Van Eyck received them with grace, seated amidst the rich furnishings of her chamber. But at Catherine’s news, the tapestry slipped from her fingers.

“Gone? Without a word to me? The thankless child!”

This reaction startled them. “You didn’t know? When did you last see her?”

“Ten days past, perhaps. I had taken up my brushes again—after years—to paint her portrait. But I did not.” She hesitated, then confessed, “When I learned her condition, I set them down. I could not make her the Virgin’s likeness—not at my age.”

Catherine frowned. “But dame, surely you see how this would wound her? She loved you dearly.”

“Perhaps. Yet I could not help it.” The old woman sighed. “I am not some man who can paint saints and sinners from the same model. My art is half craft, half devotion.”

Then Reicht Heynes, the Frisian maid, spoke bluntly from the doorway. “You drove her off, mistress—with your cold looks and sharp words. And not just you. Gerard’s folk have slighted her sorely. Who would stay where she’s unwelcome?”

Catherine flushed. “I meant to visit—”

“Aye, ‘meant,’” Reicht scoffed. “Words lift the heart, but deeds dash it down again. She sat waiting, hoping, while you stayed away.”

The Van Eyck, stung, dismissed her on the spot. Reicht merely shrugged. “Turn me out if you like. But I’ll not weep till you’re dead and buried.”

Then Denys, with sudden gravity, turned to Reicht. “Since you’re no longer a servant, be a queen instead.”

The women stared.

“Marry me,” he said. “I’ve twenty gold pieces, a strong arm, and admiration for a lass who speaks her mind.”

Reicht eyed him sidelong. “I’ll look a man over well before I leap.”

“Then look your fill,” said Denys, squaring himself proudly.

At this, the Van Eyck cried out, seized by artistic frenzy. “Stay! You are beautiful—in your folly. I must capture you!” She snatched up her pencils, commanding Reicht to hold her place before him.

Catherine gaped, convinced the woman had gone mad. Reicht, cross at neglecting her boiling pot, muttered until her mistress snapped, “Go, then! Bury yourself in your work—you’ve no soul beyond it.”

Reicht departed with a final jab. “When you’re tired of life, mistress, die. But take your time. And soldier, if you’re still of the same mind then, speak up. If not—well, ’tis all one to Reicht Heynes.”

Her words struck home. Catherine and Denys left, both certain now that Margaret had fled heartbroken.

That afternoon, Denys set out on his quest—a needle in Holland’s haystack. He would comb every town, asking after an old physician, his fair daughter, and a bowman. He would question burgomasters, watch the wells where women gathered.

For months he wandered, a pilgrim of friendship, sober and steadfast where once he might have been reckless. He flirted lightly, wasted no time, and never again declared marriage “no habit of his.”

But Margaret Brandt remained as elusive as a shadow.

Catherine’s heart grew heavy when she realized Gerard would soon return—within a month, perhaps even a week—and she would have to face him with the bitter truth: she had failed to watch over his betrothed. Worse still was the gnawing uncertainty of Margaret’s fate. At times, the dread took a darker shape.

“Oh, Kate,” she moaned, clutching her daughter’s arm, “what if the poor lass has thrown herself into the river?”

“Mother, surely she would never be so desperate!”

“Child, you don’t know the reckless hearts of young women left alone in the world. One day they’ll drown themselves for love of some fool who’d be forgotten in a week if only another man crossed their path. I’ve known them leap into the water bold as brass, only to scream for rescue the next instant—so fickle even in their despair! And then there are times when their bodies ache in ways no living soul should suffer, twisting their minds until patience snaps like a frayed thread. Then—into the depths they go, and men are to blame for it.”

“Mother!”

“Aye, monsters, the lot of them! Digging canals at every turn, tempting poor women to their doom. They know we won’t slit our own throats—too squeamish for blood, valuing our skins more than our lives. And hanging? By the time a lass has tied the noose, she’s had time to think twice. But a canal—ah, that’s swift as slipping into bed. The water does the rest, willing or not.” She shuddered. “Why, even I once stood at the edge, ready to jump.”

Kate gasped. “You?”

“Aye. Early in my marriage, when your father and I first learned each other’s rough edges. We quarreled, and I stormed out, weeping, wanting only to spite him. That cursed canal glittered at me, whispering how sweet it would be to make him grieve. I nearly stepped in—then I remembered my new gown, half-sewn, and my fine shoes, just bought from old John Bush. So I turned back instead. And what did your father say? *‘Let the quarrel lie, my lass. Not a word I spoke in anger was true, save this: I love thee.’*” Her voice softened. “I threw my arms around him then, thinking of the water I’d nearly chosen over his embrace. And that was better—far better—than rotting at the bottom of some ditch, my wedding clothes ruined. But my grief was nothing to Margaret’s.”

Kate offered a frail hope: perhaps Margaret loved her father too dearly to abandon him in his old age.

“Nay,” Catherine sighed. “Despair blots out all else—even love. The sky itself turns black. No, I put my faith not in the old man, but in the child she carries. A babe tugs at a mother’s heart, pleading for life even as it feeds on her very blood. That may stay her hand.”

“Then keep hope, Mother,” Kate urged. “Perhaps our fears are for nothing.” She hesitated before adding, “And for mercy’s sake, stop saying the babe will be a boy. It’s ill luck to name the child’s sex before birth—as if girls weren’t born as often as boys!”

Catherine rounded on her, indignant. “Threaten me with a girl, would you? What use is another lass when I have you? A boy—ah, a boy I could dandle on my knee and see my Gerard’s face again! It’s settled in my mind: she carries a son. And if I’m wrong, let disappointment come then—not from your tongue stealing my joy beforehand!”

Margaret Brandt had always been an outsider in Sevenbergen. Her quiet reserve was mistaken for pride, and the whispers of envy had reached her ears. Now, as her secret grew harder to conceal, she imagined the cruel triumph of those who had long resented her. The thought gnawed at her, driving her deeper into solitude, away from prying eyes.

She knew the truth of her own heart—knew that in the eyes of God and the law, her betrothal to Gerard was as binding as marriage. Yet the written proof was gone, carried away in his hands across distant lands. Without it, what defense had she against the world’s judgment? She was no lawyer, but she understood the cruel arithmetic of reputation: a missing contract could not outweigh the visible truth of her condition.

Shame burned in her like a fever. Every glance, every murmur, seemed to strip her bare. Even her father’s innocent words could send the blood rushing to her cheeks—not for anything he said, but for the secret she carried. And when the Van Eyck women discovered her state, the fragile illusion of safety shattered. Catherine’s kindness offered no comfort; silence meant betrayal was coming.

The desire to flee became unbearable. To vanish, to escape the condemning eyes—but how? Her father was old, rooted in his ways. She could not abandon him, yet how could she confess the truth? The weight of it choked her.

One evening, she stood by the canal, its dark waters whispering of peace. An end to fear, to shame—but no. Gerard’s love anchored her. She knelt, praying for strength, then rose with desperate resolve. *I will tell him. Before sunset.*

That night, she stood before her father, trembling.

"Father, I must speak to you."

"Speak, child."

Her voice wavered. "I have done foolishly. Disgrace is coming to this house—and I am the cause."

Peter frowned. "What folly is this?"

She clenched her hands. "Gerard took our betrothal contract with him. Without it—" She swallowed. "The world will not believe me. They will see only my shame."

His brow furrowed. "Gerard is true. He will return."

"But before then—" Her breath hitched. "Father, I cannot stay here. The whispers, the scorn—I will not bear it."

For once, the old man did not press for answers. He saw her anguish and, with a father’s instinct, chose mercy over curiosity.

"If Sevenbergen wounds you," he said quietly, "then we shall leave it."

She stared at him. "You would go—without demanding why?"

"What use are reasons when your heart is breaking?" He touched her hand. "Where you go, I go. That is enough."

Relief washed over her, so sharp it brought tears. She wept against his shoulder, spared the humiliation of confession—yet guilt lingered. She had bought her escape with silence.

A week later, they were gone, their home left behind like a shed skin. Only the Burgomaster of Tergou knew their destination—Rotterdam, a tailor’s shop in the Brede-Kirk Straet. A secret kept close, for now.

But secrets, like shadows, have a way of stretching long in the light.

Margaret Brandt found a measure of peace among strangers. Yet her trials were far from over. When a prominent townsman fell ill—a man as fanciful as he was wealthy—he exhausted every physician in Rotterdam before sending for Peter. The old man arrived to find the patient bled and purged to the brink of death. With a scoff, Peter hurled the apothecary’s bottles out the window, flung open the casement, and set to work with simpler, kinder remedies: yolks beaten in good Schiedam, meat stewed in wine and water with mild herbs. The man recovered, weak as a wrung cloth, but alive—and voluble. He sang Peter’s praises through every street, and the old physician, long overlooked, found himself suddenly famous.

For a time, fortune smiled. Peter’s pride swelled; his purse grew heavier. Yet he poured much of his earnings back into rare herbs and costly drugs—until Margaret, with a mother’s foresight, wrested a portion from his grasp. But the triumph came too late. The excitement proved fatal.

One evening, his farewell to her slurred oddly. By morning, he lay senseless, his speech stolen. Margaret, her father’s pupil for years, recognized the stroke at once. Still, she fetched another physician—one whom Peter had once thwarted—who confirmed her fears and eagerly reached for his lancet. She refused. "He despised bloodletting," she said, "especially for the aged." Peter lived, but his mind was no longer his own. Memory frayed; his prescriptions became unreliable. Patients still came, a few stubbornly waiting for his return. But Margaret saw the truth: ruin loomed.

Pride and poverty had kept her from her cousin, William Johnson. Now, desperate, she sought him—only to find him deep in drink, his servant lolling familiarly beside him. When Margaret hinted at private family matters, the woman bristled.

Margaret flushed. "I beg your pardon. I did not know my cousin had adopted the customs of this town. Another time, then." She turned to leave.

*"What d’you mean by ‘customs’?"* the woman snapped. *"A faithful servant keeps her master safe from beggarly kin!"*

Margaret whirled. "Modesty becomes you—for you are no servant. Your tongue betrays you. The ape only shows her tail once she’s climbed the tree. No, *there* sits the servant—God help him! And while that’s so, fear not—his kin will never be so poor in spirit as to seek charity where the likes of *you* may sneer at it." With one burning glance at her silent, shamefaced cousin, she left.

Tears waited until she was home. Now she faced the stark truth: two helpless men to feed, a child on the way, and only five gold pieces left. Yet that unborn child, helpless as it seemed, became her salvation.

Nature, cunning in all creatures preparing for their young, sharpened her wits. She brooded, schemed—then acted. She moved Peter to another room, scrubbed her own chamber spotless, polished the glass globe, hung it from the ceiling, dusted the crocodile, and nailed it outside. Then she schooled Martin in his new role: loitering sentinel, murmuring to passersby of the great alchemist within—a sage who, in rare moments of leisure, deigned to cure mortal ills.

Patients came. Margaret received them, barred from the "learned master" (lost in search of the grand elixir), and bade them return in two hours with symptoms. For the worshipful, she mimicked a physician’s scrutiny—pulse, skin, a grave frown—then vanished to "consult the sacred texts," emerging with illegible scrawls and mysterious vials. If she had the ingredients, she’d compound them before wide eyes, lips moving over the "ancient script."

*Populus vult decipi.* The people wished to be fooled. And fooled they were—relieved of coin, stuffed with faith and physic, and sent away satisfied.

Each evening, she knelt, begging God’s forgiveness *"for the sake of my child."* Yet as cures mounted, her conscience calloused. Martin, stiffening with age but strong as an ox, proved a willing accomplice—until drink loosened his tongue. Boasting of how *"me and my General bite the burghers,"* he drew the law’s eye.

Constables came. Margaret, trembling, was hauled before the burgomaster, flanked by scowling physicians in square caps, accusing her of unlawful practice. At first, fear choked her. Then, rallying, she spoke plain: she had been her father’s pupil, followed his methods, and *healed* where they had failed.

*"It is not for myself, sirs, but for two helpless men who depend on me. Forbid me this, and how shall I feed them? You are fathers—would you have me choose between starvation and shame?"*

The appeal faltered as her own words struck her. She hid her face, tears slipping between her fingers. The burgomaster remained unmoved.

She vowed, weeping, never to meddle again. Then came the worst blow: to pay the fine, she must break open Gerard’s *"To-morrow box,"* where she’d hidden their savings. The nails resisted. The constables grew impatient.

*"No!"* She seized a hammer, struck weakly, then with a cry, shattered it. The fine took all her gains and two gold pieces more. When they left, she slumped over the wreckage, loose hair veiling her as she sobbed, *"My love’s box is broken—and my heart with it."*

Martin found her thus. Furious, he vowed vengeance on whatever gossip had betrayed her. Margaret groaned. *"When you’ve slain every tongue in Rotterdam, shall we feast on corpses? Find me a way to feed us, or be still!"*

By evening, she rose, washed her face, and set Martin to work. The crocodile came down; a laundry basket took its place.

Even as they spoke, a patient arrived. Margaret refused medicine but offered washing. The man stormed off. *"Stupid creature,"* she sighed. Then a woman came—for a sick child. Again, Margaret proposed laundry.

So *"Doctor Margaret"* became a washerwoman. And though Martin balked at suds, she drilled him: *"A mule could wash with his hooves if driven to it!"* Laughing, he rolled up his sleeves. They worked fiercely, keeping the wolf from the door—but no more. The mended *"To-morrow box"* stood empty.

Then fate twisted anew. Among her clients was the mayor. Returning his linen, she heard his daughter was deathly ill. By careful questioning, she pieced together the symptoms: listlessness, no appetite, a shunning of all company. The girl was fading like a plucked flower.

Margaret waylaid the mayor. *"I was your unlicensed doctor. Now I’m your washerwoman. Yet let me see your daughter—not for pay, but because no healer in Rotterdam has helped her."*

Reluctantly, he agreed.

The girl, pale and sullen, turned from her. Margaret sat beside her, gentle but probing.

*"I didn’t—till you told me."* She spun a tale of two star-crossed lovers—her own story, thinly veiled. The girl, despite herself, was drawn in.

The girl did, and wept. *"You’ve done me good. Come again."*

Margaret returned, watching what stirred her. Only one topic sparked life: love. She confronted the mayor.

At her urging, the mayor paid his household in his daughter’s presence. Margaret watched, fingers on the girl’s wrist. When Ulrich, the dark-haired clerk, entered, the pulse leapt.

*"Him,"* she told the mayor. *"And he loves her in turn."*

The dignitary balked. *"A street brat I took in!"*

At last, he yielded. Margaret returned to the girl with a smile.

The girl flushed head to toe.

*"Cruel!"* the girl whispered.

Margaret grinned. *"Your father says if he loves your hair so, he may as well love the rest. So—will you wed Ulrich, or waste to your grave?"*

And the marble girl melted.

Theirs was a tale both sweet and sorrowful—Denys and Margaret had much to share. That day was a mingling of joy and grief for her, for though she had gained a steadfast friend, she also learned bitter tidings: Gerard was alone in a distant land. Denys, ever hopeful, insisted he would return, but Margaret knew better. If he had meant to come back, he would have done so by now.

Yet Denys was a balm to her spirit. He called her his *she-comrade*, lifting her heart with his jests and boisterous good humor. In turn, she mothered him, scolding him for his rough speech, forbidding him to eat elsewhere, and playfully ordering him about as she did Martin.

"Such foul words from so fair a face!" she chided. "You spoil yourself, Denys. Hold your tongue, or I shall be wroth with you—mind your manners!"

And so Denys, caught between his natural courtesy and his soldier’s roughness, made a comical effort to obey.

But fate, as ever, was relentless. Others now sought Margaret’s friendship. She had written to Margaret Van Eyck, humbly acknowledging that she was no longer the favorite she had once been, yet unable to forget the kindness shown to her in darker days. She spoke of her struggles, her resilience, and ended with a plea: *Do not reveal where I dwell—least of all to his family. I cannot forgive them. They drove him from me, and even after he was gone, their hearts never softened toward me.*

The Van Eyck, troubled, confided in Reicht, who, like a hollow reed, passed the secret to Catherine.

"So," Catherine murmured, "she blames *us* now? She stole our son, and now despises us for not embracing her after. Yet it is a mercy she lives—and so near, in Rotterdam."

Meanwhile, the English princess, now Countess Charolois, journeyed through the northern provinces in regal splendor, her train glittering with knights, ladies, minstrels, and players. Richart summoned his kin to Rotterdam to witness the spectacle.

It was there that Denys, by chance, met Catherine in the street. Their reunion was warm, and he crowed in triumph—*I have found our she-comrade!* But Catherine only scoffed.

"You’d have found her sooner had you stayed in Tergou instead of wandering Holland like a vagabond. And what good does it do? She wants naught to do with us now."

Denys scoffed in turn. "Bah! Come with me to her house."

But Catherine refused. "I’ll not go where I’m unwelcome. Unbidden guests sit ill at table."

Instead, she bade him act as mediator. Confident, he agreed—only to be swiftly humbled.

"She-comrade," he announced later, "I met a friend of yours today."

Margaret, unimpressed, replied, "You looked into the Rotter and saw yourself."

"Nay, a woman—one who seeks your goodwill. Catherine, Gerard’s mother."

Margaret’s face hardened. "Tell her she comes too late. Once I ached for her kindness, but she denied me when I needed her most. Let things stay as they are."

Denys argued, cajoled, even scolded—until Margaret’s composure shattered into tears. Alarmed, he swore never to raise the matter again.

Defeated, he returned to Catherine, who erupted in fury. The family’s resentment grew, stoking a feud that burned hotter with each passing day—save for little Kate, who alone spoke gently of Margaret.

Then came another visitor.

Margaret was overseeing her unlikely household—Martin elbow-deep in soap, Denys fumbling with an iron—when the door opened. There stood an old man, wizened yet sharp-eyed.

Ghysbrecht.

Margaret recoiled. "*Him!*"

But he raised a hand. "I come in peace. I bear a letter—from across the sea."

Her breath caught. A letter—*Gerard’s* hand.

She snatched it, trembling. "Oh, bless you for this! I forgive you every wrong!"

Clutching it to her heart, she fled to solitude, leaving Ghysbrecht to depart unnoticed—but not before casting a dark glance at Martha.

Hours passed. Margaret remained alone, lost in the words of her lost love.

When Margaret descended the stairs, she was transformed. Though her eyes still glistened with tears, a quiet resolve had settled over her. The storm of bitterness had passed, leaving behind a woman determined in her purpose.

“Denys,” she said softly, “I have my orders. I am to read my love’s letter to his family.”

Denys blinked in surprise. “You’d truly do that?”

“Without hesitation.”

“Has the letter softened you toward them, then?”

“Not in the least,” she replied firmly. “But if I despised them with every fiber of my being, I would still obey Gerard’s wish. There is sweetness in obedience, Denys—especially when it is given freely to one who is far away, who cannot command but must trust love alone to ensure it. Ah, Gerard, my heart, if you were here, I might have resisted, for your family and I have no kindness between us. But now? If you bade me stand before a raging sea and read your words to the sharks, I would do it without fear. So tell his mother this: I have a letter. If she and hers wish to hear it, they need only name the hour. I will receive them as best I can.”

Denys carried the message to Catherine, finding the family gathered at dinner. The announcement of Gerard’s letter brought instant joy—until he added Margaret’s condition.

“What?” Catherine scoffed. “Does she think none of us can read but her? Let her send the letter, and we’ll manage well enough.”

Little Kate, ever gentle, ventured, “Perhaps she cannot bear to part with it. She loves him dearly.”

Cornelis sneered. “Or she seeks to worm her way into the family by this means.”

Denys shot him a look of contempt. “That’s a foul thought. The girl hates you all like poison—make no mistake. Yet for Gerard’s sake, she offers to read his letter to you herself. It’s a kindness, whether you see it or not.”

Eli, ever the voice of reason, silenced the squabble. “Enough. Tell her this: I, his father, set aside all past grudges and thank her for the offer. But if she would double my thanks, let her entrust the letter to you, Denys. I will read it to my blood and return it faithfully—on my honor as a free burgher of Tergou.”

Denys chuckled. “A man of sense! She’ll be glad to obey Gerard without facing you all—for she’d sooner sup with serpents than break bread here, no offense.”

But when he returned to Margaret, she refused. “Not to you, not to them, not to anyone living.”

Denys groaned. “What of the obedience you spoke of?”

She arched a brow. “Obedience to Gerard is sweet. Obedience to anyone else? Who ever claimed that was sweet?”

At last, she relented enough to give him a scrap of paper—a copy of Gerard’s own words, addressed to Kate.

*"If these lines reach you, test my family’s hearts. If they are softened toward me, let them hear this letter—but never let it leave your hand. Should they still turn from you, say nothing. Let them think me dead. Disobey me in this, and you prove your love false."*

The room fell silent as the words were read aloud.

Catherine pursed her lips. “Well. That changes things. Still, she should come to us, not we to her.”

Eli sighed. “Tell her we are too many to crowd into her home. Bid her come here—the sooner, the better.”

When Denys left, Eli muttered, “A bitter draught, this. To welcome under my roof the girl who has brought us such grief… But what choice have we? We must hear Gerard’s words.”

Catherine patted his arm. “Don’t fret. She won’t come.”

Yet soon enough, Denys returned with the answer: *"She will be here at three by the clock."*

The family exchanged glances, none speaking. The reckoning was at hand.

The tension in the room was thick enough to cut with a knife. Catherine, her voice strained with weariness, finally broke the silence.

"Richart, for Heaven’s sake, don’t let this wretched girl turn us all against one another. Hasn’t she caused enough trouble already?"

Richart exhaled sharply. "Aye, she has. But fear not, Mother. Let her come, read the letter from the boy she’s ensnared with her devilish charms, and then be gone. You’ve all promised civility—no more, no less. And I’ll hold my tongue."

And so they waited, a household divided.

Margaret, for her part, arrived with her own bitterness simmering beneath a carefully constructed mask of dignity. When Denys had told her they refused to come to her—that she must go to them—her lips curled in disdain.

"Of course," she muttered. "Even in this, their pride outweighs their love for Gerard. But I’ll go—for his sake."

She dressed as if for battle, each garment chosen to defy their judgment. A dark blue kirtle, roan hose, and a fitted hood of the same hue, hemmed with silver—plain yet elegant, a quiet rebuke to their assumptions. Beauty, she knew, was a weapon, and she wielded it well.

Yet when the moment came, her courage faltered. Halfway there, she stopped abruptly.

"Denys," she whispered.

"Aye?"

"I—I must go back."

"What, forgotten something?"

"My nerve," she admitted, voice trembling. "Oh, God, I can’t do this."

Denys squeezed her shoulder. "Steady, lass. I’ll be right beside you."

With that reassurance, she pressed forward, chin high but heart pounding.

Inside the house, the family sat in stiff silence, a volatile mix of curiosity, resentment, and reluctant pity swirling among them. When Denys slipped in to announce her arrival, Eli gave a terse nod.

"Bring her in. And remember—no outbursts."

The door creaked open.

Margaret stood framed in the threshold, pale but composed, her gaze steady despite the hostility radiating from the room. She moved with deliberate grace, her condition evident in the slight heaviness of her step, the way her hand occasionally drifted to her waist.

The women softened slightly at the sight. The brothers scowled.

She withdrew the letter from her bosom, kissed it as if in private devotion, then unfolded it with hands that barely trembled.

But then she noticed—they had seated her apart, like a leper. A flicker of hurt crossed her face before she masked it. With a subtle gesture, she summoned Denys to her side, and he stood sentinel behind her, solid as a bulwark.

Then she began to read.

Her voice, rich and warm, filled the room, weaving Gerard’s words into the air like a spell.

*"I do nothing doubt, my Margaret, that long ere this shall meet thy beloved eyes, Denys, my most dear friend, will have sought thee out..."*

At the mention of his name, Denys groaned, bowing his head. Margaret, without looking, reached back and grasped his hand—a silent pact between the two who loved Gerard most.

She read on, recounting Gerard’s trials—robbed, left destitute, saved by strangers who showed him kindness when his own family had turned him away. The room listened, rapt, as his voice echoed through hers.

When she reached the tale of the beggar—the cripple who was no cripple at all—Sybrandt snorted with laughter.

Margaret’s eyes flashed. She snapped the letter shut.

Eli rounded on his son. "One more word, and you’re out the door!"

Catherine, ever the peacemaker, murmured, "Read on, child. Pay no mind to fools."

Margaret took a steadying breath and continued. Gerard’s words painted a picture of hardship and resilience, of a boy forced to rely on wit and grit to survive. And through it all—his longing for home, for her.

By the time she finished, the room was silent, the air thick with unspoken emotions.

Margaret carefully folded the letter, her hands steady now. She had done what she came to do.

The rest was up to them.

When Margaret awoke, her head rested upon Catherine’s arm, and the faces of Gerard’s family—those who had not turned against him—gathered around her with rough but earnest kindness. Giles, loud and hearty, bellowed reassurance. “Gerard’s alive and well, or he’d never have penned such a letter—long as a sermon and fairer than any I’ve seen!”

Margaret’s voice trembled. “Aye, he was alive when he wrote it. But who knows what has befallen him since? Oh, why did he leave Holland to wander among strangers fierce as lions? And why did I not send him away sooner, rather than see him torn from his own flesh and blood?” She turned to Catherine, her eyes brimming with remorse. “Forgive me, you who are his mother.”

She tried weakly to slip from the chair to her knees, but Catherine held her fast, pulling her into an embrace instead. With a sigh, Margaret surrendered, pressing the letter into Eli’s hands. “Take it. I can read no more—and I would not leave this comfort.” She nestled closer, her other arm winding around Catherine’s neck.

Eli passed the letter to Richart. “Your eyes are younger than mine. Read it.”

Richart took the parchment, marveling at the script. “Never have I seen such fine writing—like needlework, yet clear as day. What a clerk he’d have made in Amsterdam instead of tramping the world!”

As Richart prepared to read, Kate and her mother exchanged a glance, then drew out their hidden sewing—tiny caps, half-finished, delicate as monks’ cowls.

When I came to myself, I was in the litter, my merchant friend holding my hand. I babbled nonsense, then shuddered in silence. He pressed wine to my lips—

Catherine interrupted. “Bless him!”

Eli hushed her.

—and when I told him what had happened, he examined my leg. The ankle was swollen, my hose barely stayed up for lack of points. “Sir,” I said, “I shall burden you now, and my psaltery is broken—no more music for our journey.” But he only patted my cheek and bade me fret not. He propped my leg on a pillow, tending me like a father.

Carried in the litter, I saw Lombardy’s plains burst into view—a land of milk and honey! Sloping fields, rivers laughing through meadows, orchards in bloom though it be winter. Vines, olives, cattle, and goats abound. The oxen wear linen about their necks; the women, straw hats embroidered with silk flowers. We crossed a river by chained ferry—a marvel! An old woman eyed a young girl, who hid behind her crucifix, fearing the evil eye.

Venice at last! A city of wonders—canals like liquid gold, palaces on piles, the great Piazza San Marco. But from my window, I see only plain streets and houses with oiled paper for glass. So I turn inward and share what I have learned in my wanderings.

The Germans are frank but love their drink too well. They say, *He who cannot swill and gorge is no good servant.* Yet their women are chaste and faithful—unlike our Dutch lasses, who command their men.

The French are fair-spoken but not always true. Still, they have courtesy in their blood. “Pray be seated,” they say, and a napkin on a bench reserves your place. Yet they flatter women and despise them—unless she rules them.

The Italians are subtle and polished. They judge a man by his bearing, not his garb. Here, even a cardinal might arm his guest with a fork to hold meat while cutting—as if God’s own hands were not fit! Their women bleach their hair, defying nature. Their laws are strict, their punishments bloody. And their trade in sin is state-sanctioned—twenty thousand courtesans honored for drawing strangers to Venice.

Yet they are wise in small things: roses forced to bloom in winter, fountains that dance like living things. And in London and Lübeck, water flows tame through pipes—a wonder!

Today I saw St. Mark’s relics—his very body in a chest of brass, his Gospel written by his hand. I touched his ring! And I saw the Virgin’s chair, her portrait by St. Luke, now faded.

But Venice’s darker side cannot be ignored. Three traitors’ heads rot on a slab, bought by the state for two thousand ducats each. A gallows of alabaster stands ready for the Doge himself, should he betray the republic. And on a wall, a fresco depicts four brothers who poisoned one another for land—all dead in a single afternoon, their souls lost, their estate passed to strangers.

As the saying goes:

Richart finished reading, and silence fell. Denys, ever the jester, feigned offense. “*My foul mouth?* Surely the clerk misread!”

Margaret blushed. The others hid smiles behind their sewing.

Denys crossed his arms. “Well? Do I speak foully?”

No one answered.

With a sigh, he gestured for Richart to continue. But the letter was done—and so was Gerard’s time in Venice. Tomorrow, he would sail for Rome.

Two months before the events in Eli’s home, the inhabitants of a small coastal village between Naples and Rome gathered on the shore, their eyes fixed on a ship struggling against a fierce gale. The vessel, ill-suited for such a tempest, pitched violently in the waves, now seeming to escape danger, now lurching perilously closer to destruction. The crowd watched with a morbid fascination—part pity, part selfish relief that it was not their own lives at stake.

Onboard, chaos reigned. Sailors scrambled across the deck, shouting contradictory orders, their prayers and curses lost in the howling wind. Passengers huddled together, some kneeling, others sprawled in terror, clutching at whatever they could as the ship groaned beneath them. Among them stood Gerard, his face pale but his jaw set, gripping a shroud with white-knuckled determination.

Around him, the air was thick with desperate invocations—saints and deities of every name called upon in a cacophony of fear. The sailors pleaded with the Virgin Mary under her many titles, while the landsmen bargained with local patron saints, each promising lavish offerings if only they might be spared. One merchant vowed a waxen effigy of St. Christopher’s own weight, only to whisper afterward that he had no intention of fulfilling such an absurd vow.

A crack like thunder split the air—the mast had splintered, the sail torn away by the wind. The ship, now unmanageable, was at the mercy of the waves. The captain, his face ashen, ordered everything thrown overboard to lighten the vessel. Passengers frantically tossed their belongings into the sea—until one man, an aged Jew, shrieked in protest as his sack of silver was wrenched from him and cast into the depths. He howled curses, shaking his fist at the heavens, his grief as wild as the storm itself.

Amidst the panic, two figures stood apart—a Roman woman, silent and pale, cradling her child with unnatural calm, and a towering Dominican friar, his voice steady as he recited scripture and offered absolution to those who sought it. Gerard, drawn to the friar’s unshaken resolve, edged closer, as if proximity to such courage might lend him strength.

When the crew abandoned ship in the lone boat, leaving the rest to their fate, Gerard acted. He lashed the wooden figure of the Virgin to the Roman woman, giving her and her child a chance to float. She looked at him with dark, liquid eyes and whispered a single word: *"Thyself?"* But he shook his head. *"I am a man,"* he said, *"and have no child to care for."*

Then, with the friar’s help, they heaved the broken mast into the waves and clung to it as the sea hurled them toward shore. The woman paddled fiercely with one arm, her child clutched tight in the other. The friar, feeling sand beneath his feet, abandoned the mast and strode through the surf like a colossus, while Gerard followed, battered but unbroken.

They were dragged ashore by fishermen, the friar offering a blessing before striding off toward Rome without a backward glance. Gerard, shivering and spent, watched as others washed up—some alive, most not. The Roman woman found him on the beach, took his hand, and pressed it to her lips without a word. Her gratitude needed none.

The next day, Gerard entered the Eternal City—no longer a boy, but a man who had faced death and survived.

Gerard found a modest lodging on the Tiber’s western bank and set out each day in search of work, carrying samples of his lettering to every shop that might employ him. But the booksellers met him with cold indifference.

"Your hand is too bold," one remarked.

"Your ink too dark," sniffed another.

The common refrain was: "Latin is no longer in demand. Can you write Greek?"

"Not as well as Latin," Gerard admitted.

"Then you will starve in Rome."

Undeterred, he borrowed a fine Greek manuscript—costly, but necessary—and spent the next fortnight mastering the script. By day he practiced; by evening, he sought patrons. When at last he presented a Greek sample finer than any in the shops, the traders shrugged. "Greek and Latin alike gather dust. The city overflows with manuscripts from across Europe. You should have come last year."

With a heavy heart, Gerard bought a psaltery, hoping music might sustain him. His landlady, a warm-hearted Siennese, often paused to chat. One day, over a shared meal, she eyed him keenly.

"Your spirits are dashed. What troubles you?"

He confessed his struggles.

"Ah, those sly traders!" she scoffed. "They have scribes in their pay—men they underpay while charging nobles double for their work. No wonder they turn you away. You write *too well*."

"How do you know?"

"Because you leave your door unlocked, unlike that miser Pietro. Women are curious, you know." She winked. "Your skill would shame their shoddy wares."

Gerard sighed. "You judge them too harshly, kind as you are."

"Child, Romans are a cunning breed. I? I am Siennese, thanks be to the Virgin!"

He lamented leaving Augsburg.

"Augsburg?" She sniffed. "What is that compared to Rome? Never heard of it." Then, brightening, she added, "But take heart! The great lords here are mad for books—trading lands and palaces for parchment to hoard in chests. Why, even the Pope and Cardinal Bassarion could keep a dozen scribes busy. I’ll ask Teresa—she hears court gossip."

Days later, she brought names of five nobles bitten by the "writing spider." Gerard crafted fresh samples, left them at each door, and waited.

No reply came.

As his purse dwindled, he skipped meals, filling time with music. His landlady soon noticed.

"Shame on you!" she scolded. "Do you think hunger conquers misfortune? *Patienza!* Eat—I’ll feed you for the love of heaven."

"Nay, I’ll not take charity while I’ve a coin left."

"Foolish boy! Leave cooking to women. Your mother would say the same."

Her kindness stirred him. "You remind me of her."

Her face softened. "Then heed me better."

---

One neighbor intrigued him—Pietro, a Florentine painter who locked his door like a miser. The landlady scoffed. "He brought money and an unfinished picture. When buyers offered fair sums, he scorned them. Now he’s sold all but his bed—a chest, if the keyhole tells true."

Gerard pitied him. One day, hearing groans, he knocked.

A surly voice bade him enter.

Inside, a gaunt young man coiled on a chest like a wary serpent, his eyes burning. A single painting leaned face to the wall.

"Forgive my intrusion," Gerard said. "I am your neighbor—an artist too."

"A painter?" Pietro brightened, waving him to the chest. "Sit!"

"Not a painter. A scribe—a caligrapher."

Pietro’s enthusiasm dimmed. "An artist *thinks* and paints his thoughts. A scribe merely copies."

"Yet a scribe preserves the wisdom of ages—things no brush could capture."

Pietro shrugged. "Better a poor painter than a fine scribe in Rome."

Just then, footsteps approached.

"The landlady," Gerard said. "Perhaps she’ll let me dine here."

She brought his meal, expressionless, and left.

Gerard hesitated. "I eat poorly alone. Share this with me?"

Pietro’s eyes flashed. "You offer a stranger your food?"

"There’s enough."

With sudden violence, Pietro hurled the dish out the window.

"Let that teach you to pity an artist you do not know!"

Gerard flushed, torn between fury and pity. At last, coldly: "You had a right to refuse—not to waste my share. Pride is fair, but justice is better."

Pietro stared, then sighed. "I misjudged you. Forgive me. The world has soured me. Leave me now."

As Gerard turned to go, a thought struck him. "Since you stole my dinner, I claim a look at your hidden painting."

Pietro tensed. "I vowed to stab the next man who mocked it."

"Then I’ll hold my tongue."

Reluctantly, Pietro turned the canvas—a Virgin and Child soaring above a vast landscape.

Gerard studied it in silence, stepping close, then back.

"Well?" Pietro snapped at last.

Gerard drew his sword.

Pietro flinched. "What—?"

"To keep your dagger at bay while I speak." He kept the blade leveled, eyes flicking between man and painting.

"First, your colors lack Flemish depth. But your *vision*—Mother of Heaven! You’ve flung your soul onto this panel. The drapery could flow freer, the figures being airborne. But this landscape!" He marveled. "Each distance perfect, shrinking to the horizon. And you set your saints aloft, untouched by the world below. In Flanders, they’d tear this from your hands!"

Pietro’s defiance crumpled. He seized Gerard in a fervent embrace.

"They called it a madman’s dream!"

"Then they are fools," Gerard said. "Your work is immortal. Now, monster, will you sup with me?"

Pietro laughed through tears. "Gladly!"

"Then I’ll order food. You keep your picture company."

As Gerard left, Pietro clasped the canvas. "Child of my heart," he whispered, "may you outlive us both."

A week had passed since Gerard and Pietro had begun their uneasy partnership. The Italian worked in fits and starts—flinging himself upon his task with feverish energy, only to cast it aside moments later with a curse, then snatching it up again in a burst of frustration. Gerard, ever steady, worked with quiet diligence, his Dutch patience a stark contrast to Pietro’s tempestuous spirit.

Their labor now was humble—painting playing cards to keep hunger at bay. When the last strokes were finished, Pietro gathered his own unfinished masterpiece, preparing to make his daily rounds in search of a buyer. Gerard pressed him to take the cards as well.

Pietro’s face darkened, but after a moment, he embraced Gerard roughly in the Italian fashion and took them, first laying the freshly painted cards in the sun to dry.

Left alone, Gerard turned to mending a small tear in his hose. His landlady found him thus occupied and scoffed.

"Are there no women in this house to do such work?" she teased. "When you’ve finished, come speak with Teresa—the friend I told you of, the one whose husband boasts of knowing great men but does little else."

Gerard obeyed, and to his surprise, Teresa was none other than the Roman matron whose child he had saved.

"Ah, madama!" he exclaimed. "The good dame did not say it was you. And the little boy—is he well? Unharmed by his strange voyage?"

"He is well," Teresa answered, though her voice trembled.

The landlady stared between them. "What is this? You know each other?"

Teresa composed herself. "He saved my child’s life. And never spoke a word of it."

The landlady turned on Gerard. "Shame on you! Keeping such a thing from me!"

But Teresa laid a gentle hand on her friend’s arm. "Do not scold him." Then she poured forth the tale of Gerard’s courage, her words painting his deed in colors so bright he scarcely recognized it as his own. He stood there, flushing like a maiden, unused to praise.

"And to think you asked me to help your lodger," Teresa said to the landlady. "I would have done so for your sake alone, but now—" She turned to Gerard. "In serving you, I serve my own heart."

The women then fell into eager discussion—how to breach the fortress of fees and flatterers that stood between an unknown artist and Rome’s wealthy patrons. Teresa shook her head at Gerard’s naive hope that leaving his work at noble doors would suffice.

"Without bribing the servants? Without promising them a share? You might as well have thrown your art into the Tiber!"

Gerard sighed. "Then how is a poor artist to find a patron?"

"By seeking a city less greedy than this," Teresa said bitterly. "The Roman court wants no sheep without wool." She paused, thoughtful, then promised to return the next day.

The landlady, once Teresa had gone, clapped her hands. "She has a plan—I can see it!"

No sooner had she spoken than Pietro stormed in, his face thunderous. Gerard exchanged a glance with the landlady and followed him upstairs.

"They let you bring your masterpiece home again?" Gerard asked gently.

"As always."

"Fools."

"That is not the worst of it," Pietro snarled.

"What then?"

"They bought the *cards*." His hands clenched, his whole body vibrating with outrage.

Gerard laughed. "All the better! They must have admired them greatly."

Pietro’s eyes flashed. "They fell upon them like wolves! Tried to hide their greed, but I saw it. I played them, I fleeced them—curse the dull-witted oafs!" He hurled a handful of silver coins to the floor and stomped on them, sending them skittering in every direction.

Gerard dropped to his knees, gathering the scattered coins with care. "You should laugh at their ignorance—and profit by it."

Pietro’s fury cooled into something darker. "So I shall. We’ll paint a pack a day. Let all Rome gamble itself to ruin—we’ll grow fat on its vices." Then his voice softened. "Though there was one queen I hated to part with—the one you painted. Hair like autumn, eyes like sapphires… and soul. You put your heart into her."

Gerard smiled faintly. "I did."

Pietro studied him. "You love her so much, yet you left her?"

Gerard’s voice was barely a whisper. "It is because I love her that I walked this long road."

Before more could be said, the landlady’s voice rang up the stairs. "Come down! You’re wanted!"
