Gerard descended to find Teresa waiting.

"Come with me, Ser Gerard," she said.

Gerard walked in silence beside Teresa, his artist’s mind preoccupied with wondering what she intended for him, rather than simply asking. At last, she spoke unprompted.

"A friend told me of a goldsmith’s wife in need of a scribe," she said. "Her shop is nearby. We shall not have far to go."

They soon arrived at the woman’s door. Teresa, ever poised, addressed her with grace.

"Madama, Leonora mentioned you seek a skilled writer. I bring you a fine one—this young man saved my child from the sea. I pray you look upon him kindly."

The goldsmith’s wife did look—long and intently—at Gerard’s handsome face. But her answer disappointed.

"Nay, I’ve no need of a scribe. Ah! Now I recall—it was my gossip, Claelia the sausage-maker, who asked for one. She told me, and I told Leonora."

Teresa offered a courteous farewell, and they departed. Claelia lived farther off, and when they reached her house, she was not at home.

"I shall wait," Teresa declared, settling herself with such regal stillness that Gerard, unable to resist, began sketching her furtively.

At last, Claelia returned. Teresa rose, presenting Gerard with quiet dignity.

"Madama, the goldsmith’s wife, Olympia, informed me you require a scribe. I bring you a fine one—he saved my child from the sea. For Our Lady’s sake, favor him."

Claelia shook her head. "Good dame, fair sir, I’ve no need of a writer. But now you remind me—it was my friend Appia Claudia who asked. She’s a tailor, lives in the Via Lepida."

Teresa withdrew calmly.

"Madama," Gerard ventured, "this may prove tedious for you."

She arched a brow. "What was ever done without patience?" Then, softer: "We shall knock on every door in Rome, but you *shall* have justice."

"But—I fear we are followed. A man lingers behind us, sometimes near, sometimes far."

"I have seen him," Teresa replied coolly, though a faint flush touched her cheek. "It is my poor Lodovico."

She stopped, turned, and beckoned. A reluctant figure approached. When he stood before them, Teresa fixed him with a steady gaze.

"Lodovico," she said, "know this young man of whom I have often spoken. Know him and love him—for he saved your wife and child."

Lodovico’s forced smile vanished. His face softened with genuine gratitude, and he embraced Gerard warmly. Yet something in the man’s earlier stealth made Gerard uneasy.

"You’ll join us, Ser Lodovico?" Gerard asked.

"Nay, signor. I go not beyond the Tiber."

"Addio, then," Teresa said pointedly.

"When will you return home, *Teresa mia*?"

"When my errand is done, Lodovico."

They walked on in silence, Teresa now wearing a shadow of melancholy.

Appia Claudia, mercifully, did not send them across the river again, but only down the street to Lucretia, the glove-maker.

Lucretia was a cheerful, talkative woman. "A writer? Nay, I keep all my accounts in my head! But my confessor, Father Colonna—ah, *he* is mad for them!"

Teresa frowned. "He is a friar, vowed to poverty. I cannot have this young man work unpaid—he saved my child."

Lucretia laughed. "Fear not! A Colonna never lacks for coin. And such a confessor! His head is leagues away—he scarce hears a word you say. Why, once I confessed to murdering a lodger’s child and baking her in a pie, and he mumbled, ‘Say three Paternosters, eat no eggs on Wednesday, *pax vobiscum*,’ and off he wandered!"

Teresa, patient, steered her back. "Would you take him to the friar and speak for him?"

Lucretia sighed. "I cannot leave my shop. But go yourself—his door is ever open to scholars. Only, mind this: if the young man claims skill, he must *have* it. The friar tolerates no bunglers. Once, he nearly thrashed a man for a poorly carved crucifix!"

Gerard hesitated. "Might I prepare a finer sample of my work before facing him?"

Teresa agreed.

On the way home, Gerard glimpsed a flawless sheet of vellum in a shop window—but knew he could not afford it. Resigned, he returned to his lodgings, intending to erase an old deed for reuse. Yet as he studied it, he realized it was a legal bond between Ghysbrecht Van Swieten and Floris Brandt. Destroying it would be unjust.

As he descended the stairs, pondering where to buy cheaper vellum, he found Teresa waiting—with the very skin he had coveted.

"How—how did you know?" he stammered.

Teresa smiled. "Your eyes lingered on it, and you sighed. Could I let you want for it after that?"

Gerard, overwhelmed, could only thank her.

The next day, armed with a carefully penned page of Greek and Latin, he faced Fra Colonna. The friar, surrounded by manuscripts and curiosities, barely glanced up until Gerard presented his work.

Then—astonishment. The Dominican embraced him.

***

Meanwhile, in Holland, an event unfolded that would ripple toward Gerard in ways none could foresee.

Before Eli’s cottage appeared a gaudy spectacle: a man on horseback, both so bedizened they seemed one absurd creature. The rider, adorned in silver and blue, announced his purpose.

"I come for your dwarf."

Catherine bristled. "We’ve no dwarf here!"

Eli, wiser, sighed. "He means our Giles."

Giles himself settled the matter. Striding out, he declared, "If my folk are fools, I am not. I’ll go with you—now."

Catherine wept, but Giles was resolute. "I’ll visit often. And if I dislike the court, not all the dukes in Christendom will keep me!"

Mounting the tallest horse with a rope from the window—to the pursuivant’s delight—Giles rode off, leaving sorrow behind.

At court, his sharp tongue and fearless truths made him a favorite—a novelty among sycophants.

Little did he know how far his path would twist—or how his fate would intertwine with Gerard’s, across the miles and years.

Happy is the man who possesses twin anchors: Merit and the Favor of Women. Had I but a fraction of Gerard’s charm to draw upon, I might rise to laurels—or at least to some modest professorship. But while I lament my lack of such a chain, let me instead paint for you the portrait of Fra Colonna.

The true torchbearers of antiquity’s revival were not dry scholars, but storytellers—Petrarch and Boccaccio. Their labor did not blaze into immediate glory, yet they sowed seeds that would not perish. By their time, Italy was never without some keeper of Greek wisdom, and every learned Greek who set foot upon her shores was welcomed as a brother. The fourteenth century closed with the birth of Poggio, Valla, and Guarino the Elder; by the dawn of the fifteenth, Florence, under Cosimo de’ Medici, had become a nest of Platonists. Under Gemistus Pletho, these thinkers sought to dethrone Aristotle—for few minds are vast enough to honor one giant without slighting another. Theodore Gaza defended the Stagirite with reason; George of Trebizond with venom. Then Bessarion, cardinal and Greek-born, struck back in his *Adversus calumniatorem Platonis*.

Into this fray of ink and intellect stepped Francesco Colonna, a Florentine noble who, at twenty, took the Dominican habit—not for piety, but for the quiet of the cloister. He was a scholar of tongues, a collector of manuscripts, a connoisseur of art and stone. Inscriptions on ancient ruins shook his faith in written histories; years in the East enriched him with rare texts and mastery of Greek, Latin, Hebrew, Syriac. Returning, he found Italy transformed—Alfonso of Naples, the Estes, and above all, his old friend Thomas of Sarzana, now Pope Nicholas V, who filled the Vatican with five thousand manuscripts and set the learned to translating: Poggio on Xenophon, Gaza on Theophrastus, Trebizond on Eusebius.

Here, in this garden of the mind, Fra Colonna flourished—a skeptic in religion, a zealot in art. He dismissed Christian rites as mummery for the masses, yet revered those with pagan roots, blind to his own inconsistency. A man of many talents—painting, modeling, music, tongues—he lacked only the need to profit by them. His joy lay in appreciation, a rare virtue in any age, for most men are blind to some great art or science, and the dullest are often the proudest.

Fra Colonna took Gerard under his wing, and soon the young scribe’s skill became the talk of Rome’s palaces. "You must not linger with me," the friar insisted. "A man must rise in the world." And so Gerard passed into the service of greater patrons, at thrice the pay. Three hundred crowns for Aristotle’s *Rhetoric*—such was the frenzy for Greek manuscripts. The nobles fawned on him, but Gerard, ever the shrewd Dutchman, kept his head.

Then came the day he dined with Cardinal Bessarion. A mile from the head of the table, he marveled at pheasants served in full plumage, chickens cooked in glass, napkins folded into wings and fans. Afterward, the tables were whisked away, revealing sweets beneath—devoured by the ladies, eyed warily by the men. When all had dispersed, Gerard left, having dined with a prince of the Church.

Greater honor still awaited. Summoned by the cardinal, he was taken to the Vatican, ushered up a private stair, and set to copying Plutarch in a chamber beside a vast, guarded curtain. Once, curiosity moved him to peek—only to meet the crossed halberts of papal guards and glimpse figures bowing before some unseen presence. He returned to his work, chastened.

Then came Jacques Bonaventura, the Pope’s nephew, a lively soul who barged in demanding entertainment. Gerard read him Alexander’s life, and the young captain, inflamed, vowed to seek glory over pleasure.

Through it all, Gerard’s heart stayed fixed on Margaret. His art brought wealth, but he lived simply, hoarding coin for their future—a home in Bruges, Antwerp, or Augsburg, where they might live out their days in peace. He helped Pietro too, securing a noble patron for the artist’s work.

Yet though he resisted vice and luxury, a greater trial loomed. For in Rome’s gilded halls, temptation wore many faces—and the most perilous still lay in wait.

Fra Colonna had free rein of the Pope’s library, and often, when his labors ended at the same hour as Gerard’s, the two would walk the city together. To Gerard, Rome was a vision of grandeur—its churches, palaces, and ruins bathed in golden light. But the friar, ever the skeptic, tempered his enthusiasm with cold reason.

“Rome?” he scoffed. “This is but the tomb of mighty Rome.” He showed Gerard how twenty or thirty feet of ancient triumphal arches lay buried beneath the streets, how modern paths ran over the bones of forgotten columns and palaces. With a sardonic smile, he likened the city to a swallow’s nest perched on the crumbling eaves of an abbey.

Gerard marveled. “Then old Rome must have been glorious indeed.”

“Judge for yourself,” said the friar. “You see the great sewer, a feat of Rome’s infancy, yet sturdier than Vesuvius. You glimpse fragments of the Temple of Peace. But imagine the Capitol crowned with five-and-twenty temples! Even this Monte Savello—what is it but the carcass of Marcellus’s theater? And Testacio, that towering hill? A refuse heap, where Roman women flung their broken pots. Now it stands a monument to time’s mockery. *Ex pede Herculem; ex ungue leonem.*”

Gerard listened, respectful but uneasy when the friar’s admiration for pagan virtues edged toward heresy.

“Has the world lost by Christ’s coming?” he ventured, then flushed, fearing he had insulted his benefactor.

“Saints forbid!” Fra Colonna waved a hand. “’Twere rank heresy to say so.” Yet his next words spiraled into subtle evasion, praising heathen wisdom while paying hollow tribute to the Church.

Their walk led them to a church where a knot of onlookers gathered. A demon was being exorcised within. The friar let out a small, derisive moan—his customary protest against superstition.

“Come away,” he muttered.

“Nay, father, I beg you! I’ve never seen a devil cast out.”

With a shrug, the friar relented. Inside, the demoniac knelt before the altar, a scarf knotted around his neck like a leash. The priest, breviary in hand, began his incantations, calling forth fiends by name—Ashtaroth, Belial, Nebul—until at last the victim shuddered at *Nebul*.

“Ah! There you are, reptile!” The priest launched into prayer, commanding the demon’s expulsion from every limb and orifice. Then, turning to the crowd, he sighed. “A stubborn devil, this.”

What followed was a grotesque pantomime—cuffs, kicks, spittle, and candle flames pressed to flesh, all met with howls from the possessed. The priest concluded by displaying the Host, and the demon, in a final shriek, confessed defeat—not by holy rites, but by a stone on the windowsill, once touched by the angel Gabriel.

The friar moaned again. “You *believed* him?”

“Of course! Who but an infidel would doubt such a revelation?”

“A liar’s tale, bolstered by a relic. Fiends, if they exist, are not so simple.” He hustled Gerard away, muttering, “Some rogue split his wife’s skull on that stone—mark my words.”

Gerard winced. “You shamed the good priest.”

The friar chuckled. “A touch of Socratic wit blisters them every time.”

---

Days later, Gerard was summoned to the Palazzo Cesarini. Ushered into a grand chamber, he found a haughty young princess surrounded by maids. She demanded a love letter for one Ercole Orsini—then promptly declared she despised him.

“What lie shall I tell him?” she mused, chin resting on her hand, a vision of regal deceit.

Seizing the moment, Gerard sketched her profile. When she noticed, she was at first indignant, then enchanted. “You *must* color it!” she cried. Thus began a profitable arrangement: portraits, full-length studies, and a steady stream of gold.

Margaret’s dowry grew nearer.

---

Holy Thursday brought processions, piety, and spectacle. The Pope, resplendent in crimson and white, blessed the crowds from horseback. Cardinals flanked him—all bareheaded save de’ Medici, who chatted with him as an equal.

At St. John Lateran’s, the heads of St. Peter and St. Paul were unveiled—waxen effigies, the friar insisted, not relics. Gerard, awestruck, disagreed.

“They did not exist in the year 800,” the friar grumbled. “This is mere theater.”

But Gerard saw only holy wonder.

Later, they witnessed Flagellants, their backs striped with blood, laughing as they lashed themselves. “For *your* sins!” one boy jeered at a pitying woman.

“Vanity,” the friar muttered. “No whip can scourge *that* out.”

---

In Gerard’s quarters, a debate raged between him and Jacques Bonaventura over the Pope’s blessing of beasts—ridiculous, Jacques sneered. Gerard defended it.

The argument was cut short by a gentle voice.

“Happy youth, to burn so for trifles.”

They turned—and knelt. The Pope stood there, frail yet serene, his white beard like silk. He examined Gerard’s work on Plutarch and praised it.

“A master of character, this Greek,” he mused. “Boccaccio? A skillful storyteller, but narrow. His world is all monks and nuns and stolen pleasures. Plutarch shows us war, policy, love—life as it is.”

Jacques dared to mention the Pope’s own novel.

His Holiness sighed. “A folly of my youth. When I wrote tales, I little thought to wear this ring.”

And with that, the Vicar of Christ departed, leaving Gerard torn between reverence and doubt—between the grandeur of Rome and the whispers of a skeptic friar.

The Princess Claelia commissioned a full-length portrait of herself. Gerard, eager to assist, suggested his friend Pietro Vanucci for the task.

She refused with a smile. “I’ll slight Gerardo only when his work displeases me.” Knowing his skill lay more in draftsmanship than color, Gerard then proposed an unconventional approach—drawing her as a Roman statue, her form rendered in meticulous marble-like strokes upon paper.

She hesitated when he explained the required attire: the tunic, toga, and sandals of antiquity.

“I might as well stand in my smock,” she retorted with medieval bluntness.

Gerard, undeterred, praised the classical garb’s elegance. “Signora, you possess the very features of Rome—the noble bearing, the shapely arm. Modern dress does you injustice.”

She arched a brow. “Flattery, Gerardo? Very well. Return on Saturday; I shall decide then.”

The intervening days were spent in secret fittings—testing whether the ancient garments flattered her enough to justify their antiquity.

On his way to the appointment, Gerard was halted by a sight in a shop window: a printed copy of *Lactantius*. His heart sank. *The press has reached Rome.*

Entering, he feigned casual inquiry. “How long has printing flourished here?”

The shopkeeper laughed. “Not in Rome, messer. This was printed in the Apennines.”

Gerard examined the title page—*Opera et impensis Sweynheim et Pannartz Alumnorum Joannis Fust. Impressum Subiacis. A.D. 1465.*

“Will you buy?” the man urged. “No scribe matches its even letters, and at a fraction the cost.”

Gerard shook his head. “I am a calligrapher. These Fustian disciples hound me, stealing bread from my lips. Yet I bear them no ill will.” He left, troubled. *Margaret, we must act swiftly.*

At the princess’s palazzo, he was led to a luxurious chamber—panther skins underfoot, a fountain murmuring at its center. A heavy tapestry curtain divided the space.

Then—silence.

A rustle. The curtain parted.

Before him stood a vision between Venus and Minerva: Claelia, draped in the toga, her arm bared, her pose regal. Gerard caught his breath.

She blushed under his gaze. “Well? Am I monstrous enough for your art?”

“Fra Colonna would worship you,” he breathed.

She laughed, then struck a pose. For an hour, he worked in absorbed silence until she swayed, fatigued. “Enough, Gerardo. I am no marble yet.”

He apologized, offering water. She demurred at his haste. “Gently. Press it to my brow.”

As he obeyed, she sighed. “Again.”

A maid was summoned. But when Floretta approached, the princess’s nostrils flared. “Wretch!” In a flash, she seized the girl, boxed her ears, then drove her out with slaps.

Gerard gaped. “Was that necessary?”

“She reeks of musk,” Claelia snapped, then softened. “Are you angry with me?”

He spoke carefully. “Those who command should master themselves.”

She conceded with a purse for Floretta. “Tell her Gerardo pleaded for her.”

Then, seating herself, she bade him talk.

Gerard, uneasy, stammered until she leaned close, her voice trembling. “Gerar-do, teach me your magic. Make me as happy near you as I am beside you.”

Her hand slid down his arm, light yet possessive.

He stiffened. “Signora, this is madness. I am no fit object for such affection.”

She withdrew, eyes flashing. “You fear my kin’s daggers?”

“I fear God and my conscience. My heart is pledged—to Margaret, in Holland.”

The name struck like a blow. Claelia paled. “A common girl? You scorn me for *her*?”

“Not scorn—honor. I cannot feign what I do not feel.”

Her fury erupted. “Then know this: beyond that door, armed men wait to cut you down at my word. And I will send tidings to your Margaret—of your faithlessness, then your death.”

Gerard dropped to his knees. “God have mercy!” He poured out his tale—his and Margaret’s trials, their sacrifices, their love. Tears wet her hand as he clasped it. “Would you part us? What remorse would follow such cruelty?”

Her defiance crumbled. “Go,” she whispered. “Leave Rome. If we meet again, I *will* kill you.”

A gesture summoned Floretta. “See him out—by the postern.”

As he turned, Claelia stood motionless, head bowed, weeping.

At his lodgings, he wasted no time. “A noble seeks my life,” he told his landlady.

“Change your quarters,” she advised.

He did, then pored over Floris Brandt’s deed—realizing Ghysbrecht had cheated Peter Brandt of his dues. *Fool, not to have read this sooner!*

A ship to Amsterdam sailed in four days. He booked passage.

“Italy’s air is too thick with knives,” he muttered. “And Dutch skippers seldom drown.”

His landlady met him upon return, eyes alight with news.

But Gerard’s thoughts were already on the sea, and home.

Gerard awoke to consciousness—and to despair.

The fever had raged within him, a tempest in the veins, a fire in the brain. His auburn hair, long as a woman’s, lay shorn upon the table. Day by day, death’s shadow crept closer. The leech withdrew, abandoning hope. But then, as the sun dipped below the horizon on the fifth day, Gerard fell into a sleep so deep that some whispered he would never wake from it.

An old nurse, wise in the ways of suffering, declared that if his youth could sustain him for twelve hours of such slumber, he might yet live. The landlady, devout and desperate, vowed candles to the Virgin for every hour he slept.

Twelve hours passed. She rejoiced.

Twenty-four. She faltered.

Thirty. The nurse was summoned.

"Shall we wake him?"

The woman studied him—his steady breath, the dampness of his palm. "Learned men might rouse him to peer at his tongue and learn nothing. But we know better. Let nature mend what man cannot."

By the forty-eighth hour, curiosity swarmed like flies at the door. Only two were permitted entry: Fra Colonna, his eyes wet with grief, and the towering Fra Jerome, calm as ever, beads slipping through his fingers as if counting the moments between life and death.

Then, at last—sixty hours in that fathomless repose—Gerard stirred. His eyelids lifted gently, as though from a brief nap. He blinked, confused. His fingers found his shorn head. He saw Fra Colonna and smiled—then memory struck like a blade, and his face twisted in agony. A cry tore from his throat, and he turned his face to the wall.

The landlady wept. She knew the torment of waking to loss.

Fra Jerome murmured prayers. Gerard writhed beneath the bedclothes.

Fra Colonna, trembling, spoke of the immortal Muses—steadfast where mortal love had failed. But his voice broke, and he fled.

Fra Jerome lingered. "The Muses are phantoms," he intoned. "The Church alone offers peace. Earth has betrayed you; the Church stands open. Consecrate yourself to her."

Then he, too, was gone.

"The *Church*?" Gerard snarled, lurching upright, his fist clenched. "A curse upon it! Were it not for the Church, I would not lie here broken—and she would not lie cold in Holland! Margaret! My love! They let you die—blind, selfish wretches! I would have seen the signs. I would have saved you!"

Tears came, hot and furious. Then rage again, black as pitch.

"The Church! For its sake, I was torn from her. Damn the Church! Damn the world! Thieves and murderers live on, while angels perish. Curse life! Curse death! Curse the hand that made them so!"

Had Fra Jerome heard, he would have seen Gerard burned without a second thought. But the words were lost to the walls, and only the landlady returned, finding him dressing with feverish haste.

"Why rise so soon?" she pleaded.

"What use is lying here? I seek oblivion."

"Oblivion? Child, you are too young for such words!"

"Then I am old in grief," he said bitterly. He donned his finest clothes, though it was not Sunday.

The woman paled. "What new master do you serve now?"

A hollow laugh. "Satan."

And with that, Gerard stepped into the street—his cap rakish, his limbs unsteady, his face a death-mask twisted with defiance. A man already half-lost, walking into the dark.

A shadow fell upon a noble soul. Gerard’s love for Margaret had been his guiding light, pure and unwavering. Now that light was extinguished, and he drifted through a sea of despair, unmoored and lost. Worse still was the bitter sting of regret—had he stayed in Holland, had he not chased ambition across the continent, she might still be alive. The thought festered in him, poisoning his heart.

Driven by anguish, he flung himself into the whirl of vice. In Rome, where pleasure too often meant debauchery, he became its willing slave. Wine, women, dice—anything to drown the relentless ache within. The gold he had once set aside for Margaret now fueled his recklessness, and he soon led the very company he had once shunned. His spirit, once so tender, hardened. He abandoned his careful script for hasty portraiture, then abandoned even that when the lure of revelry called.

The descent was swift. He left his old lodgings, unable to bear the landlady’s gentle reproaches, and moved where none would remind him of what he had been. His new companions were the dissolute, the jaded, the cruel. Among them, Pietro Vanucci—a cynic, a libertine, a man who mocked virtue—became his closest shadow. Gerard, once so full of fire, now moved through life like a ghost, cold to friendship, deaf to conscience.

Then came the day of the buffalo race up the Tiber, a spectacle of folly devised by Vanucci. The revelers gathered, each with a beauty on his arm, laughing, drinking, daring one another to greater excess. Gerard arrived late, leading a figure so striking the others fell silent—tall, olive-skinned, with eyes like dark jewels. The women bristled; the men gaped. Only Vanucci, coiled in his seat like a serpent, watched with sly amusement.

The journey began, the boat drawn by snorting beasts, the party mocking their sluggish pace. A foolhardy gallant, goading one too many times, was tossed into the river, and the laughter rose again. But Gerard’s companion, sharp-tongued and fearless, turned on the women when their whispers grew too pointed.

*"Your beauty is bought in shops!"* she jeered. *"Paint on your cheeks, dye in your hair, padding in your bodices—false from head to heel!"*

The retort was swift. *"And you, who are so perfect—are you even a woman?"* A handful of almonds thrown into her lap betrayed her: the instinctive closing of the knees revealed a boy beneath the disguise. Laughter erupted, but the women, disarmed by his beauty, only fawned over him the more.

Yet Gerard, standing apart, felt no mirth. The sight of Princess Claelia passing in her galley had struck him like a blow. Once, he had turned away from her, faithful to Margaret. Now, what was he? A drunkard, a wastrel, a man who kept company with painted boys and jaded rakes. Shame curdled in him.

From that moment, his despair deepened. He abandoned even the hollow laughter of his companions, wandering alone through Rome’s ruins, brooding, drinking, staring into the dark waters of the Tiber. The thought that had once horrified him now whispered with terrible allure: *Here lies peace. Here lies an end.*

And all the while, unseen, a foe watched. Not a friend to pull him back, but an enemy, waiting for the final stumble.

The abyss yawned before him. And Gerard, blind to all but his pain, stepped closer to its edge.

The chamber was opulent, its floor strewn with the striped and spotted pelts of exotic beasts. A woman sat rigid, her arms outstretched, fingers half-curled into fists. Her face was a tempest—flushed one moment, pallid the next—and her foot tapped incessantly against the rich furs beneath her.

A servant drew aside the heavy curtain, and the lady’s cheeks darkened with fresh color.

*"Altezza,"* the maid whispered, eyes downcast, *"the man is here."*

The lady snatched up a black mask and pressed it to her face. In an instant, the flush vanished, leaving only the stark contrast of the dark silk against her marble skin—a sight both striking and unsettling.

The man who entered was unremarkable in every way—neither handsome nor repulsive, his clothes plain but respectable. Only his eyes betrayed him, darting ceaselessly, like a creature accustomed to shadows. He bowed deeply, repeatedly, then stood waiting.

*"They have told you why you are summoned?"* the lady asked.

Her brow furrowed. *"Explain."*

*"The lesser vendetta is death alone. We take a man as he leaves a church, or in an unguarded hour. The greater—"* he paused, *"—we wait until he is fresh from sin, so his soul is damned as well as his body. Though, in truth, the fashion for such work has waned of late."*

The assassin exhaled, relieved. *"Then the deed is simple. Yet I must know the man—his name, his dwelling."*

For the first time, the killer showed unease. He urged caution, precision.

*"Do not fear,"* she replied coldly. *"Listen well. He is young, tall, with auburn hair and dark blue eyes—a face of honesty, though it lies. He lodges at the glover’s house on the Via Claudia, the corner dwelling. There are but three men within: himself, a dark and stunted painter, and a slender boy. The one who betrayed me is a stranger, fair, and taller than you."*

The bravo absorbed every word. *"It is enough."*

*"Wait,"* she commanded. *"He haunts the Tiber after dusk—no doubt to meet his whore, who dares name me her rival. Strike him there. Let her find him cold upon the bank."*

The assassin displayed a steel gauntlet. *"Our strikes require such force, we must guard our hands. This is our hammer."* He parted his doublet, revealing a glint of mail beneath, then laid a stiletto upon the table. Its edge gleamed.

The lady recoiled—then, with sudden fascination, took the blade in her pale fingers, testing its point against her skin.

*"Beware, madam,"* he warned.

*"His flesh is as fair as mine,"* she murmured, a flicker of pity crossing her face—gone as swiftly as it came. *"But his heart is black as pitch. Tell me—do I not act justly, removing a slanderer, a traitor?"*

She barely heard him. *"I could have loved him—to the death. As now I hate him. Fool! To scorn a princess, then grovel before the dregs of the city, making me a mockery!"* Her gaze snapped up. *"Why do you linger? Go. Be quick."*

*"Ah. I had forgotten."* She pushed a heavy purse across the table. *"Here is more than half. Return for the rest when the deed is done."*

*"You will see me soon, Signora."* He bowed himself out, leaving her alone.

The princess sat motionless, the mask still shielding her face, her nails digging into the wood. Jealousy, wounded pride, and fear of scandal burned within her—for until Gerard, no stain had touched her name. Now she was fury incarnate, a tigress poised to strike.

Yet as the assassin’s footsteps faded, a chill crept over her. A shadow of doubt. A whisper of regret.

Gerard and Margaret were souls out of time. But the princess was the very essence of her age—proud, passionate, vengeful, reckless. Power was her curse. Left to her own devices, her nerves might have tempered her fury, her crimes mere scratches. But power lent her the hands of men, and in those hands, her whims became deadly.

And so, as one lost soul contemplated ending his own life, another’s gold sharpened a dagger for him.

Two days after the events I have recounted, Gerard found himself wandering through one of Rome’s grimier streets when a sudden thunderstorm forced him to take shelter in a dim, low-ceilinged tavern. The rain drummed heavily against the roof as he called for wine, and with each cup, his thoughts grew muddled. Eventually, he slumped forward, his head resting on his hands, drifting in and out of a dull stupor.

As the storm raged outside, the tavern filled with rough voices and coarse laughter, rousing him slightly. Through the haze, he became aware of two men nearby, speaking in hushed tones. One was a pardoner, his robes marking him as a seller of indulgences. The other, dressed plainly but with a furtive air, kept his hat pulled low, obscuring his face—a man who clearly preferred shadows to scrutiny.

They were negotiating a transaction, not of goods, but of sin. The pardoner recited prices from a worn parchment, a grim ledger of transgressions and their corresponding fees. Crimes varied in cost, some surprisingly affordable, others steep—though murder, curiously, was priced lower than lesser offenses that might shock a moral man. The haggling concluded with exchanged coin and murmured assurances, the bargain struck before the sin itself had even been committed.

Gerard, still slumped at the table, caught only fragments of their exchange. He lacked the will to intervene, his mind clouded by grief and wine. When the two men parted ways, he lifted his head just enough to glare at the retreating figure of the buyer—a would-be assassin, now absolved in advance.

*If Margaret were alive,* he thought bitterly, *I’d throttle you where you stand.* But she was gone, and with her, his last tether to outrage. The world could rot for all he cared.

Later, as he staggered home, he noticed a figure in a slouched hat pacing briskly across the street. The same man from the tavern, perhaps? It hardly mattered. Gerard’s heart was too numb to kindle suspicion or fear. He passed by without a second glance, the weight of his sorrow drowning all else.

Two nights had passed since Gerard’s strange disappearance, and Pietro Vanucci sat at the supper table, his fingers drumming impatiently against the wood. The hour was late—past nine—and still, Gerard had not returned.

"Where in the devil’s name is that boy?" Pietro muttered, pushing his plate away. "Andrea, go and see if he’s slipped in unnoticed."

Andrea sighed but obeyed, climbing the stairs to Gerard’s chamber. He returned moments later, not with Gerard, but with a slip of parchment clutched in his hand. "This was on his table," he said, his voice uneasy. "It bears your name."

Pietro snatched the note and read it aloud, his tone laced with bitter mockery, though his fingers trembled slightly.

Pietro scoffed. "A burden, is it? Yet not so heavy as to keep him from missing supper."

"Ha! As if my shoulders could bear his sorrows as well as my own."

Andrea’s breath hitched.

*"For my body, ere this ye read, it will lie in Tiber. Trouble not to look for it. 'Tis not worth the pains. Oh unhappy day that it was born—oh happy night that rids me of it."*

Pietro forced a laugh, though his face had gone ashen. "The fool’s jesting. He’ll come slinking in by dawn, mark my words."

But Andrea was already on his feet, his voice rising in panic. "He means to drown himself! Holy saints, help us! We must find him!" He bolted from the room, shouting into the street, calling for aid.

A small crowd gathered, sluggish and reluctant. The Tiber at night was no place for men—too many whispered horrors lurked in its depths. Yet a single figure, a lackey in the shadows, stepped forward. "I’ll go," he said.

And so they set off—Andrea, the lackey, and a handful of others—their footsteps echoing in the moonlit streets. The air grew damp as they neared the river, the scent of water thick in their nostrils. Then, rounding a corner, they saw him—a man, bareheaded, clothes soaked, his hair glistening wet in the pale light.

"There he is!" one cried.

The stranger froze, then fled.

Andrea gave chase, his heart hammering, but the man twisted into a maze of alleys and vanished. When the pursuers regrouped, Pietro shook his head. "That wasn’t Gerard. The build was wrong, the face unfamiliar."

"Then why run?" someone muttered.

They followed the trail of dripping water—not toward the river, but to the looming gates of a monastery. A chill passed through them. Then Andrea spotted the trail again, leading back to the Tiber’s edge.

At the bank, the ground was sodden, as if a man had dragged himself from the water. But was it Gerard? They paid a fisherman to drag the river, nets sinking into the silent depths. The moon watched, indifferent.

Andrea ran home, hoping against hope—but Gerard’s room was empty, his bed untouched.

Dawn came. The search was abandoned.

The days that followed were silent, the Tiber keeping its secrets. And Gerard?

Gone.

The truth lay hidden along the riverbank, its story written in shadows and silence—an event so singular it might never find its equal in all the ages of the world. Yet it passed unseen, unread, by those who lacked the eyes to decipher it.

Gerard had made his choice. With a resolve as cold as the water that would claim him, he penned his farewell to Pietro and Andrea, then stole toward the river as dusk deepened into night. He carried no hesitation, only the weight of copper and silver in his pockets to drag him down faster. The spot he chose was quiet, forgotten—a place where no curious eyes lingered.

Or so he thought.

A figure stood at the mouth of an alley, motionless. Gerard stiffened. Had he been followed? Watched? He forced himself to walk casually past, then circled back. The man was gone—only to reappear moments later, bending as if to adjust his shoe. Moonlight glinted on steel. A gauntlet. A bravo.

Yet Gerard, blind to the thought of enemies, saw only an instrument. He approached, desperate.

*"Sell me your blade,"* he urged, pressing his coins into the man’s grip. *"One stroke—that’s all I ask. Must I beg? Look, I’ll make it easy for you."* He tore open his doublet, baring his throat.

The assassin stared, uncomprehending.

*"What good are you?"* Gerard spat. *"Too cowardly to strike a man who faces you? Then turn away and let me do it myself!"*

With a final snarl, he flung himself into the black water.

The splash shattered the night. And then—

A second plunge. The killer, moving at last, surged after him into the river.

What happened next would be told by the assassin himself.

A woman’s sorrows run deeper than a man’s pen can often trace. We sketch in bare lines what her heart must color in full—griefs we scarcely fathom, trials we dimly perceive. So it was with Margaret Brandt. Until that fateful day when she entered Eli’s house as a foe and left a friend, her suffering had been but faintly outlined. Now, as her greatest trial neared, Gerard was far from her side.

She did not often seek Eli’s house, though his favor had warmed toward her. For every refusal, she had a ready excuse—but never spoke the truth: that two men within those walls filled her with a dread she could not name. Their faces bore the stamp of wickedness, their silent exchanges spoke of secrets best left unread. Instead, she welcomed Catherine and her daughter into her own home. Catherine came daily, her heart alight with the coming joy, for she was one of those women who lived to nurture life, to guide young mothers as if the children were her own.

“The poor lass has no one who knows a thing about such matters,” Catherine fretted to Eli. “What good are a pair of soldiers at a time like this? They ought to be turned out at the door!”

And so Catherine stayed, a comfort to Margaret, whose fears grew as the days passed. She would die, she thought, and Gerard would never know. Yet hardship had strengthened her where ease might have weakened another. Poverty and her father’s frailty had forced her to labor ceaselessly, and now, by Catherine’s counsel, she worked until the very end.

Then, at sunset on a quiet evening, her trial was over. Weak but safe, she lay with a tiny face beside her, the world suddenly brighter, wider—a mother now.

“Why do you weep, child?” Catherine murmured, brushing a tear from Margaret’s cheek.

“He is not here to see.”

“Ah, but he will be before the babe is weaned. And think—God has been good to you. It might have been a girl, as my Kate once threatened me! But here lies the finest boy in Holland, stout and sound, praise the saints.”

Margaret smiled through her tears. “I am ungrateful to weep. Only—if Gerard could but see him. I bore his absence in my pain, but now it cuts deeper that he is not here to share my joy.” Her voice broke. “Prithee, come to me, Gerard! Dear, dear Gerard!” Her trembling arms reached for what was not there.

Catherine turned away, her own eyes wet. When she looked back, Margaret’s gaze held something strange.

“Did you hear nothing?” Margaret whispered.

“No, love. What?”

“I called for Gerard just now.”

“Aye, I heard that well enough.”

“And he answered me.”

Catherine shook her head. “Hush, now.”

“But I heard it—clear as day. He spoke my name. ‘Margaret.’ Only… his voice was sad. Though perhaps that was the distance. Voices always sound sad from far away.” She sighed, then brightened. “Why do you fret, mother? I feel no sorrow tonight. Not a pain, not a care.” And her eyes, soft with wonder, returned to the child in her arms.

That same night, in distant Rome, Gerard flung himself into the Tiber. At the very hour she heard his voice, he cried her name in the jaws of death and despair.

How shall this be explained? I cannot.

In the dimly lit guest chamber of a Dominican convent, a lone figure lay upon a narrow bed, his body wracked with exhaustion. The violent fits of nausea that had tormented him had finally subsided, leaving him as weak as Margaret had been that fateful night in Holland. A fire crackled on the hearth, its orange glow flickering over the walls, and beside it hung the stranger’s damp clothes, slowly drying in the warmth.

A massive friar sat at his bedside, the leather-bound breviary in his hands whispering with each turn of a page. The patient’s eyes flickered open occasionally, drawn by the murmured prayers, before fluttering shut again with a groan. The monk knelt, pressing his forehead to the floor in fervent supplication before rising with a sigh.

“Day breaks,” he said softly. “I must prepare for matins.”

The stranger stirred. “Father Jerome—before you go—how did I come to be here?”

The friar’s voice was solemn. “By the hand of Heaven. You cast away God’s gift, and He returned it to you. Think on that. You have tasted the bitterness of the world. Now let the Church offer you peace.”

With a final blessing, the friar departed, leaving the man alone with his thoughts. He drifted in and out of consciousness, the events of the past hours slipping through his fingers like water.

When he next awoke, a new figure sat beside him—a layman with sharp, restless eyes, a stark contrast to the tranquil presence of Friar Jerome. The man leaned forward, his voice low.

“How do you feel?”

“Weak… terribly weak.” The stranger frowned. “Where have I seen you before?”

The man hesitated, then gave a grim smile. “None the worse for my gauntlet, I hope? I struck you—else we’d both be at the bottom of the Tiber.”

Recognition flickered in the stranger’s eyes. “You—you saved me?”

“Aye. I was by the river on… business, when you came to me, begging for death. But before I could oblige, I knew you—the man who once saved my wife and child upon the sea.”

A shudder passed through the stranger. “Teresa’s husband. And yet—an assassin?”

“At your service,” the man replied dryly. “You flung yourself into the Tiber, commanded me to stand aside. Had it been anyone else, I’d have obeyed. But you—you cried out a name as you sank. *Margaret.*”

The stranger’s breath hitched.

“And my heart answered with *Teresa*,” the assassin continued. “How could I face her if I let you die? So I went in after you. You fought me—but I dragged you to shore.”

The stranger closed his eyes, the weight of his own folly pressing down on him. He had cursed Heaven, defied it—and yet here he was, pulled from death by the hand of a killer. Was this divine mercy or mockery?

A distant organ swelled, its notes rising through the convent walls, followed by the pure voices of the choir. Among them, one stood out—a boy’s voice, clear and angelic, lifting toward the heavens. The sound wrapped around him, stirring memories of his own youth, of simpler days when faith had been unshaken.

A tear slipped down his cheek. “The Church is peace,” he whispered. “And I have strayed so far.”

Then, as if in answer to his grief, a familiar face appeared—Father Anselm, the man who had once tended his wounded body in Juliers. The sight of him was like a balm.

“Father,” he choked out, “you healed my flesh before. Can you now heal my soul?”

Anselm’s expression softened. He sat beside him, placing a gentle hand upon his brow. “Confess, my son. Unburden yourself.”

And so, with trembling voice, the stranger poured out his sins—his rage, his despair, his defiance of God. Anselm listened, his eyes filled with sorrow and understanding.

“Your sins are great,” he said at last. “But so too was your temptation. Rest now. I will speak with the prior.”

As Anselm left, the stranger clasped his hands together, his lips moving in silent prayer. For the first time in months, he felt the crushing weight begin to lift.

Penitence had come at last. And with it, the longing to remain within these walls—to turn his back on the world that had brought him only pain.

Beyond the convent, whispers spread of his death. The Princess Claelia, upon hearing the news, stood frozen, her face a mask of horror.

“So soon?” she breathed. Then her grief twisted into fury. “Then let his murderer follow him.”

And in the shadows of her palace, steel glinted as men waited, poised to strike when the traitor came to claim his reward.

Gerard, now a novice in the monastery, bore his penances with quiet resolve. Among them was the effort to receive Lodovico with kindness, though the man’s presence had once filled him with revulsion. Yet this act of discipline, begun as self-denial, soon bore unexpected fruit.

Lodovico, the assassin whose blade had been meant for Gerard’s heart, had instead saved him—and in doing so, found himself altered. There was a strange pride in his eyes whenever he looked upon the life he had spared, as if he could scarcely believe he had done such a thing. At first, it might have seemed almost comical, this newfound tenderness in a man of blood. But the heart, once opened to mercy, cannot easily close again. The sweetness of sparing a life turned Lodovico’s thoughts away from killing. Remorse crept in, and then something like repentance.

Teresa, ever watchful, seized the moment. She had threatened before to leave him if he did not abandon his dark trade, but now her words carried weight. The friars, too, saw the change in him and lent their guidance. Soon, Lodovico and Teresa were sent away—not in flight, but in hope. They boarded a ship bound for Leghorn, where Teresa had kin, and there the former assassin took up the humble work of a porter.

At first, he grumbled. The labor was dull, the days long. But dullness was better than the alternative—a swift and bloody end at the hands of Princess Claelia’s hired blades. His exile spared him that fate, though he never knew it.

The princess, robbed of her vengeance, fell into brooding silence. Her old confessor was dismissed; in his place, she sought one famed for piety—none other than Fra Jerome. When she poured out her grievances to him, expecting sympathy, she received none.

He listened, grim-faced, then shattered her illusions with the force of a thunderclap.

*"You are the true criminal,"* he told her, his voice like steel. *"You tempted a man to murder with your gold, and now, blind with pride, you thirst for more blood—as if those you hired were guiltier than you."*

She protested, haughty even in confession. *"I am not accustomed to such reproof from my spiritual guides."*

But Fra Jerome did not waver. He stripped her of every excuse, threatened her with divine wrath, and left her trembling at his feet.

*"Oh, holy father,"* she wept, *"help me save my soul!"*

*"Cast off your jewels,"* he commanded. *"Your silks and velvets. Go to Loretto in the garb of a beggar, barefoot but for sandals. Wash the feet of pilgrims, and confess your sin to the humblest friars you meet."*

She begged to keep her mask—her beauty was too well known. With a grunt, he relented, but only just. *"Cover your eyes, then. No more."*

When she reached for his hand in gratitude, he pulled away. *"This hand holds the Eucharist. It is not for the likes of you to touch."*

Broken, she whispered, *"At least bless me before you go."*

*"Ask for that blessing when you return from Loretto,"* he said, and left her there.

So it was that the strange events by the Tiber left their mark on all who had played a part. Lodovico, the killer turned laborer. The princess, humbled to penance. And Gerard—once dragged half-dead into the monastery, now regarded within its walls as something like a saint.

His probation was brief. Soon he took priestly vows, then the full mantle of a Dominican friar. With his new life came a new name: Brother Clement. The old Gerard was gone, buried with the world he had left behind.

And so the story of Gerard ended. The story of Brother Clement had begun.

In the humble home of a tailor in Rotterdam, a great man came quietly into the world this year—unnoticed, unheralded. Yet, at the same time, a mighty figure fell with thunderous ceremony. Philip, Duke of Burgundy, Earl of Holland, and lord of many lands, lay stricken in Bruges.

For the poor, sickness was a matter of nature’s whim—they suffered, they recovered, or they died, unseen. But for the wealthy, illness was a battleground where the physicians waged war, and often, the cure proved deadlier than the disease. The Duke’s ailment, unnamed then but known now as diphtheria, left him weak, his age compounding the danger. And so, Dr. Remedy, in his wisdom, drew blood.

The Duke grew colder. A dire sign.

Science, in its desperation, prescribed a remedy from the arcane: *“Flay an ape at once and press its flesh to his breast!”*

Officers of the court scattered in frantic search, but no ape could be found. Buffaloes, lizards, Turks, leopards—every beast but the one needed. *“There was an ape here before,”* muttered one. *“I could swear I saw it.”* Indeed, there had been—until the mastiff, enforcing mankind’s creed of *“Be of your time!”* had torn the creature apart for stealing its supper.

The seneschal’s desperate gaze fell upon an unlikely substitute. *“Here—this might serve. Is it not apelike enough, save for its clothes?”*

The chancellor scowled. *“The Princess Marie would have our heads. She dotes on this one.”*

The *“this”* in question was none other than our friend Giles, strutting obliviously in his cloth of gold.

With no ape to be had, Dr. Remedy turned to the next best thing. *“A dog, then—but it must be of one solid color.”*

A liver-colored hound was flayed, its still-warm hide pressed to the Duke’s chest.

And so, Philip the Good perished, slain by his physicians.

He left behind thirty-one children—only one legitimate, the rest scattered like leaves. Nineteen were provided for; the rest fended for themselves. The chronicles traced his noble blood back to the kings of Troy, through Thierry of Aquitaine, Chilperic, and Pharamond. But in truth, such lineage was no rarity—every prince of the age claimed descent from Troy. *“Priam”* was but the medieval word for *“Adam.”*

The Duke’s body was borne to Burgundy, laid to rest in a grand mausoleum of black marble in Dijon. Holland echoed with the news of his passing, unaware that within her borders, in that same year, a far greater light had been kindled—one that would outshine dukes and kings.

For who now remembers the house where Philip the Good died? Yet men still gaze upon the tailor’s dwelling in Rotterdam, where the true marvel of the age was born.

Dukes come and go, leaving the world unchanged. But once in centuries, a child enters the world like a torch lit by divine fire—and *“Let there be light!”* resounds across the earth.

The Dominicans, once the most formidable order in Christendom, now found their influence waning before the rising power of their rivals, the Franciscans. Even in England—a land both wealthy and devout—where they had once reigned supreme as the Black Friars, their dominance was crumbling.

Thus, the order’s shrewd leaders sought to cultivate zealous and capable men, sending them forth to reclaim lost ground. Among them was Clement, whose gifts—his mastery of languages, his eloquence, and his fervor—marked him as a promising instrument. He was to be dispatched to England, a land ripe for reconversion, to preach and correspond with Rome.

But Jerome, ever cautious, resisted this plan.

*"Clement,"* he argued, *"still carries the world’s milk in his veins—its passions, its frailties. Let not his newfound piety blind us to prudence. Test him first, temper him, lest we lean upon a reed that snaps beneath us."*

The prior assented. *"Then take him in hand yourself."*

And so Jerome did.

He led Clement to a field where young men gathered for sport—a place where Clement’s former companions might still linger. Soon enough, Pietro Vanucci and Andrea passed by, casting indifferent glances at the two friars. They did not recognize their old friend beneath the shaven crown and somber robes.

Clement’s breath caught, but he lowered his eyes and murmured a prayer.

*"Would you not speak to them?"* Jerome prodded.

*"Good,"* said Jerome, and his report to the prior was measured but approving.

Next, Jerome subjected Clement to sterner trials. He took him to the beds of the dying, to noisome dungeons where men lay coiled in filth and despair. Clement’s body recoiled, but his spirit held firm. He offered comfort, prayer, and hope where there had been none. Jerome noted his resolve but also his weakness.

*"His flesh is frail,"* Jerome admitted, *"but his will is strong."*

*"Then let it be strengthened further,"* replied the prior.

The greatest trial came unexpectedly.

One morning, a crowd gathered in a Roman street, necks craning toward a scaffold. A governor had been murdered in his bed—his throat slit, his hand severed in defense. The culprit, his own servant, was to be executed with brutal precision.

The condemned was first fastened to the wall of his victim’s house, his left hand struck off in grim symmetry. A freshly killed fowl was pressed to the stump—some physician’s futile superstition—before he was dragged to the scaffold. There, a young friar—Clement—clutched a crucifix and prayed fervently for his soul.

Then the executioner shoved Clement aside, felled the man with a mallet, and slit his throat.

The crowd roared in horror.

Clement fainted.

A towering friar swept him up like a child and carried him away.

Back at the convent, Clement confessed his failure with tears.

*"Courage,"* said the prior. *"A Dominican is not forged in a day. You shall face another trial—but not on an empty stomach."*

Soon, another criminal stood upon the scaffold—a thief and murderer, a man whose soul seemed beyond redemption. Clement spent the night with him in prison, praying, urging repentance. At dawn, he walked beside him to the gallows, holding the cross before his eyes. The man wept, embraced Clement, and died garroted. Then the executioner quartered his corpse with an axe.

The people, who had watched the living man die unmoved, now cried out at each blow to the lifeless flesh.

Clement shuddered but stood firm.

Then Jerome’s voice hissed in his ear:

Clement hesitated, his breath uneven, but he obeyed.

He climbed onto the cart and delivered his first sermon to a crowd. At first, his voice trembled; his hands shook. But as he spoke—of sin, of redemption, of the thief who had died beside Christ—his fear melted into fire. He held them, swayed them, made them weep.

When he finished, Jerome stared at him, astonished.

*"That was my first attempt,"* Clement said humbly. *"Your command took me by surprise. Did I—was I—?"*

*"You were born to this,"* Jerome admitted grudgingly. *"Give the glory to God."*

Yet Jerome remained wary. When a man named Julio Antonelli faced trial by ordeal—accused of sacrilege, his guilt uncertain—Clement watched as he was bound and lowered into the Tiber. If he floated, he was guilty; if he sank, innocent. The ropes grew heavy, and he sank—proclaimed innocent, but half-drowned.

*"Should he not be compensated?"* Clement asked afterward. *"For the terror, the pain?"*

*"He is rewarded enough in his acquittal,"* Jerome replied sternly. *"In this world, men suffer unjustly. But in the next, justice prevails."*

Clement bowed his head. *"May your wisdom take root in my heart."*

But the final test came in a debate with Fra Colonna, a scholar enamored of pagan learning. When Jerome dismissed his work as obscene, Colonna erupted—defending the ancients’ art, philosophy, and even their piety with a torrent of examples.

*"We invent nothing!"* he cried. *"Our rituals, our relics, even our miracles—they are echoes of older faiths. The heathens had their Palladium, we have our sacred images. They kissed statues, we kiss relics. Their gods bled, ours weep. Where is the difference?"*

Jerome scoffed. *"The difference is truth."*

*"Truth?"* Colonna laughed. *"Then why do we cling to fables even the pagans mocked?"*

Clement listened, torn between admiration and unease.

Jerome turned to him. *"What say you, brother? Does ancient wisdom outshine the light of Christ?"*

Clement hesitated, then spoke softly. *"Truth is one, though paths to it may differ. Let us take what is good—but never mistake the shadow for the substance."*

Jerome’s lips thinned, but he said nothing.

The trial had ended.

Clement had passed.

A Catherine in a strange house is both blessing and tempest. Her presence brightens the hearth, sets the windows gleaming, and breathes life into every corner—yet she is a force unstoppable, a whirlwind of order that spares neither chair nor soul.

When Margaret’s time drew near, Catherine marshaled her forces with quiet cunning. She turned her gaze upon Denys and Martin, those good-natured giants, and with a smile as sweet as it was treacherous, she suggested—nay, insisted—that a lengthy stroll might serve them well. "A very lengthy stroll," she added, eyes sharp as needles. "Perhaps into next week."

The men, slow to take a hint but quick to flee one, soon learned the art of vanishing at dawn and creeping home at dusk, tiptoeing like thieves beneath a tyrant’s roof. Even then, their mere presence seemed to grate upon Catherine’s nerves. Was it some primal instinct that bristled at the sight of warriors in a house of cradles? Whatever the cause, her tongue grew sharper than any sword, and before long, the kitchen stood emptied of its heroes.

Martin, too stiff for flight, had retreated only as far as the street, where he perched on a stool like a displaced monarch, sunning his grievances. Margaret, descending at last, found no Denys beside him.

"Gone?" she echoed, her violet eyes wide with hurt. "Without a word?"

Catherine scrubbed a bowl with sudden vigor. "He left while you slept. No sense in waking you."

A tear trembled on Margaret’s lashes. "Poor Denys."

Catherine’s conscience pricked her then. Without another word, she marched outside, softening her voice to honey. "Come in, Martin. The sun’s too harsh for your old bones."

But Martin, stubborn as oak, shook his head. "I’ve grown used to the air. No meddling out here."

Catherine’s sweetness curdled. "Oh, you cross old wretch!" she shrieked, flouncing back inside, where she soothed her ruffled spirit with the sovereign remedy of all maternal grievances—she washed the baby.

Margaret hovered, restless, dipping a tentative finger into the water—as if testing fate itself. Then her gaze caught on the child’s tiny hand. "Look!" she breathed. "His finger—just like *his*."

Catherine peered. A mole, no bigger than a freckle, marked the infant’s skin. Her breath caught. "Nature’s a meddler," she muttered. "Strong as sin and twice as sly. You never noticed it on *him*?"

Margaret shook her head, eyes shining. "But now I’ll kiss it every morn and night, till the one who bears its twin comes home to us." And she swept the babe into her arms, smothering him in kisses—a wolf devouring her lamb in love.

Catherine watched, her stern face softening. She had seen such fierce tenderness before.

So passed Margaret’s days—a quiet rhythm of motherhood, each moment stitched with joy. While far away, in the icy silence of a cloister, a man who had once been her Gerard moved like a ghost among the stones, hands folded, eyes downcast.

Two lives, two worlds: on one side, the towering Alps—harsh, sublime, piercing heaven. On the other, the gentle slopes of home, green and warm and humming with life. And between them? Only a page. Only the width of a single breath.

The new pope, favoring the Dominican order, summoned a capable friar to teach at the University of Basle. Clement, versed in languages and once a pupil of Guarini the younger, was chosen. His journey to England delayed, he set out for Basle, preaching along the way.

Barefoot, eyes lowered, his soul wrapped in contemplation, Clement walked a path marked by contrasts—love, despair, impiety, remorse, and now, resignation. The world that had once threatened him now bowed before his piety. Thieves pressed stolen coins into his hands, begging prayers. Yet this pilgrimage, though safe, was not without its trials—not of the body, but of the spirit.

On the first Sunday of his journey, he did what he had never dared before: he prayed for Margaret’s soul. For months, religious despair had gripped him, convinced his sins were beyond redemption. But now, a fragile peace settled upon him. He no longer felt unworthy to intercede for her. And so, daily, he whispered her name in his prayers, the last earthly thread binding him to the past.

The road brought encounters that tested his spirit. In one village, he found a young man convulsing from a tarantula’s bite—or perhaps the fear of one. The musicians’ frantic tunes only worsened his agony. Clement, recalling the healing power of music, plucked a psaltery and played until the man’s own whispered melody emerged. Learning the strange tune, Clement played it tirelessly until the sufferer slept, his life spared.

Elsewhere, he intervened in a dispute over a mulberry branch. An Englishman, ignorant of the tree’s sacred status in Italy, had plucked leaves to shield himself from the sun. The podesta, enraged, would have imprisoned him, but Clement brokered peace, explaining the foreigner’s ignorance. Grateful, the Kentish squire—Rolfe of Betshanger—offered boundless hospitality. "Come to England," he urged, pressing a purse into Clement’s hands. "We sit forty to dinner daily. What’s one more?"

Their exchange grew deeper. Rolfe confessed, lamenting his neglect of faith abroad. Clement, in turn, revealed his mission to England. "But how shall I learn your tongue?" he asked. Rolfe, ever practical, bartered his book of hours—Latin and English side by side—for Clement’s prayers. "Not a bargain," the friar corrected gently. "A gift."

Days later, Clement met two Germans laboring under a broken-down cart—a printing press in tow. At his call, "Sweynheim! Pannartz!" they fled, fearing witchcraft. But reason prevailed. They were Fust’s disciples, their craft the very wings of knowledge. Clement, once an enemy of the press, now blessed it. "Print no ill books," he warned. "They would fly like locusts, laying waste souls."

In Tuscany, a masked lady knelt to wash his feet, her penance for a crime—love twisted to murderous intent. She confessed in whispers: a youthful passion, rejection, a hired blade. "He never returned. I sent him to his grave."

Clement listened, then spoke softly. "The man you sought to kill lives."

She trembled. "Lives?"

"Gerard, the Hollander. He survived—not by your mercy, but by Heaven’s. He is now a friar, dead to the world you knew."

Recognition struck. She lifted her head, staring through the mask—then gasped. The penitent collapsed at his feet, her guilt and redemption crashing together in silence.

Thus Clement journeyed, his path crossing souls in need, each encounter a thread in the tapestry of his own redemption. The road to Basle was long, but every step carried him further from the man he had been, nearer to the servant he had vowed to become.

Clement sighed. Doubt crept into his heart—had he guided this tempestuous soul wisely? Yet his training had taught him patience; in moments of uncertainty, to wait, to listen, to trust that clarity would come. So he held his silence and prayed for wisdom. He moved only to withdraw his foot gently, but the penitent clutched it with a desperate cry, pressing her forehead against it as tears fell. Shame and remorse burned in her, flushing her throat crimson as she trembled before him.

“Daughter,” he said softly, “take heart. Torment yourself no more over Gerard, who is gone. I am Brother Clement, sent to you not for judgment, but to guide your soul toward peace.”

She sobbed. “Yes—yes—”

“Leave this pilgrimage. Return to Rome. True repentance is not found in distant trials, but in the humble battles of daily life—patience with those near us, kindness to those who rely on us. It is easy to kneel before strangers; harder to walk gently among our own.”

“I will never raise a hand against her again.”

“I speak not only of servants, but of all whose lives touch yours—kin, friends, even those who try you. Let this be your penance: each night, and each dawn after prayer, remember your sin and God’s mercy. Let humility soften your heart. The world bows to the rich; seek instead the quiet poor, those who suffer without complaint. Serve them, and you serve Heaven.”

“And must I marry?”

“You must. Within the year, wed a man who fears God. You are clay, daughter—shaped by the hands beside you. Bound to folly, you will falter; joined to grace, you may yet rise, a pious princess… or even a saint.”

Her eyes widened. “*Me?*”

“You.”

He bid her rise and begin her new path. She lifted herself to her knees, unmasking to meet his gaze—a look of mingled sorrow and resolve—before bowing her head. “I will obey you as I would an angel. Yet my heart aches, for I fear I will never see you again. Let me at least dry your feet.”

“It is needless.”

“It is no penance. Please—do not deny me this.”

He relented. As she tended to him, her voice trembled. “I will have Masses said for *her* soul. May I?”

The request startled him. A faint warmth touched his cheeks. “A kind thought,” he murmured. “Do as you will. Such deeds return to the doer like blessings. I am *your* confessor, not hers. Your soul is my charge now.” He leaned closer, urgency in his whisper. “Daughter, I see angels and demons warring for you this very hour. Fight—*fight*—on the side of light. Do you remember all I’ve said?”

“Every word,” she breathed. “They are carved into my heart.”

“One last counsel: pray much to Christ, and little to His saints.”

“I will.”

“Then we part here. You to your duty, I to mine.”

Her voice broke. “Farewell.”

“Farewell?” He straightened, sudden fervor brightening his gaze. “Christians do not say ‘forever.’ We part now, as earth parts from the sun—only to meet again in radiance. You and I divide for this life. And what is life? A breath in eternity. We are but lines in the great story of the Church, grains in the sand of time. Farewell—for the brief span called a lifetime! We part in sorrow; we will meet in joy. We part as mortals; we will rise as spirits. We part in shadow; we will stand together in the endless light, where Christ reigns, and all souls are made pure. There, in the blink of an eye, beyond time, we will meet again—you, and she, and I—amid saints and angels, in the presence of God Himself. *Forever. Forever. Forever.*”

And so they parted—he with eyes uplifted, aflame with holy fire; she, stepping back, casting one last lingering glance at the figure who had stirred her soul. She left subdued, yet strangely at peace.

Clement journeyed to Basel, where he taught, preached, and immersed himself in study and devotion. His life became a testament to discipline—his mind fixed on scripture, his flesh subdued by fasting. Those around him began to whisper: here walked a man touched by grace, a descendant of the martyrs.

But in quiet moments, his thoughts strayed to the princess, and to the souls he had left behind—wondering, hoping, praying that his words had taken root. For in the end, all paths led upward. All separations were fleeting.

And all endings were but pauses in the eternal.

Little Gerard was nearly three months old when a messenger arrived from Tergou, breathless and urgent, summoning Catherine home.

"Go back and tell them I won't come," Catherine said sharply. "They've got Kate."

The messenger left, and she turned back to Margaret, sighing. "Well, child, I suppose I must go. They’re all in chaos—this is the third time they’ve sent for me. If I don’t go now, my man will drag me back by the ear tomorrow."

She spent the rest of the day instructing Margaret on every possible ailment an infant might suffer, pressing coins into her hand despite Margaret’s refusals.

"Come now," Catherine said, growing impatient. "Speak your heart. We’re parting, and who knows if we’ll see each other again?"

"Mother, don’t say such things!"

"Ah, girl, I’ve lived long enough to know how often partings are forever. When I was your age, I thought we’d all live eternally. Now, out with it—what troubles you?"

Margaret hesitated, then confessed. "Your Cornelis said to me, *‘So you’ve come to share with us, eh?’* I told him I’d sooner starve than take what’s begrudged."

Catherine scoffed. "Bother his spiteful tongue! What does it matter? He’d never know."

"But *I* would know," Margaret said firmly. "Unless my child were starving, I’ll take no charity laced with scorn. Your love is enough—I treasure that more than silver."

Catherine relented, though not without grumbling. But when the moment of parting came, both women clung to each other, tears spilling freely.

After Catherine left, the child wailed inconsolably. Suspecting a pin, Margaret searched his clothes—only to find a gold angel pressed against his back.

"So that’s why she gave in so quick," Margaret murmured, wrapping the coin in linen and tucking it away. "We’ll not touch it unless we must, little one."

Days passed, then weeks. Gerard did not return. No word came. Margaret’s heart grew heavy, her mind clouded with uneasy thoughts. Her savings dwindled, and soon the cupboard stood bare.

With a sigh, she retrieved Catherine’s gold piece—only for fate to intervene. A Frenchman, sent by his princess to collect fine illuminations, admired her work and paid handsomely. Margaret knelt by the cradle, showering her son with kisses and gold, whispering prayers of gratitude.

But fortune was fickle. Martin, her faithful lodger, fell ill and died. The cost of his burial drained her purse. Now his chair sat empty by the hearth, and Margaret could not bear to see another fill it.

She needed help—someone to carry her laundry. A young cooper named Luke Peterson offered his services, blushing as he spoke.

"I’ll carry your basket before or after my work," he said earnestly. "No need for payment—we’re neighbors."

Margaret hesitated. "At least let me give you supper when you bring the linen home."

Luke agreed, his cheeks flushing deeper.

For a time, life steadied. But Gerard’s silence gnawed at her. Each day without word twisted her heart tighter, until she cried aloud, "Would that I knew the worst!"

Then came a heavy footstep on the stair—unfamiliar, ominous. The door opened, and Eli stood there, grave-faced.

"The skipper has returned," he said.

Margaret’s breath caught. "Is he alive?"

"He must be. No one has seen him dead."

"But has anyone seen him alive?"

Eli shook his head. "Not in Rome. Kate thinks he’s gone elsewhere."

Margaret’s hands trembled as she prepared a meal for him, her mind adrift in dread. Before leaving, Eli laid a hand on her head.

"Whether he lives or not, you are my daughter now. If there’s aught I can do..."

"Pray for him," she whispered. "And for me."

When the door closed behind him, Margaret sank beside the cradle, clutching her son.

"Oh, my poor fatherless boy," she wept. "My fatherless boy!"

Not long after, as the family in Tergou gathered for their midday meal, Luke Peterson burst into the room, dust clinging to his clothes. “Good folk! Mistress Catherine is needed at once in Rotterdam.”

“*I* am Catherine,” said the elder woman, while Kate added softly, “She must mean Margaret.”

“Aye, mistress,” Luke nodded. “She said to me, ‘Good Luke, ride swift to Tergou, find Eli the hosier, and beg his wife Catherine to come to me, for the love of God.’ I didn’t wait for dawn.”

Catherine’s hands flew to her cheeks. “Holy saints! Has he returned, Kate? No—she would have said so. What could it be?” Her mind raced with possibilities.

“Perhaps the lad knows more,” Kate ventured timidly.

“Aye, that I do,” said Luke. “The babe is failing. And she’s wrapped her heart around it.”

Catherine stood so quickly her chair scraped the floor. “What ails the child?”

“I cannot say. But it’s been wasting away these past weeks.”

A sly glance passed between Cornelis and Sybrandt, unnoticed by Catherine, whose fierce gaze was fixed on her husband. “Now, Eli,” she warned, voice sharp, “if you dare breathe a word against my going, we’ll quarrel after all these years.”

“Who’s stopping you, foolish woman? Argue with the wind if you must, while I fetch Peter’s mule.”

“Bless you!” she cried, relief softening her face. “You’ve never failed me when it mattered.” Then, to the others: “Eat if you can—I must ready myself.”

She took Luke with her in the cart, plying him with questions—sometimes coaxing, sometimes stern—until she had wrung every scrap of knowledge from him.

Margaret met her at the door, pale and trembling, and flung her arms around Catherine’s neck, her eyes desperate.

“He lives, thank God,” Catherine said after studying her face. She examined the frail child, then the hollow-eyed mother. “You were right to send for me. The babe is poisoned.”

“Poisoned? By whom?”

“By *you*. You’ve been fretting.”

“I *must* fret! How can I not?”

“Nonsense, girl. A nursing mother has no right to sorrow. She must turn her mind from grief to the blessing in her arms. Do you not know a child fades when its mother grieves? This is what comes of your books and letters—fit for men, but they steal a woman’s sense. The child must be weaned.”

Margaret’s face twisted in fury. “Cruel! I regret sending for you. Would you rob me of my last comfort? Nursing him, I forget I am the most wretched soul alive.”

“Yet you do *not* forget,” Catherine retorted, “or he would not be as he is.”

“*Mother!*” Margaret pleaded.

Catherine relented slightly. “It’s hard, I know. But think—would it not be harder to see his sweet face in a coffin?”

Margaret gasped as if struck.

“And how will you bear your other sorrows with your heart full and your arms empty?”

Margaret crumpled. “I’ll do anything. Only save him.”

“Good lass. Trust me—I see clearer than you.”

But the babe himself proved stubborn. Margaret, torn between pride and misery, said, “See? He loves me too well to leave me.”

Catherine was ready. On her journey, she had noticed a healthy young mother nursing a child outside her home. She went to her now, explained the need, and asked if she would nurse the failing infant until he could be weaned. The woman agreed with a smile, setting her own child aside and following Catherine inside.

With a quick curtsey, she took little Gerard into her arms, murmuring softly, examining him with gentle pity before settling to nurse him as if he were her own.

Margaret, frozen in shock, stared—then burst into ragged sobs.

The nurse looked up, startled. “What’s amiss? Wife, you said the mother was willing.”

“She *is* willing—just a fool. Pay her no heed.” Catherine turned on Margaret. “For shame! Here’s a woman leaving her own to save yours, and you weep in her face?”

Margaret pressed her hands to her mouth. “I *hate* her,” she whispered fiercely.

Catherine laughed. “Better said aloud than stewed in silence. Here—hate *this* if you can.” She placed the child in Margaret’s lap.

Margaret turned her face away. “He’s not mine now. She should have taken him—why leave him here? Oh!” She wept bitterly.

Catherine let her cry, offering no empty comfort.

When Joan’s aid was needed, Catherine took Gerard outside for fresh air. Margaret never protested—but upon their return, her cheeks were always wet.

The arrangement was brief. Soon, Margaret was as eager as Catherine to wean him. His health returned—only for teeth to trouble him next. But here, Catherine’s wisdom shone. Amidst her grief for Gerard, Margaret found moments of joy as each tiny tooth emerged.

“Teeth?” she marveled. “No—pearls. Each one a wonder.”

Catherine, too, was caught in the illusion. Had someone called a field of wheat as fine, they’d have been scorned from the house. Each new tooth was hailed with triumph.

Through Joan, Catherine learned that her husband, Jorian, had once served Ghysbrecht Van Swieten but fallen from favor. Now sexton and carpenter, he scraped by. In turn, Catherine shared Margaret’s plight and Gerard’s silence.

Joan sighed. “Trouble fills the world.”

One day, she hinted, “My man knows more of Gerard than he says. But he’s close as a clam. Come at sunset—draw him out. Only, for pity’s sake, don’t say I sent you. The one beating he ever gave me was for babbling his affairs.”

Catherine visited. Jorian gladly boasted of aiding Gerard—old news to her. But when pressed on why Gerard neither returned nor wrote, he shot his wife a dark look (met with blank innocence) and clammed up.

“What should I know?” he grunted. “Think the burgomaster shares secrets with the likes of me?”

“Then Ghysbrecht knows something?” Catherine pounced.

“Likely. Who else would?”

“I’ll ask him.”

“Do. Tell him *I* said so,” Jorian sneered. “Go make an enemy for me. A poor man gets naught but trouble from women’s tongues.” He withdrew, prickly as a hedgehog.

His evasion tormented them. Ghysbrecht had been Gerard’s foe—stopping his marriage, imprisoning him, hunting him. And now his former servant hinted the burgomaster held the key to Gerard’s silence.

Catherine urged Margaret to try. “You’re young, fair—perhaps he’ll speak to you.”

Margaret, child in arms, knocked timidly at Jorian’s door at sunset.

“Come in,” he called.

She entered. He took one look, snorted, and stormed out.

“Is this for me?” Margaret asked Joan, flushing.

Joan’s tone cooled. “Forgive him. He blames you for his poverty. Something about a missing parchment—he got nothing from Ghysbrecht because of it.”

Margaret paled. “Gerard took it.”

“Likely. But Jorian says you swore to keep them safe. I don’t fault his anger—so near fortune, only to lose it through those he helped.” She softened. “Still, I tell him: folk like us, free of trouble, shouldn’t judge those deep in it. Must you go?”

“I won’t stay where I’m unwelcome.”

Joan kissed the bairn. “He’s blameless, poor lamb.”

Stung, Margaret resolved to act—but kept even Catherine in the dark.

When Catherine returned to Tergou after six weeks, Margaret marked the child’s birthday by dressing in her finest, carrying her son to church, and praying long and fervently for Gerard’s return.

That same hour, far off in Basle, Father Clement offered mass for Margaret’s departed soul.

A lie is not like a blow from a blade—clean, sharp, its damage done and seen. No, a lie is a seed, sown in darkness, sprouting unseen, its roots twisting deep, its branches spreading wide beyond any man’s reckoning. A single falsehood, spoken in malice or greed, may topple lives like a storm felling trees.

So it was with the lie that severed Gerard from Margaret.

The brothers, Cornelis and Sybrandt, had whispered their deceit to spare their inheritance one claimant. Yet what had they gained? Little Gerard, the son they had sought to disown, now filled their mother’s heart. Worse still, Eli—seeing no hope of his lost son’s return—had named the boy his heir, securing Margaret’s place among them. The brothers exchanged a look of bitter defeat. Cornelis nearly spoke, nearly confessed that Gerard might yet live—but their mother’s sharp eye silenced him.

Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, the other architect of this ruin, was a man crumbling under the weight of his own sins. Age had gnawed at his body, but conscience gnawed fiercer at his soul. He had bargained with himself—kept the land, the gold, but delivered Gerard’s letter to Margaret, as if that small mercy might balance the scales.
