Yet when her child was born, his treachery took a darker hue. He had wronged the daughter of a friend, the granddaughter of another, and now her shame would be his doing.

These thoughts haunted him, sharp as specters, until one day he found her standing in his hall, veiled, a child in her arms. She let the veil fall, and her eyes struck him like a blade.

He flinched. *“For aught I know,”* he stammered. *“I hope he lives—for your sake.”*

She seized his shoulder. *“You know why he does not come. You know why he sends no word. Tell me!”*

He denied, he faltered, but she pressed him, relentless. Then she knelt, holding the child up to him—this boy, Peter’s grandchild, Floris’s blood. *“Look at him,”* she pleaded. *“Look at this face, innocent as yours once was. Have you no memory left?”*

The old man recoiled as if burned. *“Avaunt!”* he shrieked, tearing at his hair. *“Must I see the dead in every face? Hear their voices in every word? I am in hell already!”* His legs gave way; servants bore him off, a broken thing.

Margaret left, sick with pity and dread. *“I have killed him,”* she whispered. Yet the mystery remained, darker than before.

Money grew scarce again. Then, one day, silver appeared on her table—a scrawl beside it: *For Gerard his wife.* Her heart leaped. *“He lives! He sends this!”* She kissed the note, tucked it into her bosom.

Months passed. The coins came, left by unseen hands—on the table, in a basket, once tossed through the window. No one claimed the deed—not Catherine, not Eli, not Margaret Van Eyck. And with each gift, she whispered to her boy, *“We are wading in deep waters, my love.”*

She dressed him well, as if Gerard himself might see. The neighbors sneered. Let them.

Then came a change in Peter Brandt. His clouded mind cleared, sudden as a break in storm-wracked skies. He spoke of Gerard—wondered if he languished in prison, or had been pressed into war, or sent to some distant scriptorium. *“Or taken vows,”* he murmured. *“He had that turn of mind.”*

Margaret bit her lip. *“Father, you deceive yourself, as I have done.”*

But then—his eyes widened. He sat bolt upright, crying, *“I see him! In a boat—on a great river—coming this way!”* His gaze pierced the walls, seeing what none else could. Then, just as sudden, the light left him. *“Gone. All dark.”*

He sank back, murmuring blessings, whispering of love. Then, like a torch guttering its last bright flare before the dark, he was gone.

And Margaret knelt, clutching her child, alone in the gathering shadows.

Margaret’s grief was not the kind that could be soothed with hollow comforts. The loss of her father struck deeper than any wound she had yet borne. Catherine, ever practical, tried to console her with reason—how death had been a mercy, how the burden of care was lighter now. But Margaret only turned hollow eyes upon her and whispered, *"Such cares were all the joy I had."*

The empty chair by the hearth seemed to mock her. She had loved the old man not as a duty, but as something tender and fragile—a scholar reduced to a child’s helplessness, dependent on her hands for bread, her voice for comfort. Now the silence where his voice had been was unbearable.

Catherine, chastened, fell quiet. At last, she admitted, *"I spoke from my head, not my heart. If I were you, I’d weep just the same."* That small honesty, that rare moment of true kinship, brought Margaret the first flicker of solace.

But Catherine’s sympathy was fleeting. Soon enough, she turned to her usual meddling. *"You were never meant to live alone,"* she declared. *"Gerard is gone—dead or faithless, who can say? But here’s Luke Peterson, honest as daylight, and sweet on you besides. Why waste your youth waiting for a ghost?"*

Margaret stared, bewildered. *"Luke? Our basket-boy?"*

*"Basket-boy? He’s near twenty, and a steady hand! Never a drunkard, unlike half the louts in Rotterdam. I’ve asked about."*

A faint, weary smile touched Margaret’s lips. *"He only comes for the puddings."*

Catherine scoffed. *"Aye, and for the cook who makes them. I’ve known his mind a year now."*

Margaret’s cheeks flushed. *"If he dares think of me while Gerard—"* She bit her lip. *"No. I’ll not believe him dead till I see his grave, nor faithless till I see him wed to another."*

Luke, poor fool, walked into the storm unwittingly. Where once he had been welcomed with warmth, he now met only frost. Confused, he stammered, *"Have I done something to displease you?"*

*"Nothing,"* Margaret said curtly. *"But keep away awhile."*

Catherine, ever the blunderer, confessed her interference. Luke blinked. *"You told her? But—she must’ve known! I’ve been courting her every Saturday night!"*

*"Courting?"* Margaret’s voice sharpened. *"Is that how Rotterdam men do it? Sitting at a woman’s table and calling it love?"*

*"I like your puddings,"* Luke admitted, *"but I like you more."*

*"Then you’ve supped your last here,"* she said coldly. *"I’ll carry my own basket."*

Luke slunk away, wounded. But by the next Saturday, he was back—huddled on the doorstep, red-eyed. *"I don’t know where else to go,"* he muttered.

Catherine relayed his misery. Margaret sighed. *"Let him in, then. But only if he remembers I’m another man’s wife."*

Luke hesitated at the terms. Catherine rolled her eyes. *"Promises are pie-crust, boy. Make them, break them—just get inside first. Humor her now, and if Gerard never comes… well, time wears down the loneliest heart."*

Brother Clement had spent more than a year teaching and preaching in Basel when Jerome appeared before him one day, dust-covered yet triumphant.

"Give glory to God, Brother Clement," Jerome declared. "The time has come—you may now join me on our journey to England."

"I am ready," Clement replied. "Expecting your arrival these many months, I have devoted my spare hours to mastering the English tongue."

"A wise preparation," Jerome acknowledged. He explained that his delay had been purposeful—he had sought and secured extraordinary authority from the Pope to gather alms for the Church in England and to hear confessions even within secular monasteries. "Now let us gird ourselves and go forth to strike a blow for Holy Church—and against the Franciscans."

Their path led them down the Rhine, preaching as they went. In larger towns, both spoke; in smaller villages, they often parted ways, each taking a side of the river before reuniting at an appointed place.

Their styles differed. Jerome’s oratory was lofty, commanding, though narrower in scope, his delivery steady as a deep river. Clement’s, by contrast, was a shifting stream—now gentle, now fierce, now intimate, now soaring. Where Jerome stood firm as an oak, Clement bent like a reed to the hearts of his listeners, then rose like a flame when the spirit moved him. He could thunder like a storm, blaze like lightning, and sweep all before him in a torrent of eloquence.

(To recount his words here would be a task both difficult and unnecessary—others have captured such fire better than I. Yet know this: Clement had a gift Jerome lacked—he listened before he spoke. He learned the hidden sins of each town, the wounds beneath its surface, and shaped his sermons to strike true.)

In one city, they shared a pulpit. Jerome preached against vanity in dress, his voice sharp with satire. The people listened, unmoved—for vanity was not their vice.

Then Clement spoke. He spoke of drunkenness.

It was the sin they cherished, the sin their clergy ignored, the sin that birthed theft, violence, even murder. He reminded them of a drunken parricide, of men who had roasted a comrade alive. He spoke of their late prince, dead not of apoplexy but of wine; of an alderman drowned in a puddle of his own making.

"Your children shiver in rags," he cried, "while you drown your souls and your coin in drink! You become beasts—foolish as calves, savage as boars. How long before your wives and daughters follow? In Bohemia, they do already. Would you see the mothers who bore you, the wives you vowed to cherish, sunk as low as yourselves?"

A roar of horror rose from the crowd.

"Ah, it wounds you to imagine it!" Clement thundered. "Yet think—how much more must it wound the pure eyes of God to see His image in man debased to a swinish thing?"

He painted two pictures then: the drunkard’s home, squalid and weeping; the sober man’s, bright with peace. The women wept and murmured, "True, true!"

The sermon stirred an uproar. Men grumbled; women feigned defiance, praising their husbands’ "right to a drop." Yet that very evening, a hundred of those same women sought Clement, pressing gifts into his hands, kissing his robe, blessing him for daring to speak against their "sots."

Jerome frowned. "Drunkenness is no heresy, Brother. Must a whole sermon be wasted on it?"

Yet as they traveled, Jerome could not deny the truth: Clement’s words sank deeper than his own. They made men think, weep, even change.

"It is some trick of northern speech," Jerome told himself, uneasy. "He lowers himself to their level." Yet at times, Clement’s words rose higher than Jerome ever could. The contradiction nettled him—until he chided himself for petty pride.

"Souls are as varied as locks," he conceded. "And preachers must be as varied as keys. This novice, it seems, holds the key to these northern hearts."

So they journeyed on, sometimes drifting downstream, more often walking, preaching, confessing, until at last they reached Düsseldorf.

There stood the little quay where Gerard and Denys had once boarded a boat up the Rhine. The friars stepped onto the stones. The streets were unchanged, the Silver Lion still stood—yet the man who walked through them now was not the same. Barefoot, hands folded, eyes cast down, his heart was cool as stone. A true son of Dominic. A servant of Holy Church.

Catherine fixed her husband with a steady gaze. “Eli,” she said, “answer me one question like a man, and I’ll trouble you no more today. What is wormwood?”

Eli hesitated, caught off guard by the sudden demand, but soon rallied. “Something bitter as gall,” he replied.

“A fair answer,” Catherine conceded, “but not the one I seek.”

“Then answer it yourself,” Eli grumbled.

“So I shall,” she said. “Wormwood is—to have two idle hands in the house, wearing out thy shoes and mine while they wait for us to die.”

Eli groaned. The barb struck true.

“Methinks waiting for their own father’s grave, with naught to do but brood, is enough to sour better men than those two. Why not set them up in trade? Give them a chance to mend.”

Eli frowned. “And risk them drinking and gaming away even the shelves beneath their wares?”

“Do you take me for a fool?” Catherine retorted. “I mean to watch them sharp at first, then slacken the rein—as the saying goes.”

“Where would you place them? Not here, to steal our own custom.”

“Rotterdam,” said Catherine firmly. “There’s trade enough for all. And I could see them settled.”

*Ah, self-deception!* The truth, unspoken, was that Rotterdam lay nearer to Gerard.

After much debate—and many hollow promises of reform from Cornelis and Sybrandt—Catherine set out for Rotterdam with Kate in tow, scouring the streets for a suitable shop. They found one, well-situated but in sore need of repair. The disarray worked in their favor, lowering the price, and soon they were scrubbing, polishing, and fitting shelves, transforming the neglected space into a respectable trade-house and dwelling.

Meanwhile, Luke Peterson, ever eager to serve Margaret, would ask, “What can I do for you?”

Her answer was always the same, weary and soft: “Nothing, Luke. Nothing.”

“Surely,” he persisted, “a woman of your wit can think of some task for me?”

“Nothing, Luke. Nothing.”

But at last, she relented. “If you could make something to ease my sweet Kate’s burdens…”

Luke, ever the willing servant, set to work with joy. Soon he presented Kate with a small, cushioned cart, sturdy yet light. At dusk, he would harness himself to it and draw her along the boulevard, Margaret and Catherine walking beside. To passersby, they might have seemed a merry party—but the truth was quieter, sadder.

Kate cradled little Gerard in her lap, her thin face alight with tenderness. The sight—a babe like a rosebud held in the cup of a fading lily—drew jeers from the coarse-minded.

“How’s the taste of thistles, Luke?” they called. “Can your mistress not afford a donkey?”

Luke paid them no heed, but Kate flinched for him.

“You’ve done enough, good Luke,” she said softly. “The cart was kindness itself. But I’ll not have you mocked for drawing me. Let us hire some poor soul to do it.”

Luke shook his head. “Their words are wind. If they vexed me, I’d crack their skulls together. I’ll pull you till my mistress bids me stop.”

“Luke,” Margaret said gently, “if you would please me, obey Kate in this.”

“Then I obey,” he said at once.

Not all scorned him. Joan, Jorian Ketel’s wife, spoke kindly. “Strength spent on the weak is strength well spent. And her prayers will bless you—she has not long in this world. See how she smiles through pain?”

But Luke, simple and true, answered, “I want no prayers. I do it for my mistress—for Margaret.”

Still, he pressed her for tasks, hungry to serve. And one day, worn by his persistence, she turned on him with a look and tone that no words could capture.

“Find me my boy’s father,” she said.

At this, Margaret’s tears fell freely. Luke whimpered in sympathy—a man’s frame housing a girl’s tender heart.

*"Prithee, weep not so, sweet mistress,"* he pleaded. *"I’d bring him back to life if I could, rather than see thee grieve so sore."*

Margaret wiped her eyes, her sorrow laced with bitterness. *"I weep not for his death, but for their doubled tongues."* Then, steadying herself, she laid a hand upon his shoulder and spoke with solemn certainty. *"Luke, he is not dead. The dying see clearer than the living. Listen—my father, in his last hour, when I, fool that I was, thought him healing, sat in that very chair and cried, ‘I see him! I see him!’—not like a failing man, but aflame. ‘Sore disfigured—on a great river—coming this way.’"*

She clutched Luke’s arm. *"If you had a woman’s heart, you’d pity me and find him. The father of my child!"*

*"Alack, I would if I knew how,"* Luke sighed. *"But how can I?"*

Luke pondered long, his mind slow but earnest. *"A great river… The Maas is wide, but had he sailed it, he’d be here by now. The Rhine, then—greater still, and long."*

*"Aye,"* Margaret agreed, hope flickering. *"Denys bade him come down the Rhine. Yet what if he turns aside before reaching home? What if he pines not for me as I for him?"* She searched Luke’s face, desperate for denial.

Instead, he said bluntly, *"It looks very like it. What a fool he must be!"*

*"What do we know?"* she protested.

Luke frowned. *"Let me think. My mind does not gallop."* At last, he struck upon a plan: he would ride to a station sixty miles up the Rhine, where all boats docked, and there intercept Gerard. Yet knowing neither face nor form, he would need proof.

*"Give me a letter for him,"* he urged. *"Else he may think me a liar."*

Margaret flushed. *"Good, kind Luke! How I’ve wronged thee!"* By supper, she pressed into his hands a long letter, bound with silk and sealed.

Luke weighed it, then spoke gravely. *"Say your father’s vision was true. Say I find this man, and he proves worthier than I think him—and comes home to thee. What then becomes of me?"*

Margaret’s cheeks burned. *"Heaven will reward thee. I shall kneel and bless thee all my days, love thee as a mother loves her son. I am aged by sorrow—thou shouldst not go. ’Tis selfish."*

*"Humph!"* Luke grunted. *"And if he reads thy letter, hears how thou pinest—and yet, traitor or fool, turns not home—what then? Must I die unwed, and thou lonely to thy grave?"*

Margaret trembled. *"If—God forbid!—he refuses… then he will return my marriage lines. I shall be honest, yet wretched. My boy no bastard. And then…"* She faltered. *"I could enter no man’s house but thine."*

*"Ay, but will you?"* Luke pressed.

*"Ask me not! Perhaps… when my wounds cease bleeding. Or else—"* Her voice broke. *"The Maas may take me. Luke, do not go! ’Tis madness!"*

But Luke, slow to conceive a thought, was slower to abandon one. At dawn, he departed, Margaret’s last gold angel in his purse.

Twelve hours later, two friars entered Holland from the south. Jerome, unable to preach in the Low Dutch tongue, pressed toward the coast, eager for England. Fate, however, wove another thread.

In a riverside village, Clement preached—and noticed a grey nun weeping.

*"Not for myself,"* she confessed, *"but for my lost sister. Lured from her vows, she now lives in sin at an inn, a decoy for men’s lusts. We dare not seize her—the baron’s servant owns the place, and would burn our convent."*

*"Souls are not saved by force,"* Clement mused. He persuaded Jerome to lodge at the convent while he attempted redemption.

Disguised as a horseman, Clement entered the inn, heart quaking but resolve steeled. He lured the fallen nun, Mary, with gold, then revealed himself—a Dominican crucifix in hand.

*"Dare you rob the Virgin?"* she had cried, recoiling.

*"You are no robber,"* he countered, *"or you would not care. Mary, your saint sends me!"*

Terror and remorse seized her. He spoke of the convent’s grief, of Ursula’s tears, of angels weeping. *"Sin is not sweet, nor holiness bitter. Eternity knocks."*

Trembling, she yielded. *"I would return—but how face them?"*

He bade her trample her jewels—*"the wages of sin"*—then led her forth. The landlord barred their path, but Clement, in righteous fury, hurled him aside. *"Touch her, and your soul shall roast!"*

Mary faltered at the convent gate, but Ursula flung it open, weeping. The abbess, though stern, embraced her. Clement, his task done, strode toward the Rhine—and toward the boy who waited with Margaret’s letter.

The letter was tender, aching—a woman’s plea to one who had left her in silence. She spoke of their son, his tiny hands, the mole on his finger. *"Know you any with such a mark? The mothers envy me—but I envy wives, for thou comest not."*

Some claimed him dead. *"But if thou wert dead, how could I live?"* Others called him false. *"This I’ll believe from no lips but thine."* She recalled her father’s dying vision—Gerard on a river, disfigured. *"Is it so? Hast thou lost a limb? Then thou need’st me more, not less."*

No reproach marred her words—only love, raw and unyielding.

Luke, bearing this letter, mingled with boatmen, seeking Gerard Eliassoen. At last, a black friar eyed him sharply.

Luke’s heart cracked. *"Why did the Lord make women?"* he muttered, dashing the letter down—then snatching it up again. He begged labor from the innkeeper, not for coin, but to keep his hands busy and his heart from breaking.

And so he waited, coopering barrels in fury, as the boats drifted down the Rhine.

A letter brimming with earnest longing seldom leaves the heart unchanged. Margaret poured hers out—her fears, her faith in her dying father’s vision, or perhaps his illusion. Yet once the words were sent and Luke departed, doubt crept in. Had she been too credulous? Guilt pricked at her conscience over Luke, and Catherine’s scolding only deepened her despondency.

Then came Joan Ketel, radiant with an idea. *"Margaret, there’s a cure for your sorrow—the hermit of Gouda, a holy man who sees what others cannot. When the mood takes him, he speaks truth."*

*"I’ve heard of him,"* Margaret murmured, hopeless.

With gentle persistence, Joan convinced her to walk to Gouda, basket in hand—butter and eggs for the hermit. Their friendship had been forged in hardship. Six weeks prior, Joan’s husband, Jorian, had fallen ill, wracked with pain. The physician’s remedies failed, and despair set in—until Margaret arrived.

*"Open the window,"* she had ordered. *"Change his linen."*

*"Why trouble for a dying man?"* Joan protested.

Margaret returned with an apron full of flowering feverfew, brewed it, and coaxed Jorian back from death’s edge. *"Never speak of this,"* she warned. *"They’d lock me away from my child."*

Now, standing before the hermit’s cave, Margaret pressed her lips to the aperture. *"Holy hermit, I bring gifts—butter and eggs. And I, a deserted wife, a mother with a babe, beg you—tell me if he lives, if he is true or false."*

A faint voice answered: *"Trouble me not with earthly things. Send a friar—I am dying."*

Margaret’s heart sank. *"Is it so? Then let us in to help you!"*

They left, heavy-hearted. Joan grumbled, *"Are we demons, to be barred from a deathbed?"* But Margaret walked in silence, her sorrow too deep for words.

Joan tried to console her. *"Jorian will speak soon—he’s near bursting with it. He’s grateful to you, truly."*

*"Seeing is believing,"* Margaret replied bitterly.

*"He says you saved him with feverfew when grander medicines failed. ‘A man of my size, cured by a weed!’ But he knows the truth. And he’s noticed Gerard’s kin circling you—friends, or something else."*

*"What did he say?"* Margaret pressed.

Margaret shook her head. *"We are in deep waters, my child and I."*

Saturday night came—no Luke. *"Poor Luke,"* Margaret sighed. *"He went on such a thankless errand."*

*"He’s one in a hundred,"* Catherine said warmly. *"If he returns, will you be kinder to him?"*

*"Yes,"* Margaret whispered.

---

Brother Clement, guided by the nuns, avoided the river’s bend and strode ahead, reaching a station miles nearer the coast than where Luke waited for Gerard Eliassoen. The next morning, he set off early, arriving in Rotterdam by noon.

He hurried to the port, expecting Jerome. Instead, he found monks of his order—but no Jerome.

*"Has Jerome arrived?"* he asked.

*"Oh yes,"* one replied, pointing to a ship already under sail.

Clement’s heart lurched. *"Gone without me? Jerome! Jerome!"*

The monks turned, startled. *"You must be Brother Clement,"* one said, greeting him warmly. They handed him a letter from Jerome—a cold, dismissive scrawl.

*"A ship was leaving for England. I could not delay. Follow if you wish, but you’d do better staying here. Preach to your own. You lack the steel for those islanders. And beware—I met one who asked after you by your old name. Guard yourself. The world’s snares are subtle. Remember: Solitude, Fasting, and Prayer are your weapons. Farewell."*

Clement stood stricken. Was there some flaw in him that repelled even Jerome? Or was Jerome himself devoid of Christian love?

*"Worldly hearts are no colder than this,"* he murmured. *"The only one who ever truly loved me lies in a grave."*

He wandered to Sevenbergen, passing Peter’s cottage—now repaired, children playing at the door. He turned away, unwilling to disturb the memory.

In the churchyard, he searched for Margaret’s grave. It was not there.

*"Poverty is cruel,"* he whispered. *"No stone to mark her rest."*

Inside the church, he kissed the steps and prayed for her soul. Outside, he met an old man.

*"Margaret? Oh, her! She left long ago. Gone to Rotterdam, or was it Amsterdam? And now dead? Ah, the young go first, while we old weeds linger."*

Clement sighed. *"God bless you, my son."*

He returned to the convent, where he prayed for Jerome and Margaret deep into the night, his soul seeking a cold, quiet peace.

The following Sunday, after Mass, the house of Catherine in the Hoog Straet hummed with activity. The shop stood ready, its sign freshly painted—a white lamb suckling a gilt sheep—bearing the names of Cornelis and Sybrandt, who were to open their venture the next day. Eli had arrived, bringing additional wares from his own stores to ensure their sons began on prosperous footing. Pride warmed the parents’ hearts as they watched their boys stroll the garden, murmuring vows to forsake their idle past for the steadier rewards of trade. Catherine, ever sharp-eared, caught their words through an open window and relayed them to Eli with quiet triumph.

That evening, a celebratory supper was to unite the family—Margaret and her little one included—toasting the new enterprise with advice from the elders and wine to bless their conversion from waywardness to commerce. Margaret, seeking respite from her lingering melancholy, had come early to assist Catherine. Both women’s cheeks glowed from the heat of the hearth and their labors.

Joan arrived bearing a salad from Jorian’s garden. "He picked it for you, Margaret," she said with a playful frown. "You’re all his chatter now—I’ll grow jealous! When I told him of today’s feast, he near stripped his plot bare. Oh, but you should’ve heard the sermon at the new kirk! Never have I heard such preaching."

Margaret’s eyes brightened. "If only I had been there. My soul feels parched, thirsting for such dew."

Joan nodded. "He speaks again this afternoon. Unless you’re needed here—"

Catherine waved a hand. "Go, child. You’ll be better company warmed by a sermon than sighing over the stewpot."

Margaret hesitated, then glanced at her plain cap. "But the women will all be in their fine mutches..."

"Take my handkerchief from the basket," Catherine insisted. "The boy stays with me—Kate needs him today. Poor lamb, it’s one of her bad spells."

Margaret carried the child upstairs, where Kate lay abed, her face pale but serene. "Ah, sweeting," Margaret murmured, seeing the girl’s forced smile. "I’ve brought you your little sunbeam."

"Two sunbeams, I think," Kate whispered, though pain pinched her brow.

Margaret laughed softly. "In your lap? Nay, he’s too heavy for you now."

Kate’s arms tightened. "Love lightens his weight."

After a hurried toilette, Margaret paused at the door. "I’m off to the kirk. This preacher—they say his words are balm."

Kate sighed. "Bring me every word back? Oh, that I could go myself!" Her eyes shimmered.

Margaret kissed her brow. "I’ll try." She glanced at the boy, longing to kiss him too, but held back. "Your father’s rule—never wake a sleeping child. When he stirs, teach him those new words for Aunt Kate."

Downstairs, Catherine and Joan peeled vegetables with brisk efficiency. "Mind this, Joan," Catherine said. "Those who help cook may also feast. That’s a law in this house."

Joan grinned. "Better than most laws in Holland! My Luke’s supper is in the wind today—he’s digging ditches, thank the saints."

When Margaret descended, fresh-faced and tidy, Catherine caught her chin. "Look at you! Rotterdam’s finest couldn’t hold a candle." She pressed a kiss to her cheek. "Now heed—Eli won’t delay supper for the Duke himself. Don’t linger after the service."

The two women stood at the door, watching Margaret until she turned the corner. Catherine’s voice softened. "Joan, it’s like seeing my own daughter off to kirk again. My heart aches for her. She’ll hear no ill of Gerard, though if he lives, he’s treated her cruelly. That’s why I mean to see her wed to your good Luke—a steady man, no grand passions, but a quiet hearth. What’s one grandchild more or less?"

Joan chuckled, and the door closed on the bustling house, its warmth a small fortress against the world’s chill.

Margaret stepped into the vast, unfinished nave of St. Laurens as the sermon had already begun. The church was a grand, half-shaped thing—its side aisles roofed, its central vault open to the sky, the pillars stark and whitewashed against the rough stone. Only one window held glass; the rest were jagged gaps in the walls, letting the summer afternoon pour through in shafts of gold. The light danced across the uneven surfaces, painting the cool shadows with rivers of fire, turning the pillars into glowing sentinels. Above, the blue sky arched close, as if heaven itself leaned down to listen.

The crowd was a sea of white caps, motionless, hushed. The preacher’s voice—gentle, earnest, melodic—held them in thrall. Margaret slipped behind a pillar in the north aisle, where the words barely reached her, but the rhythm of his speech was enough. A quiet peace settled over her, the beauty of the place and the murmur of devotion smoothing the restless waves of her heart. She let her eyes drift half-shut, lulled into soft reverie.

Then she noticed a lady shifting from her place, retreating from the sun’s glare. Quick and silent, Margaret took the vacated spot beside a pillar in the south aisle, now close enough to hear every word. Yet her attention soon snagged on a shadow—a man’s head and shoulders bobbing in odd rhythm. She glanced back through the open window and saw the sexton, Jorian Ketel, digging. A faint smile tugged at her lips.

But then—

A single tone from the preacher’s voice struck her like a physical blow. Her breath caught; her hand flew to her chest as if to steady the sudden tremor inside her. She turned sharply toward the pulpit, but all she saw was the back of his head, the smooth tonsure gleaming in the light. A sigh escaped her. That shaven crown seemed to deny the strange familiarity of his voice. Still, she leaned forward, listening, waiting for that note again—the one that had stirred something deep and half-remembered.

It did not come. But his voice, rising and falling, wove through her like a thread of old music, tugging at forgotten joys. She kept her eyes down, unwilling to break the spell.

Then—silence.

The preacher had stopped mid-sentence. Margaret’s heart lurched. Had he finished? No—the crowd sat still, expectant. Then she noticed the turning of faces, the ripple of murmurs. She looked behind her, thinking some disturbance had drawn their attention. But there was nothing.

Only when she turned back did she see it.

The friar’s face was deathly pale, his eyes—wide, unnatural—fixed on her. Not past her, not near her, but *on* her. As if she were a ghost. A shudder ran through the congregation. Women gasped; men whispered. Margaret shrank behind the pillar, her pulse hammering. One fleeting glance had been enough. In that stricken face, she had seen something that froze her blood.

The sermon stumbled on, the preacher’s voice now fevered, exalted, as if he fought to outrun his own shock. When it ended, the crowd spilled out in murmuring clusters. Margaret moved with them, but at the door, she turned aside, slipping into the churchyard.

She made her way to Peter’s grave—her father’s resting place. Though poor, she had given him a headstone. Now she knelt on the slab, pressed her lips to the cold stone, then drew her apron over her head like a veil.

*"Father,"* she whispered, *"you always said I was strong. But these waters run deeper than I knew. I’ll follow that friar to the ends of the earth if I must—but at arm’s length. And he will tell me why he looked at me as if I were the dead returned."*

Her fingers curled against the stone. *"Speak for me, father. For I am wading in deep waters now."*

From where she sat, she could see the church door. And there she waited, face hidden, watching—a woman turned hunter, patient as the grave.

The cool, sun-dappled church, its vaulted ceiling painted in hues of celestial purple, wrapped Father Clement in its serene embrace—just as it did Margaret. But for him, it was more than beauty; it was a silent hymn to the Creator of all things good and pure. His gaze drifted over the crowded nave, where a sea of white caps, edged with delicate gold filigree, stretched before him. He had wandered far, yet nowhere had he seen anything like these—save perhaps fields of snow.

That morning, his voice had thundered with divine wrath, but now, beneath the gentle afternoon light, such fire seemed out of place. His heart swelled with compassion for the flock before him—so hungry for the love of God, so seldom fed. And so, he spoke not of judgment, but of grace, his words tender, earnest, weaving the glad tidings of salvation into the quiet air. The people listened, rapt, for he was no whirlwind preacher, no pulpit weathervane spinning with every gust of rhetoric. He moved souls, not limbs.

Yet he was mindful of those in shadowed corners, and soon, aflame with the desire that none should miss the message, he turned toward the south aisle.

And there—bathed in a shaft of golden light—stood Margaret Brandt.

For a moment, his mind refused to grasp it. The sight struck him numb. Then, like a bolt, recognition tore through him. His voice faltered. His hands trembled.

There she was, her auburn hair aflame in the sunlight, haloed like a saint’s, her face aglow with life—no specter, no memory, but flesh and blood. She leaned lightly against a pillar, her lashes lowered as she listened, just as she had listened to him long ago. She had not changed. Time had only deepened her beauty, ripened it like summer fruit.

His breath seized. The congregation blurred into nothingness. A rustling murmur rose around him, but he heard none of it. His whole being was fixed upon her, waiting for the vision to dissolve.

But she remained.

Then—swift as a spark—her eyes met his.

_Violet eyes. Hers._

A cry rose in his throat, her name trembling on his lips—but before it could escape, she turned away, vanishing into the crowd. Yet not before one last fleeting glance, quick as lightning, struck him to the core. It was not recognition, not yet—but something tremulous, questioning, the silent heartbeat before the storm.

His hands clenched the pulpit. His mind reeled. He muttered something incoherent, then forced himself to stumble onward, his sermon fraying into broken threads. But as the shock ebbed, a feverish energy took hold. If this was no vision, then it was a sign—a revelation. She had died. She had been buried here, near this very pillar. Why else would she appear?

When the service ended, he descended, shaken, but with a new purpose. He would find her grave.

The church stood empty now. Stepping out into the churchyard, he approached the sexton—a man he knew, though the man did not know him. The beard that had once framed his face was gone, his cheeks thinned by fasting, his hair cropped and threaded with premature grey.

"My son," Clement said softly, "if you remember those laid to rest here, tell me—is anyone buried inside the church, near the pillars?"

The sexton—Jorian—shook his head. "Nay, father. All lie here in the yard. Why?"

Clement hesitated. "Then... where is Margaret Brandt buried?"

Jorian frowned. "Margaret Brandt? Died three years back, you say?"

"Yes. From Sevenbergen."

Jorian scratched his head, then snorted. "Either you're jesting with me, father, or someone's jested with you. There's no Margaret Brandt buried here—leastways, not in my time."

Clement's breath caught. "But... she was poor. Her father was Peter, the physician. She came here—to die."

Jorian's expression darkened. He planted his spade firmly. "Now see here, holy sir—are you fooling me, or has some wag fooled you?" His tone was sharp, but then, seeing the friar's stricken face, he relented. "Ah, forgive me. Mayhap you mean Peter Brandt’s grave? He lies by the west door. I’ll show you."

He pulled on his doublet, ready to lead the way—unaware that someone already waited there, watching.

And unaware that the past was not so easily buried.

Jorian fastened his doublet as he prepared to visit Peter’s tomb, his words tumbling out in a steady stream.

“They called him a magician out Sevenbergen way, you know. And folk say he left them with a parting glimpse of his craft—told them he saw Margaret’s lad returning down the Rhine, clad in fine clothes and flush with coin, but his face scarred by foreign steel, and maybe missing a limb or two. But ah, nothing came of it. Margaret still pines for her boy, and Peter lies quiet as the rest beneath his slab. You’ll see for yourself.”

He braced his hands on the edge of the grave to hoist himself out, but the friar clutched his shoulder, fingers trembling. His voice was a hushed, strained thing.

“How long has Peter Brandt been dead?”

“Two months or so. Why?”

“And his daughter buried him, you say?”

“Nay, *I* buried him. She paid for the stone and the rites.”

“Then—he had but one daughter? Margaret?”

“Aye, none other that he owned to.”

The friar’s breath hitched. “Then you believe Margaret lives?”

“Believe? I’d be dead if she didn’t. Riddle me that.”

“God help me, how should I? You love her!”

Jorian snorted. “Not beyond sense. I’m a wedded man with four stout lads of my own. No, the truth is she saved my life scarce six weeks past. Dead women don’t pull living men from the grave. I was rotting away—couldn’t keep a bite down, nor the leeches mend me. My Joan said, ‘’Tis time to buy thy shroud.’ ‘Aye,’ said I, ‘but try borrowing one first.’ Then in comes Margaret—this same lass you’d have dead three years—flings open the windows, shifts my bed, and cures me quick as blinking. With what? A sorry weed from my own garden—sweet feverfew! A weed, mind you, till it mended me. Now I bow to the blasted thing whenever I pass. But—hold, father! You’ve gone pale as milk, now red, now white again. What in Heaven’s name ails you?”

“The shock—the joy—the terror—” Clement gasped. “What is she to you? Are you kin to Margaret Brandt?”

“Nay. But I knew one who loved her near to death. Her passing near broke him, body and soul. And yet you say she lives. And I believe you.”

Jorian fell silent, then spoke gravely. “Father, you’ve asked me much, and I’ve answered true. Now, for Our Lady’s sake, answer me two things: Did you truly know one who loved this lass? And where?”

Clement nearly spoke his name—then bit back the word, remembering Jerome’s warning. “In Italy,” he said.

Jorian’s eyes narrowed. “If you knew him, name him.”

“Gerard Eliassoen.”

Jorian stiffened. “Strange. Wait—who told you Margaret was dead?”

“I was with Gerard when a letter came from Margaret Van Eyck. It said she he loved was dead and buried.” Clement swayed. “Let me sit—my strength fails. Foul play, Jorian. Foul play.”

“Father,” said Jorian, helping him down, “I thank Heaven you came to me. Sit—you’re white as a shroud. Fasting robs a man’s strength. My gut twists with this. I think I see the knot of it now. And if I’m right, there are two knaves in this town whose skulls I’d crack like clods.” He snatched up his spade and drove it furiously into the earth.

“Foul play? Aye, and if you know where Gerard is now, waste no time—warn him of the trap. I’m no quick wit, but even a slow hound picks the scent. I think you and I hold two ends of the same vile rope.”

Then, after the meandering prelude common to his kind, Jorian laid it bare. He’d been hired by Ghysbrecht, burgomaster of Tergou, to mend an upper floor. Nearing the end, he’d returned for his tools and heard raised voices below. Curiosity pinned him flat. The burgomaster spoke of a letter Cornelis and Sybrandt meant to swap for one Hans Memling carried to Gerard.

“Seemed they’d the will but not the spine,” Jorian said. “So the old fox lured them—showed a drawer full of silver. Do the deed, he said, and each could scoop all the coin one hand could hold. Well, father, I thought little of it then—save the bargain itself, which kept me awake half the night. But next dawn, who should I spy but Cornelis and Sybrandt slinking out, each with a black eye. ‘Ah,’ thought I, ‘Hans left his mark on ye. Hope that’s all you got for your pains.’ Then off they crept to Ghysbrecht’s. I followed, hid, and watched.”

Clement’s nostrils flared, his fingers twitching.

“The burgomaster had a letter in hand—I’m no scholar, but my eyes are sharp. The drawer opened. Those two rats plunged in their claws, hauling out fistfuls of silver—greedy as crows! Sybrandt smeared his hand with glue, stole extra coins stuck to his knuckles—the fool! So, you see, the Devil got his due. They sold their souls for a handful of silver. There, father—it’s off my chest at last. I’ve longed to tell someone, but not the women. Margaret’d have lost every friend had I spoken. Those two vipers are the darlings—always so. See their fine new shop in Hoog Straet? The sign’s a gilded sheep and lamb. Wolves at the teat were fitter. And today the whole clan feasts there. Oh, it’s a fair world.”

Clement sat like stone.

“Not a word?” Jorian growled. “Not even a curse for those heartless whoresons? The lad was alone in a strange land—wasn’t that grief enough? Must his own blood steal the one comfort left him? And think of her—sitting at her window year after year, watching for Gerard! No woman ever loved truer, my Joan says. Not a day passes but she weeps. And but for those lying thieves, that sweet lad—whose life *I* saved—might be with her now, the happiest man in Holland. And she, the happiest lass, not the saddest! By the blood of Christ, I thirst to see their—”

Clement erupted.

He shot to his feet, face contorted, a silent storm of fury. Then he was gone—charging down the street like a madman, fists clenched high.

Jorian gaped after him, his own rage dwarfed by the friar’s tempest.

Then—a small hand clutched his arm.

Margaret Brandt stood there, pale as mist. She had waited by Peter’s tomb, but the friar never came. Searching the church, she’d seen his shadow loom monstrously, then vanish. Now she and Jorian stared at each other, then at the distant, frenzied figure.

“What did he say to you?” she whispered.

Jorian swallowed. “He asked where you were buried.”

Her fingers dug into his sleeve. “And?”

“I told him nowhere—you live. Then he spoke of knowing Gerard in Italy—of a letter saying you were dead. It broke Gerard’s heart. The letter was from Margaret Van Eyck—or so he thought. But I told him the truth: Ghysbrecht and those two snakes swapped the real letter for a lie. And now—ah, lass, I fear I’ve loosed a storm.”

Her knees buckled. “He *lives*.” The words were a prayer. Then terror seized her. “Jorian—you don’t know him as I do. That blow he gave Ghysbrecht—the sound of it! Quick, we must stop him!”

“What? That friar—Gerard?”

“Yes! He’s come back to me at last. Oh, hurry! Before blood is spilled!”

They ran, her legs trembling, his jerkin half-fastened. As they neared Hoog Straet, a roar echoed from within. The friar burst out, white-faced, fists shaking at the sky.

Margaret screamed, then sagged against Jorian. He bellowed after the friar, but the man was deaf with rage, already vanishing down the street.

“Help me inside,” Margaret whispered, suddenly calm. “Let me face what’s done.”

The house was silent—unnaturally so. The door stood ajar. Jorian pushed it open.

What they saw inside would change everything.

The supper table was laid, and Eli’s household had gathered—all but Margaret. Catherine frowned, puzzled that Eli insisted on waiting.

“I told her you wouldn’t wait for the duke himself,” she said.

“She’s not the duke,” Eli replied gruffly. “She’s a good lass who’s waited years, not minutes, for a thankless son of mine. Put the food out—we’ll eat the moment she comes.”

The rich aroma of the meal proved too tempting. “She’ll come once we start,” Eli conceded. “Mistress Joan, sit you down—you’re a guest here, not a servant.”

No sooner had the knives been raised than the door burst open. But it was not Margaret who entered—it was a Dominican friar, his face twisted with fury.

In an instant, he loomed over Cornelis and Sybrandt, his hands clawing the air above their heads like talons. Then came the curse—a torrent of damnation so vivid and venomous it seemed to scorch the very air.

*“Cursed be the lying lips that swore Margaret dead—may they rot before the grave and sear on hell’s iron! Cursed be the hands that forged those letters—may the hangman strike them off to clutch eternal fire! Cursed be the hearts that schemed to sunder love—may they wither joyless, loveless, hopeless, and burn beyond the grave!”*

He cursed their flesh from scalp to sole, then spun toward Eli, flinging a letter onto the table.

*“Read, you hard-hearted fool! See what monsters you’ve bred!”* His voice cracked like a whip. *“We meet again at Judgment—never on earth!”*

And like a storm, he was gone. The household sat frozen, the feast untouched before them.

Margaret arrived then, Jorian at her side. Her eyes darted over the scene—no blood, no violence—only stricken faces. She gasped in relief.

Catherine shrieked and turned away. Eli, scanning the false letter, roared to his feet, sword drawn.

The brothers scrambled beneath the table, howling. Eli lunged, but Margaret and Jorian seized his arms, holding him back just long enough for the pair to flee into the night.

Eli hacked down the shop’s signboard and flung it into the fire. Catherine sobbed into her apron, while Margaret, pale and trembling, tried in vain to comfort her.

*“Oh, my poor wicked boys!”* Catherine wailed. *“Better he’d stayed in Italy than come to curse his own!”*

*“Hold your tongue!”* Eli snapped. *“Your coddling made them what they are!”* Then, gentler, he beckoned Margaret. *“Come here, lass. You’re my daughter now.”*

She stumbled to him, resting her head against his chest, too shattered for tears.

*“Poor lamb,”* he murmured.

With a ragged breath, Eli turned to Jorian and Joan. *“Eat, friends. Trouble’s no excuse for starving guests.”* But Jorian recoiled.

Eli’s face fell. *“Aye. My own son cursed my table.”* He sighed. *“God’s will be done.”*

The supper was tossed to the yard. The house sank into silence, heavy with grief.

And Clement? He lay prostrate before the altar, forehead pressed to the cold stone, torn between rage, remorse, and a fragile, dawning joy—*she lived*.

But then—a sharper sting. He had abandoned a dying man.

Groaning, he rose. Rain lashed outside; wolves howled in the dark. Yet he strode into the storm, beating his breast.

Duty called, and penance was never done.

What words could capture the storm that raged within a soul so tender, so devout, when faced with a trial so sudden and terrible? To stand in his place—to feel the weight of his sorrow, his conscience, his love—would be to know a suffering beyond the reach of mere description. A volume might trace its shadows, yet in the end, the heart alone could fathom it.

Let it suffice, then, to mark his next deeds, for they reveal the man once the first tempest had passed.

After a night spent in prayer beside the dying hermit, he turned his steps not toward Rotterdam, but Tergou. His purpose? To face the man who had been his ruin—the burgomaster—and with the parchment in hand (itself a relic of strange fortune), to wrest from him what was rightfully Margaret’s.

Weary and dust-streaked, he paused at the town’s fountain to break his meager meal of black bread and water. But before he could finish, a servant girl came hastening, begging him to come at once—her master lay dying and in need of shrift. Without a word, he returned the bread to his scrip and followed.

She led him—to the Stadthouse.

A flicker of cold recognition passed through him as he crossed the threshold, but he mastered it at once. Up the stairs they went, and there, propped upon pillows, ghastly as death already, lay Ghysbrecht van Swieten.

Clement paused in the doorway, his mind racing through the past—the tower, the forest, the stolen letter. Then, softly, he spoke:

Even as he said it, his voice trembled.

The dying man’s eyes lit with feeble relief. “Thank Heaven,” he gasped. “Thou art come in time to absolve me, father. Pray for my soul—thou and thy brethren.”

“Absolution follows confession, my son,” said Clement. “And confession must hold nothing back, lest thy soul be lost. Search thy heart while I pray for light to guide thee.”

He knelt, murmured his prayer, then rose and fixed his gaze upon Ghysbrecht. “Now—confess thy sins.”

“Ah, father,” sighed the burgomaster, “they are many.”

“Then let thy penitence be deep, and God’s mercy deeper.”

Ghysbrecht clasped his hands and began—haltingly, with every show of remorse. He admitted to eating meat in Lent, to skipping Mass on holy days, to laxity in lesser observances. A careful tally of faults, meticulously recounted.

When he fell silent, Clement said quietly, “These are frailties. Now—confess thy crimes.”

“Crimes?” The sick man blinked. “What crimes, if these are none?”

“Do I confess to thee,” Clement asked sharply, “or thou to me?”

“Forgive me, father! I—I know not what you call crimes.”

“The seven deadly sins. Art thou clean of them?”

“Heaven forbid I should be stained so! I scarcely know their names.”

“Yet many who cannot name them live by them. Begin with the root of theft, of lies, of murder.”

“I am free of that one, surely! What is it?”

“Avarice, my son.”

“Avarice?” Ghysbrecht gave a weak laugh. “I have been thrifty, true—but kept a good table, given alms. No, no, I am no miser. The next sin, father—perhaps that will catch me.”

“We are not done with this one. The Church is not to be mocked.”

“Mock her? Now, of all times?” The burgomaster’s voice quavered. “Avarice? Avarice?” He shook his head, bewildered.

“Hast thou ever robbed the fatherless?” Clement pressed.

“I? Robbed the—” Ghysbrecht’s breath caught. “Not that I recall.”

“Again thou triflest. One more evasion, and I leave thee—and the demons waiting at thy bedside will drag thee down to hell.”

“No! Do not go!” The old man clutched at him, wild-eyed. “The Church knows all, you say. Then—then I must have robbed the fatherless. I will confess! Only—my memory fails. Where shall I begin?”

A clever dodge—but futile now.

“Hast thou forgotten Floris Brandt?” Clement’s voice was stone.

Ghysbrecht jerked upright, his face a mask of terror. “How—how know you that?”

“The Church sees what is hidden,” said Clement coldly. “Thou didst summon her, thinking to deceive her. ‘I will not confess to the cure,’ thou saidst in thy heart, ‘but to some friar who knows me not. So shall I cheat death and die unshriven.’ But God, in mercy, sent thee one thou couldst not deceive. He tested thee, warned thee—yet still thou playest the penitent while clinging to thy sin. If thou wilt not confess, then die as thou hast lived. Even now, the fiends gather for thee.”

He turned to leave.

Ghysbrecht seized his robe with a desperate wail. “Mercy! Stay! I will confess—all! I robbed my friend Floris. And worse—I kept his son Peter’s land from him, and from Margaret after him. I meant to give it back—truly! But I—I could not.”

“Avarice,” said Clement softly. “But it is not too late.”

“No—I will leave it to her in my will. She need not wait long—a month, perhaps two—”

“And for a month’s gain, thou wouldst lose thy soul forever? Fool!”

The burgomaster groaned, pleading for reason. But Clement, with a patience that cost him dearly—for every moment in that man’s presence sickened him—worked upon him, loosening his grip on stolen wealth grain by grain. More than once, his hand strayed toward the deed hidden in his breast, but he held back. Yesterday’s fury had taught him caution.

And at last—without ever revealing the parchment—he prevailed. Ghysbrecht, broken, agreed to full and swift restitution. (How it was done, and what strange fruits it bore, shall be told in its place.)

Two acts marked Clement’s state of mind. This was the first.

The second? When he knew beyond doubt that Margaret was secure—rich, and safe—

He vanished.

The little house on Hoog Straet lay heavy with silence, as though death itself had taken up residence. Catherine, usually so bustling and bright, moved through the rooms like a shadow, her eyes raw from weeping. The weight of the previous day’s wrath still pressed upon them all, but none bore it so listlessly as she.

After a meager dinner, she gathered what resolve she had left and made her way to the convent, hoping to plead with Gerard, to lay the first fragile stone of reconciliation. But the porter, slow to understand her request, delivered bitter news instead: Gerard had departed the night before and would not return.

Defeated, Catherine carried the tidings to Margaret, who sat motionless, her arms locked around her child as if he were the last anchor in a storm-tossed sea.

"Come back with me," Catherine urged. "Eli might listen to you. You could soften him."

Margaret exhaled, slow and weary. "If you think I can do any good, I’ll come."

But when they arrived, they found Eli and a carpenter at work, stripping the names of Cornelis and Sybrandt from the family record and replacing them with Margaret Brandt’s. The sight struck Catherine like a blade.

Margaret only gazed at it, cold. "Can he make me spend the money unjustly?"

Catherine sighed. "You’re a good soul."

"Best to be, since he is strongest."

---

The next day, Giles arrived, and Catherine poured out the tale of the disgraced brothers, seeking pity for them—cast out, cursed, left to the mercy of the world. But Giles had no patience for false sympathy.

"They got off light," he scoffed. "Hanging at Margaret’s door would’ve been justice." Then, with sudden vigor, he grinned. "But Gerard’s back! I’ll show him what it means to have a brother at court—one with the heart to serve and the wit to guide."

Margaret murmured, "Bless thee, Giles."

Little Kate sighed. "You were ever his true friend. But what can you do for him now?"

---

The house settled back into silence, broken only when a well-dressed stranger entered, asking for Margaret Brandt. Catherine, ever the gossip despite her sorrow, perked up. "You’re wanted, lass."

Margaret lifted her head, hollow-eyed. "Here I am."

Then came the shuffling of unsteady feet. Ghysbrecht Van Swieten, pale as a specter, was helped into the room.

Catherine shrieked and threw her apron over her face. Margaret turned sharply away, as if the sight of him might poison her.

The old man’s voice was a whisper. "Good people… a dying man begs your forgiveness."

Catherine’s voice shook. "Come to admire your handiwork, more like!" She sobbed. "Look at her—she’s near fainting!"

Margaret’s breath came thin. "Let him say his piece and go. He is the murderer of me and mine."

Ghysbrecht swayed. "I am too weak to stand… yet none bid me sit."

Eli, torn between anger and duty, growled, "We don’t turn visitors from our hearth. But man, you’ve dealt us too much pain."

Then little Kate, gentle but firm, spoke. "Father, mother—death squares all accounts. And his time is shorter than mine."

Eli, chastened, set a chair for their enemy.

Ghysbrecht’s attendants brought in two boxes before withdrawing, leaving him alone with those he had wronged. With trembling hands, he opened them.

"These deeds are yours, Margaret Brandt—your grandfather’s land, stolen by me. And here, three hundred and forty gold angels, the rents I took beyond what was owed." His voice cracked. "I was not born dishonest… yet you see what I became."

Catherine gaped. "And you the burgomaster! Whipped thieves in your day, yet here you stand." Then, softening, "But better late than never. Margaret, you’re rich!"

Margaret did not turn. "Let him keep his gold. Give me back my Gerard, stolen by his lies."

Ghysbrecht bowed his head. "Would that I could. What I can do, I have. It cost me my last strength to rise from my deathbed and bring this myself."

Margaret’s voice was ice. "Since even you, who worship gold, have done this, may God forgive you. But I am only a woman with a broken heart. Kate—speak for me."

Kate’s voice, soft as a prayer, filled the room. "Her sorrow is beyond riches. Her boy is fatherless; she is neither maid, wife, nor widow. The wound is too fresh. Give her time. But we, whose hurts are lesser, forgive you. Go in peace."

Ghysbrecht, too weak for food or drink, was lifted into his litter. As he left, he murmured, "This land was a bed of nettles to me. Now that it’s gone, I feel lighter."

---

Once he was gone, Catherine marveled at the fortune before them. "Look at it, Margaret! So much gold!"

But Margaret only stared, her face empty, until suddenly she wrenched her hands together and cried out in agony—

The wealth that might have kept Gerard by her side had come when it no longer mattered. The gold, the land—all useless now. A single year earlier, and their lives would have been different.

But fate had dealt its blow.

And love had come too late.

Here is the rewritten chapter of *The Cloister and the Hearth*, preserving its historical fiction tone while refining clarity and emotional depth:

---

Not many days after Margaret Van Eyck’s death, the will was read. To Margaret Brandt, her "dear daughter," she left her house, her sketches, and her paintings—treasures that would one day be worth a fortune. Among them was a diptych Margaret cherished above all others: *A Betrothal*, painted jointly by Jan and Margaret Van Eyck. The solemnity of the ceremony shone in the man’s grave face, the woman’s demure contentment.

Margaret sent word to Reicht Heynes to remain in the house until she could bear to enter it without aching for the voice and face now gone. "Guard the picture in the little cupboard," she instructed—the diptych, fragile as memory itself.

Then Luke Peterson returned, triumphant. Gerard was a monk. *Free at last!*

"Since he can’t wed you, I can," he declared.

Margaret’s eyes flashed. "I have seen him."

"But he’s a friar now."

"He was my husband and my child’s father long before he took vows. And I have seen him."

Luke scowled, baffled. "I’ve a cousin, a lawyer. I’ll ask him—are you wed or single?"

"Ask *me*, not a lawyer. Would you make me the town’s gossip?"

"It’s already gossip without my help."

"Then do not add to it, if you value my respect. Speak of this again, and never come near me."

Luke sighed. "To others, you’re a dove. To me, a tyrant."

"Because you woo me, Luke. Look around—plenty of lasses fairer than I, unspoiled by tears. Choose any. Speak to her, and I’ll speak to her mother. They’ll not refuse you."

Luke reddened. "I see your game. But if I can’t have your heart, I’ll not take your coin. I loved you poor—now you’re rich, you’d rather be a friar’s leman than an honest man’s wife? Then part we here—you to your sorrow, me to my bachelor’s grave."

"Farewell, Luke. And may God forgive you those words."

---

For days, Margaret dreaded and longed for Gerard’s return. *He stays away out of shame—as I would.* Yet surely the news of his son would draw him. But as time passed, suspicion crept in. *What if he stays away on purpose?* What if his brothers in the cloister had warned him never to see her again? The thought hollowed her heart.

Now anger stirred alongside grief. *Did he ever love me? To abandon his child without a word?* Even Luke, rough as he was, showed more care.

Then Giles burst in, roaring. "I’ve done it! Our Gerard is Vicar of Gouda!"

---

The dwarf’s rise at court had been swift. His wit, his voice, his size—all made him a favorite. The young Duchess and her ladies petted him; the court poet buttered his verses with flattery. But Giles’ true triumph came when he challenged the Duke’s giant to a trial of strength—and won.

"Climbed a pole like a squirrel," he crowed to Margaret. "Then wrestled the oaf blindfolded—made him bow to me! After that, none dared cross me."

Now he recounted his audience with the princess:

"I came to her gloomy. ‘What ails thee?’ she asked. ‘At heart,’ said I. ‘In love?’ she teased. The maids giggled. ‘Not so mad,’ I said. ‘I’ve seen women at court.’

"She bade me speak freely. So I did: ‘Princes never keep their word.’

"She stiffened—then melted when I reminded her of Gerard, the fair youth who’d brought a letter years ago. ‘Your brother?’ she gasped, eyeing me head to toe.

"I told her of the promised living, forgotten. ‘My mother said, *See thou to it.*’ She wept. ‘Then thy brother is Vicar of Gouda—so sure as I rule Burgundy.’"

Margaret seized Giles, kissing him. "Bless you! Now we shall see him again!"

---

Yet Gerard did not come. Giles had him cried in Rotterdam—still nothing. Margaret seethed. *He flees me because I live.*

Then a Tergovian brought word: Ghysbrecht Van Swieten had seen Gerard last.

She went to him. The old burgomaster, now feeble, greeted her warmly. "I saw him—not Gerard, but Friar Clement." He recounted their meeting: how Gerard had come to force the deed’s return, yet stayed to save his enemy’s soul.

"‘I forgive thee,’ he said—*he*, whom I wronged most!" Ghysbrecht wept. "A saint."

Margaret, moved, forgave him too. "Help me now. Gerard avoids me—he will lose his living."

Ghysbrecht summoned clerks, sending pleas to every town in Holland. "We *will* find the vicar."

---

But fresh sorrow came. Sybrandt, drunk, fell from a roof, his spine broken. He lay groaning, cursing his fate—and Gerard. "Lift the curse!" he begged Margaret.

She tended him, though he had been her foe. Catherine arrived, heartbroken. "I am punished for deceiving Eli."

Sybrandt’s pleas wore on her: "Find Gerard. He alone can heal me."

Margaret spent freely, hiring searchers—even Luke, who returned, thinner, pleading reconciliation.

"Be my brother," she said wearily. "Help me find my marriage lines."

"I’ll bring them—or him," Luke vowed.

But the months dragged. Gouda’s folk petitioned for a true vicar. Giles’ influence waned. Time was running out.

---

The chapter closes on Margaret’s quiet resolve: *I will not judge him until I hear his own voice.* But the question lingers—where is Gerard? And will he return before it is too late?

---

This version tightens the prose, heightens emotional beats, and maintains the historical texture while improving readability. Let me know if you'd like any refinements!

The hermit of Gouda was, in truth, the vicar of Gouda—though he knew it not, so absolute was his seclusion. When the storm of his passion had passed, remorse seized him like a serpent coiling about his heart. Only those who have climbed high in faith and then fallen can understand the terror that now gripped him. He stood as one who, lured by pride to a cliff’s edge, looks down into the abyss and trembles.

*"Ah, Jerome,"* he groaned, *"how much wiser you were than I! Your warnings were bitter, but they were medicine for my soul."*

Searching his own heart, he saw the truth plainly: Margaret still held him. *"I love her more than God,"* he whispered in despair. *"More than the Church. What fruit can such love bear but ruin?"* A shudder ran through him. *"Let the strong wrestle with temptation—the weak must flee. And who is weaker than I?"*

His faith, his penance—had they been anything more than a fragile structure of cards, swept away by a single breath of earthly longing? *"I must begin anew,"* he resolved, *"and build upon rock, not sand."*

He would leave Holland at once, seek some distant cloister, and spend years in solitude before returning. By then, his heart would be hardened against earthly affections. And Margaret—she would forget him, marry another. The very pain this thought brought him proved the necessity of his flight. *"Better she should hate me than damn her soul with mine."*

Yet before he left, he would ensure her worldly comfort. The thought that he could lift her from poverty was his one solace. *"Let her never lose another true love for want of gold,"* he murmured, though the words tore at him.

His first task was done—confronting Ghysbrecht, forcing him to restore what he had stolen. Then, as fate would have it, he came to the dying hermit of Gouda. The man had been a soldier once, wearing a steel corselet beneath his hair shirt, calling himself *"Christ’s soldier now, where once I was Satan’s."* Clement shrived him, prayed with him, and in turn sought counsel from one who faced death with such peace.

*"The world, the flesh, and the devil,"* whispered the hermit, *"I fought them all. But only in solitude did I find victory."*

Those words, and the hermit’s serene passing, struck deep into Clement’s soul. Here was a man who had ended his days in holy stillness, his battles won. And Clement knew much of hermits—had read their lives in the Vatican’s manuscripts, spoken with mendicant friars, heard Jerome’s tales of Camaldoli’s anchorites, where nobles and warriors had ended their days in caves, wrestling with their own weakness until they turned it to strength.

*"Heaven has led me here,"* he thought. *"This cell shall be my fortress."*

Yet he would not take its comforts without penance. He compared it to the brutal solitudes of the ancient saints—Jerome’s desert, Benedict’s thorn-choked crevice, the damp dungeons and stone coffins of others. *"This place is too kind,"* he muttered. *"I must make it a crucible."*

And so he vowed: *"For a year and a day, I will see no man’s face."*

He filled his hours with prayer, labor, and mortification. By night, he stole out to dig gardens for the poor, left baskets at humble doors, carved scripture into the rocks along the road—words of comfort for weary travelers, who had never heard the Gospels in their own tongue. *"Perhaps,"* he thought, *"a chance word may light some darkened heart."*

The villagers, awed by his unseen presence, his wise counsel, his refusal of all but bread and water, soon hailed him as a saint. Yet inside the cell, Clement knew himself no saint. *"The birds do not trust me,"* he lamented. *"The owl that lived here fled. They know a true hermit’s spirit is lacking."*

For a time, he numbed his grief with labor. But then came a darkness deeper than any before—a despair so vast it choked prayer itself. Voices whispered: *"Kill yourself. End it."* He begged God for any other trial—flames, scourges, demons in visible form—anything but this hollow, gnawing void.

*"What if solitude destroys more souls than it saves?"* he wondered wildly. *"What if the hermits who vanished were not martyrs but lost souls, devoured by their own thoughts?"*

He tried music, as David had for Saul, but his own hands could not lift his spirit. *"Saul had a holy minstrel,"* he wept. *"I am alone with the darkness."*

And so he lay, crushed beneath the weight of his own heart, while the ancient warning echoed in his ears:

---

(1) In an age before printed Scripture, the poor found solace in words carved on stone—gospels in their own tongue, fresh as dew on parched earth.

(2) Some ancient writers hinted that hermits lost to "serpents" were not martyrs but victims of despair—a thought Clement now knew too well.

One day, as Clement lay in his cave, sighing in the depths of despair, a thought struck him like a spark in the dark. If he could carve holy words upon his walls—sacred invocations, prayers, and verses of scripture—then perhaps the very stones around him might become a bulwark against the tempests of his soul. So he seized his chisel, not as a warrior takes up his sword, but as a laborer grips his tool, and with feverish devotion, he began to etch the words of God into the cold rock.

Like the great sculptors of Italy who shaped marble with divine fury, Clement did not pause to sketch his lines. He carved from memory, from the depths of his faith, letting the chisel dance under his hand as if each stroke were a prayer. He begged candle-ends and rancid oil from his few visitors—"Anything that burns," he said—and by night his cave glowed like a forge, casting flickering light upon the sacred words rising from the stone.

One by one, the holy talismans took shape around him, a fortress of scripture against despair. Beneath the great crucifix fastened to his wall, he carved the words of St. Augustine:

*O Christian soul, look upon the wounds of the suffering Christ, the blood of the dying Redeemer, the price paid for thy salvation! Weigh these things in thy mind, that He may be fixed in thy heart as He was fixed upon the cross. Remember His passion, and no earthly trial shall seem too hard to bear.*

The words soothed him, if only a little. Exhausted at last, he fell into a deep sleep. When he awoke, he heard a soft chirping. There, upon his breviary, perched a robin, its bright eyes darting, its feathers fluffed and smoothed in quick succession. Clement held his breath, afraid to frighten it away. The bird hopped onto his bare foot, its tiny claws barely felt, then flitted back to the book. With slow, careful movements, Clement reached for a crumb of bread and flicked it toward the robin. The bird seized the offering and fled, but soon returned for more.

"Bless you, little one," Clement murmured. "You are a ray of light in my darkness."

And so the days passed. The cold deepened, but his heart grew warmer. Despair faded like a retreating shadow. His prayers filled with fervor, his psalms with joy. More birds came, fluttering in from the snow, finding sanctuary with him. One frosty night, as he sang praises to his psaltery, his voice echoing through the hollow cave, he heard a low, mournful howl outside. Peering through the cracks of his door, he saw a great red wolf, its muzzle lifted to the sky, answering his song with its own wild music.

"My sins are leaving me," Clement whispered. "The creatures of God acknowledge me at last."

But peace was not to last. Visions came—dreams so vivid they blurred the line between waking and sleep. At first, they were celestial: saints with rainbow wings, voices like music. Then they shifted. He wandered ruins with Fra Colonna, saw temples rise from dust, heard philosophers speak in forgotten tongues. And always, beneath the marvels, a dread lurked—something waiting, something terrible.

Then, one night, the dream changed. Margaret stood before him, her hair like a halo, her smile tender. "Gerard," she whispered, "by day be whose you will, but at night be mine." He woke trembling, his heart pounding.

The battle had begun anew. He fasted longer, scourged himself till blood ran, plunged into icy streams to numb his flesh. Yet still she came, radiant in his dreams, more real than memory. He knew it was the Enemy's work, a demon wearing her face. The hermits of old had faced such trials—St. Anthony, St. Macarius—all tempted by phantoms of desire.

He fought with every weapon of asceticism: hunger, cold, pain. One night, desperate, he threw himself upon a bed of thorns, rolling until his skin was torn and bleeding. At last, exhausted, he fell into a dreamless sleep.

When he woke, he was victorious—or so he thought. The visions ceased. The birds grew tamer, perching on his hand, allowing him to gild their claws. He dared to hope.

Then, one day, a woman's voice called to him from outside, asking after Gerard. He answered, "Pray for the soul of Gerard, son of Eli," and she was gone. But the voice haunted him. That night, Margaret returned in his dreams, more vivid than ever. He could count the strands of her hair, see the light in her eyes.

"I must leave this place," he whispered. "Memory chains me here."

He took to sleeping in the woods, shivering beneath the stars. One clear, frosty night, he returned to his cell, marveling at the vastness of heaven. "How can men live for this small world," he murmured, "when that great one shines above us?"

He paused at his door. It was closed. He had left it open. A prickle of unease ran through him. Peering through a crack, he saw his small oil lamp still burning.

"Either I shut it," he muttered, "or the fiend has shut it to trouble me."

He pushed inside, lighting a taper with nervous hands. As the flame caught, a sigh whispered through the cave. His pulse leaped. He turned, shielding the light—and there, in the farthest corner, stood Margaret Brandt.

Her posture was one to stir pity rather than fear—unless the eyes that beheld her were clouded by suspicion. Her bosom fluttered like a trapped bird, her cheeks flushed and paled in turns, and her trembling hand braced against the wall for support, so unsteady were her limbs. The look in her eyes—purple as twilight—was a marvel of mingled love, reverence, pity, and tender memory, all fixed upon the gaunt and hollow-eyed hermit before her.

"Aha!" he cried. "Thou comest at last in flesh and blood—come to me as thou camest to holy Anthony. But I am armed against thee." He snatched up his crucifix and thrust it toward her, the candle in his other hand shaking violently. "*Exorcizo te!*"

"Ah, no!" she pleaded, lifting her hands in supplication. "Do me no harm! It is Margaret."

"Liar!" he shouted. "Margaret was fair, but not so radiant as thou. Thou flinchest at that holy name—thou subtle hypocrite! *In Nomine Dei, exorcizo vos!*"

"Jesu, mercy!" gasped Margaret, terror-stricken. "Curse me not! I will go home. I thought—I hoped—" Her voice broke. "For the love of manhood, do not Latin me away! Gerard, is this how we meet again, after all this time, after all?" She sank nearly to her knees, sobbing—half in wounded love, half in superstitious dread.

Had his mind not been steeped in visions of demons, he might have doubted longer whether this trembling, grief-stricken creature was truly woman or fiend. But the sacred name she invoked shattered his delusion. The truth struck him like lightning.

With a cry of remorse, he tried to lift her, but his limbs, weakened by fasting and the shock of emotion, failed him. "What—is it truly thee?" he gasped. "Have I met thee with such cruelty? Forgive me!"

For a long moment, neither could speak, choked by the weight of feeling.

"I do not blame thee much," Margaret said at last, mastering her tears with effort. "Thou tookest me for another—for a devil! Oh—oh!"

"Forgive me, gentle soul." When at last he could form more than broken words, he said, "I have been sorely plagued by the Evil One since I came here."

Margaret shuddered, glancing around the dismal cell. "No wonder. Then take my hand, I beg thee, and let me lead thee from this foul place."

He stared at her in astonishment. "What—desert my cell? Return to the world? Is this why thou hast sought me?" His voice was heavy with reproach.

"Ay, Gerard! I come to take thee to thy vicarage at Gouda—thanks to Heaven and thy brother Giles. Thy mother and I have made it fair for thee. 'Tis snug enough in winter, I promise thee. But wait till spring, when the hawthorn blooms and roses climb thy walls. Didst think we had forgotten thee—forgotten all the foolish things thou lovest?" Her voice softened. "And thy mother waits even now—'tis late for her to be abed. Prithee, come! The moment we quit this wretched hole, I will show thee a treasure thou hast—unknowing—given thyself. Else thou wouldst never have fled from us so!"

Her words faltered. "Alas, what is wrong? What have I said to make thee look so?"

For he had drawn back, his face a mask of suspicion and dread.

"Unhappy girl," he said solemnly, though his voice quaked, "wouldst have me risk my soul—and thine—for a paltry vicarage and its flowers? This is not thy doing. The pitiless Fiend sends thee, his unwitting tool, to tempt me. But I will unmask him even to thee, that thou mayst loathe him as I do." He drew a sharp breath. "Margaret, my lost love—knowest thou why I am here? Because I love thee."

"Oh, no! If thou lovedst me, thou wouldst not have hidden from me. There was no need!"

"Let there be no deceit between us, who have loved so truly—and who, after this night, must meet no more on earth."

"God forbid!" she cried.

"I love thee still—else thou wouldst have wed another by now, nor been the one to seek me in this desolate place. But I am a priest, a monk. What can come of us living side by side, feeding a passion once innocent, now forbidden? No—though my heart break, I must be firm. 'Tis I who am the man, the priest. We must not meet again—not till solitude has schooled me, and thou art wed to another."

"I will bear my doom—but not thine. I would die ten times over rather than see thee rot in this dismal den when a fair manse awaits thee!"

Clement groaned. Each word she spoke only sharpened the agony of duty.

"My beloved," he said, torn between tenderness and iron resolve, "I bless thee for this one more glimpse of thy sweet face. And may God forgive thee—and bless thee—for shattering in a moment the peace I have built through six months of solitude. No matter. A year of penance may restore me." His voice broke. "Poor Margaret, I seem cruel—yet I am kind. 'Tis best we part—now, this moment."

"Part? Never! We have seen what comes of parting. Gerard, thou hast not heard half my tale—no, not a tenth! 'Tis not for comfort alone I bring thee to Gouda. Hear me!"

"I dare not. Thy very voice is temptation—its music a snare to memory."

"Then I say thou *shalt* hear me, Gerard! I will not leave this place unheard!"

"Then must we part by other means," he said sadly.

"What means? Wouldst thrust me out by force—being the stronger?"

"Nay, Margaret. Thou knowest I would suffer death rather than harm thee. Thy sweet body is dearer to me than mine own—but a thousand times dearer are our souls, both thine and mine. I have endured this direst temptation long enough. Now I must fly. Farewell!"

He lunged for the door, flung it open, and was halfway out when she darted after him, seizing his arm.

"Then another must speak for me! I meant to reward thee for yielding—but since thou wilt not, his voice must plead. Turn but a moment—look!"

"Nay—"

"I say *ay*! And then turn thy back if thou canst!"

She loosened her grip, trusting he would not deny so small a kindness. But seeing his chance, he wrenched free with a cry:

"Fly, Clement, fly!"

His monastic fervor lent him a burst of strength. He tore from her grasp, leaped the brook, and ran. But hearing no pursuit, he halted and looked back.

She lay prone, hands outstretched. In his haste, he had thrown her down.

The sight struck him like a blade. He took a step toward her—then, with a wild impulse, flung himself into the icy stream instead.

"Here, kill my body!" he gasped, half-submerged. "But save my soul!"

As he stood there, the freezing water numbing his limbs, Margaret uttered a long, broken moan and pushed herself to her knees. The moonlight showed her face—pale, eyes glistening with tears. Then came her words, clear in the silent night:

"Oh, God! Thou who knowest all—Thou seest how I am used. Forgive me, then! For I will not live another day!"

With that, she sprang to her feet and fled past him like a wounded creature, her cry ringing in his ears:

A single syllable, yet brimming with anguish—wounded love, wounded pride, despair, the brink of madness.

Clement heard, and terror and remorse froze him worse than the water. He had driven her away—forever. In the midst of his grim triumph, a black impulse rose to curse the Church, to curse Heaven itself, for demanding such cruelty of mortal flesh.

At last, half-dead, he dragged himself from the stream and stumbled back to his cell.

"I am safe now," he groaned. "She will never return. Unmanly, ungrateful wretch that I am!" He collapsed on the floor, half-hoping never to rise again.

But glancing at the hour-glass, he saw midnight had passed. Slowly, stiffly, he peeled off his soaked garments, moaning at the pain he had caused her. Then he donned the old hermit's bristled cilice and over it the breastplate—arms he had never dared wear before, doubting his worth. Now he needed every defense: the cilice to torment his flesh, the breastplate for holy strength.

Kneeling, he prayed humbly for death that very night. Then, lighting all his candles, he recited psalms mechanically, each word leaden on his tongue. Between verses, he groaned aloud.

Then his eye caught something in the corner—a small bundle, unnoticed before. At one end gleamed threads of gold amid silk.

Curious, he crept closer—and his breath caught.

"A seraph!" he whispered. "A heavenly seraph! Heaven has witnessed my trial and approves my harshness—sending this angel to cheer me."

Kneeling in awe, he gazed at the child—golden hair, skin like dawn, cheeks like ripened fruit.

"Let me feast my sorrowing eyes on thee," he murmured, "ere thou wingest back to thy blessed home, leaving my cell darker than before."
