But his movement disturbed the little visitor. Two eyes, blue as heaven, flew open. Seeing the wild figure looming, the child whimpered, "*MUMMA! MUM-MA!*" Tears spilled down his cheeks.

Perhaps, after six months of solitude, Clement saw childish beauty more vividly than we. This fair-haired boy seemed more angelic than any painted cherub.

Yet the spell was broken—though not unhappily. Clement had always loved children, and monastic life had nurtured that tenderness. The child's innocent distress, his trembling lips and helpless cry, pierced the hermit's heart.

Softly, gently, he soothed him, and soon wonder replaced fear. The boy studied this strange, armored man who spoke so kindly.

Clement, perplexed, could only guess how this little one had come here. The open door—some desperate mother abandoning her child? The thought made his heart ache.

"Now bless thee, bless thee," he murmured. "I would not trade thee for a cherub in paradise."

"*At's pooty*," the boy declared, pointing to the breastplate.

Clement almost laughed. "What else here is pretty, little one?"

"*Ookum-gars*," the child said gravely.

"So thou likest my armor?"

"*Nay. Ay. En um ittle, ery ittle? Not ike torks. Hate torks um bigger an baby.*"

In broken words, the child confided his fear of storks—their great flapping wings, their looming size.

Clement smiled. "But my birds are tiny and sweet. Wouldst like them?"

"*Den I ikes 'm. I ont my mammy.*"

"Ah, sweet one, I fear I must serve in her stead. Hast thou no father?"

The question hung in the air—and at that very moment, unseen, a third listener stood outside, every word striking her heart like a hammer.

For Margaret, fleeing in despair, had not gone far. Love and fear for her child had drawn her back. Now she crouched by the door, breathless, eyes wide, drinking in the scene within.

The child, asked about his father, shook his head at first. Then, as if recalling something, he said, "*Dad-da ill man; run away and left poor mum-ma.*"

Margaret winced. Some meddling fool had put those words in his mouth—and now they spilled out before Gerard.

His response stunned her.

"The villain!" he burst out. "The heartless wretch—to desert thee, sweet one! Ah, he knows not what joy he cast away. Well, little dove, I must be father and mother to thee now."

Her heart swelled. He did not know. He *did not know*.

As he spoke of fetching eggs and milk for the child, teaching him to read, her eyes burned with tears.

"*I can yead*," the boy boasted.

"Can thou? All the better. We shall read good books together. And I will show thee the way to Heaven—a fair place, full of little ones like thee, waiting to love thee. Wouldst like to go there one day?"

"*Ay, along wi' my mammy.*"

"What, not without her?"

"*Nay. I ont my mammy. Where is my mammy?*"

Oh, how it cost her not to rush in!

"Fret not," he soothed. "Mayhap she will come when thou sleepest. Wilt thou be good and rest?"

"*I not eepy. Ikes to talk.*"

"Then talk we shall. What is thy name?"

"*Baby.*"

"None other?"

"*Nay.*"

"Shall I tell thee a story? Or sing thee a song?"

The child clapped his hands. "*I ikes tories. I ikes tongs.*"

"Choose, then—a song or a story?"

The boy’s face twisted in indecision. "*Ting I a tong. Nay, tell I a tory. Nay—*" His lip quivered, torn between choices.

Then, brightening: "*Ting I a tory.*"

"Sing thee a story? Well, why not?"

He shed his breastplate and rough cilice, donning a softer garment. Then, seating the boy on his knee, he took up his psaltery and played a gentle tune.

"*I ikes dat,*" the child approved.

"Then list the story."

He chanted a tale in a lilting cadence, the boy enraptured.

"*I ikes oo,*" came the sleepy murmur. "*Ot is oo? Is oo a man?*"

"*Ay, little heart—and a great sinner too.*"

"*I ikes great tingers. Ting one other tory.*"

A second story followed, then a drowsy protest: "*I is not eepy...*" before the child succumbed, eyelids fluttering shut.

Clement laid aside the psaltery and cradled him, humming a lullaby from his own childhood. As the boy sank into deep sleep, he gazed down, his face a blend of tenderness and sorrow.

A sigh escaped him.

Then—like moonlight given form—Margaret stood before him, her hand on his shoulder, her voice trembling.

"Gerard... thou dost not reject us. Thou *canst* not."

(1) More than one hermit had received such an unwanted gift.
(2) Likely "looking-glass."
(3) Craft—meaning trade or profession.

The hermit’s eyes darted wildly between the sleeping child in his arms and Margaret, who knelt before him, her face alight with a radiance he had never seen before—maternal pride. His breath came in short gasps, his mind reeling.

*"Mine?"* he whispered hoarsely, his gaze flickering between the boy and her.

Margaret laughed softly, a sound like music in the dim cell. "What, does a father not know his own flesh? A man of God, too? Fie, Gerard, to feign ignorance! Look at him—see the gold in his hair, the mole upon his finger—just like yours. Ask your mother if you doubt me!"

Clement’s hands trembled. The child stirred but did not wake, nestled against his chest. Margaret reached out, her fingers brushing the boy’s tiny hand before lifting it to her lips, pressing a fervent kiss. "He found you for me," she murmured. "When I watched you feed the birds from your window, it was this little mark that told me—*you* were his father."

A choked sob broke from Clement’s throat. The weight of solitude, of relentless fasting, had left him frail. His knees buckled, and he swayed, nearly collapsing. Margaret caught him, her touch steadying. "Hush now," she soothed. "You are not the first man to return from wandering with more than he left with."

But the truth struck deeper. Here was his son—*his*—a living bond to the life he had forsaken. And Margaret, fierce and tender, holding him to the earth with her love.

---

The moon cast silver light over the path to Gouda manse, where a single candle burned in the window. Inside, Catherine knelt in prayer, her face lined with years of grief. A sharp rap at the glass—*"Mother!"*—and before she could rise, the door burst open.

Clement fell at her feet, arms outstretched.

*"My boy!"* she cried, clutching him to her breast.

No titles mattered now—no hermit, no priest. Only a son, returned.

Margaret watched from the threshold, her heart full. The long night had ended. Dawn would find Gerard Eliassoen in his rightful place—no longer a fugitive from the world, but its shepherd.

And the child, the living thread between past and future, slept on, unknowing, in his father’s arms.

Margaret returned to Rotterdam long before Gerard awoke, leaving her boy behind—a bold stroke of maternal strategy. She dispatched the faithful Reicht to Gouda with a vicar’s gray frock and broad felt hat, armed with meticulous instructions on managing her new master.

Then she sought out Jorian Ketel, reasoning that if secrecy was the game, he was her man. Together, they executed two cunning maneuvers—neither cruel, though Margaret fancied them so. But as she carried those deeds to her grave, so shall we.

She stayed away from Gouda parsonage.

Words spoken in the heat of argument often fester in silence. Turning over their exchange, she took offense at Gerard’s assumption that she could ever confuse the priest with the lover. *What did he think of me?* Pride stiffened her resolve. *No more visits to Gouda.*

Little Gerard remained behind, a living anchor for his father’s soul—a sacrifice that tore at her heart. She had never been parted from him for an hour, and his absence left her hollow. By nightfall of the first day, she could bear it no longer. Under cover of darkness, she stole to the parsonage, lurking like a thief until she spotted Reicht alone in the kitchen. A soft tap on the window, a whispered plea: *"Bring him to me, unseen."*

Reicht obliged, and mother and child melted into each other, joy and sorrow mingling in their embrace. A plan was hatched: Reicht would bring the boy halfway each day, where Margaret would meet them. Between the laughter and gamboling, the women spoke in hushed tones—of Luke Peterson.

*"I near promised to marry him,"* Margaret confessed. *"I said, ‘Say the word, and I’ll wed him.’"*

*"Poor Luke,"* Reicht sighed.

*"You’ve no fancy for him yourself, have you?"* Margaret teased.

Reicht’s head lifted sharply. *"Me?"*

And so the plotting began—two women against one unsuspecting boy.

But after five days of stolen reunions, guilt pricked Margaret. She wept—just as a cart arrived at her door. In it sat the Vicar of Gouda, clean-shaven, bright-eyed, clad in the gray frock and hat she had sent.

She fled upstairs to scrub away tears, don a fresh cap, and compose herself before descending. He seized her hands, kissed them, a tear falling upon her skin. She turned her face away, hiding her own.

*"Sweet Margaret,"* he murmured, *"why stay apart from your own good work? We’ve waited for you every day."*

*"What—when I was a hermit and a fool? Forget my ravings. No mortal could have dragged me from that madness but you. And now, I beg you—come to Gouda."*

She feigned indifference (though her heart clamored to go). *"We’ll see. Tell me first of your flock."*

He laughed. *"Two who’d feuded for years came to me, each angling for my favor. By God’s grace, I turned them to friends instead."*

She waved a hand. *"Tell me of yourself, not others."*

*"At first, sunlight blinded me. Now Reicht—"* He caught himself.

*"Between us—her voice cuts like a blade. All voices seem loud now but yours."* He smiled. *"But here’s news: An old man swears no parish in Holland is so plagued by the devil as Gouda. ‘We had a holy hermit,’ says he, ‘but the fiend came and took him, leaving naught but a scrap of skin—like a hedgehog’s.’"*

Margaret’s lips curved.

*"Stranger still,"* Gerard mused, *"the cave collapsed. Had I resisted you, I’d lie buried there. Providence, surely—condemning my solitude, blessing my new path. What say you?"*

He relented. *"Take the cart, then. I’ll walk ahead—a man again, bearing joyful tidings."*

When she arrived at the manse, she found father and son together—the boy a whirl of motion, the man sketching patiently, though *"he’s quicksilver, impossible to fix."*

That night, by the peat fire’s glow, Gerard marveled at the beauty in simple things—the shine of brass, the linen sheets like snow. *"Dives never fretted as I do, lying in such luxury."* He turned to Margaret. *"You were ever fair, but never so bright as now."*

She scoffed.

*"And you, mother—"* He praised Catherine’s aged beauty, her eyes holding love’s quiet light.

*"Enough, flatterer,"* Catherine laughed. *"To bed—you preach at dawn."*

The next day, his sermon held the crowd rapt—two hours that felt like moments. From terror to solace, he wove scripture into their hearts. In a corner, two women wept.

*"Where did he learn such power?"* Catherine whispered.

Margaret kissed her. *"I may not be happy, mother—but I am proud."*

She vowed silently never to let her love come between Gerard and Heaven.

The storm had passed. Now, like waves after a gale, their lives still rolled, but toward inevitable calm.

Bound by love, by an irregular but binding marriage, yet severed by Church and conscience, they lived in a delicate balance. She visited Gouda often, welcomed like a queen. Catherine and Eli, drawn by Rotterdam’s bustle, left Tergou to settle near her.

Yet Margaret was not happy. Her smiles held pensiveness; at times, she snapped. If monks visited Gerard, she turned heel and left. She despised them for parting her from him—a bitterness she passed to her son.

Gerard bore it patiently. He had found his calling, loved his flock, and they adored him. His cheerfulness returned, though part was artifice—a peacemaker’s tool. He reconciled enemies with laughter, tamed animals (even a mule named Jack), and played the jester when needed.

Once, hearing a braggart claim he could outdrink any in Gouda, Gerard challenged him—then pitted him against Jack. *"Which of these asses drinks more?"*

Another time, farmers arguing over hay sought his judgment. He summoned Jack. *"Here’s the finest critic in Holland."*

Years slipped by. In 1471, Denys, marching to England’s wars, offered to marry Margaret—*"for a comrade’s honor."* She smiled, gave him silver, and sent him off.

In 1473, Sybrandt died, blessing the two who had reunited him with love.

In 1474, Luke Peterson wed Reicht—Margaret’s scheming having steered him gently away from herself.

By 1476, Gerard clashed with the Pope over doctrinal excesses, drawing Franciscan ire. Margaret, ever his counsel, guided him through storms.

And in 1477, little Gerard—spoiled by doting women—was sent to Deventer’s famed school, Margaret’s heart torn but yielding to necessity.

So passed eight years. Gerard, content in duty, was happy.

Margaret was not.

But love, tempered by sacrifice, held them—guardians of each other’s souls.

A priest is never more a priest than when standing at the threshold between this life and the next. Gerard performed the last rites for Margaret with the same solemn care he would have given the humblest soul in his parish, though his hands moved as if guided by some force beyond his own will. The shock of her passing left him numb, the full weight of grief not yet settled upon him. When at last it struck, it came like a storm—first a surge of fervent gratitude for her peaceful, faithful end, then a desperate longing to carry her away from this unfamiliar place, to lay her to rest in the earth of Gouda, where she belonged.

That very evening, a covered cart rolled slowly toward the village, its single horse plodding beneath a sky heavy with unshed tears. Inside lay a coffin, and beside it, Gerard, his arms wrapped around the cold lead as if he could will warmth back into it. The cart’s driver, a silent man with rough hands and a gentle heart, walked beside the horse, stealing glances at the broken figure within but daring not to speak. When night fell and they stopped at an inn, the driver and the landlord tried to coax Gerard away—to eat, to rest, to release his grip on death for even a moment. But he snarled at them, half-wild with sorrow, and clung tighter to the coffin until they relented, draping a cloak over his trembling shoulders and leaving him to his vigil.

At noon the next day, the cart creaked to a halt before the manse, where a cluster of parishioners had gathered, each nursing some small complaint or worry, missing their shepherd already. When they saw the coffin, and their priest hollow-eyed and shattered, a great cry went up among them—not just for Margaret, but for him. The sound roused Gerard from his daze, and he looked upon them with red-rimmed eyes. *"Oh, my children,"* he tried to say, but the words dissolved into a choked silence.

The following day, against all counsel, he buried her himself. His voice, though frayed at the edges, did not falter as he read the service, and the tears of the villagers fell like rain upon the fresh-turned earth. When the last prayer was spoken, he remained standing over the grave, motionless, until the mourners drifted away, leaving him alone with his grief.

He might have stood there till dusk, lost in memory, had the sexton not begun to shovel dirt onto the coffin without warning. The first thud of earth against wood tore a scream from Gerard’s throat—raw, inhuman. The sexton froze. Gerard swayed, clutching at his chest as if something inside had torn loose. The man rushed to steady him, calling for help, and Jorian Ketel came running, his own face streaked with tears. Together, they half-carried Gerard back to the manse.

*"Ah, Jorian,"* he gasped, *"something broke in me—I felt it snap. Here, Jorian. Here."* His hand pressed to his breast, where the pain burned brightest.

A fortnight had passed when a gaunt, weary figure crossed the threshold of the Dominican convent on the outskirts of Gouda. His face was pallid, his shoulders bent—this was Gerard, the vicar of Gouda, come not to seek refuge, but to surrender himself to the quietude of the cloister, to live out his last days as he had begun them: in devotion.

He came as a novice, though in truth, he was a man already broken, his body failing, his spirit worn thin. The weight of the world had grown too heavy, and his own home—once a sanctuary—had become a prison of memories, each corner whispering of *her*. Here, at least, he might find peace, near Brother Ambrose, his steadfast friend, and among the brothers who had long welcomed him with open arms.

Ambrose, ever compassionate, saw the ruin grief had wrought upon him and harbored a quiet hope: that with time and care, Gerard might yet be restored—not just to health, but to his parish, to his calling. To that end, he placed him in a comfortable cell above the gateway, forbidding fasts or harsh penances. Let him rest. Let him heal.

But fate was unkind.

The new prior arrived soon after, a man of iron will and little patience. His first days were spent in rooting out laxity, in reforging discipline where it had slackened. And then he learned of Gerard’s presence—a vicar turned novice. His lip curled.

“A fat parson in my house?” he scoffed. “No. Let him renounce his vicarage or leave.”

Summoned before the prior, Gerard obeyed. When the two men faced one another, recognition struck them like a thunderclap.

The air between them crackled with the weight of the past, with old wounds and older grudges. And in that moment, Gerard knew—his refuge had vanished. The battle was not yet over.

Jerome remained as stern as ever, yet a quiet change had softened his heart toward Gerard. During his time in England, he had felt the absence of his brother more than he cared to admit, and since his return, he often wondered what had become of him. The tales from Gouda had lifted Gerard in his esteem—especially the defiance of Pope and Minorites over what Jerome called *"the damnable heresy of the immaculate conception."* But when a young monk, eyes wet with tears, revealed the true cause of Gerard’s decline, the old disdain flared anew.

He resolved to halt this disgrace. Twice daily, he visited Clement in his cell, wielding all his old authority and eloquence to shake him from this earthly despair. The Church needed his piety, his voice—now more than ever. Gerard listened, praised Jerome’s logic, admired his strength, confessed his own frailty—yet still, day by day, he faded like mist before the dawn.

One afternoon, Jerome spoke bluntly. *"I know your tale—and I was proud of it. But now, Clement, you undo it all. This is not virtue; it is the victory of flesh over spirit. Better to have yielded and repented than to resist while she lived, only to surrender now."*

Clement smiled, gentle as ever. *"Dear Jerome, you wrong me. There is no passion left—only love, and I will not blush for it, not here, not in heaven. Think of the faithful hound that dies upon his master’s grave—is that beast guilty of passion? Neither am I. Passion might have saved my life, but damned my soul. She was my strength, my solace. Her face lifted me in the pulpit; her voice soothed me when men proved ungrateful. She was woven into every good deed I ever did—and when she was gone, I could not walk alone."*

He paused, then added, softer still: *"And do not think me rebellious, Jerome. It is God’s mercy that calls me. When the earth fell upon her coffin, something broke within me—something that cannot mend. I heard it snap. From that hour, bread lost its taste, meat its savor. Were my eyes bandaged now, I could not tell one from the other."*

*"Holy saints!"* Jerome muttered.

*"And from that same hour,"* Gerard continued, *"my sorrow lifted. I smile often now—do you know why? I believe it is thus: He who holds the keys of life and death touched me that moment and said, 'Enough. You have fought, you have wept. Now come home.' And so I come, gladly."*

Jerome groaned. *"The Church has ever had her gentle, fragile servants,"* he said at last. *"I would give ten years of my own life to save yours—but I see it cannot be. Die in peace."*

And so it was. In the days that followed, Gerard slipped toward death with a serenity that drew even the oldest monks to his side, eager to catch his final whispers. He spoke to Giles—begged him to care for Jack, to watch over little Gerard’s inheritance, to kiss their parents in his stead, to send Denys home to Burgundy. *"Poor soul,"* he murmured, *"he will be lost here without me."*

Then came a private hour with Jerome. None knew what passed between them, but when Jerome left, his head was bowed, his hands clasped upon his breast, his sighs heavy.

That night, the monks who kept vigil heard Gerard murmur praises, thanks, fragments of joy. Once, his voice drifted, as if in vision: *"She walks in green fields… white-robed… she smiles… she beckons…"*

Near dawn, three soft taps struck the wall—slow, deliberate. Gerard stirred. *"I come, love. I come."*

When the brothers gathered at his side, he spoke once more, clear and strong. *"Mark this well: I die holding no faith in man’s own merit. I stand with the Apostles, with the fathers of the Church—we are saved not by our works, not by our wisdom, but by faith alone."*

A hush fell. Then, at his request, they swept the floor, strewed ashes in the shape of a cross, and laid him upon it. *"Shall we stretch out your arms?"* one asked.

*"God forbid,"* he whispered. *"I am not worthy of that."*

As dawn broke, his face turned upward, rapt, his hands folded—like Margaret’s. And as the sun rose, at the very hour she had died, he breathed his last word:

Then—he was gone.

They prepared his body. Beneath his robe, they found a hair shirt. *"A saint!"* the younger monks cried. But beneath *that* lay a long, auburn tress. Horror and debate erupted—until Jerome silenced them. *"Who are you to judge?"* he thundered at the loudest accuser. *"For all you know, it is a relic. To your cell—a week’s penance for your haste."*

Then, when the coffin was sealed, Jerome laid the lock upon Gerard’s breast. *"There, Clement,"* he said—and set himself a penance for the act.

The next day, they buried him in Gouda’s churchyard, beside a leaden coffin. Jerome spoke over the grave as the people wept. Later, in the refectory, he addressed the brotherhood:

*"We have buried a saint. Feast, do not mourn—for he is free. I alone shall fast, for I wronged him in life. And you, young brothers, pry not into whether that lock was love’s token or a holy relic. Look instead to the heart that bore it—and follow his example, if you can."*

And so the lovers were united at last—not by the Church, but by the earth. A kinder fate than life had granted.

Now, as another hand sets this stone upon their memory, ask not for pity. Theirs was no tragedy. Life is ever barred by sorrow; death is but a door. If you cannot bear to hear that these two died young—who would now be dust regardless—how will you endure when your own beloved slip from your arms into the sky?

Take from their tale not grief, but strength. For the purest hearts are often gathered first—not lost, but lifted.

Though I find little virtue in the tradition of tying loose ends, I yield to it now—not out of conviction, but weariness. Let me gather what fragments remain of those who have crossed this tale, though I owe no accounting to every soul that lined Gerard’s path from Holland to Rome. They served their purpose: to prove his world was peopled not with hollow figures, but with flesh and blood.

Eli and Catherine endured to a hoary age, outliving even memory’s grasp upon their son and Margaret. Giles, too, defied time—gone to Bavaria, where at ninety he still drew breath, though little more than a trumpet-voiced specter of bone and leather. As for Cornelis, freed of rivals and long absolved by his mother (who clung to him as her scattered brood left her hollow), he waited, ever waiting, for his inheritance. But Catherine’s wit outlived his patience: rust consumes the idle blade before the whetstone wears away. At sixty-five, he shriveled in her arms—she, hale at eighty-six—and in his dying gasp, he whispered of renovations he’d make once the house was his. “Yes, my love,” she murmured, soothing a dreamer to his last sleep. His clay was borne to the grave by the very feet he’d hoped to bury.

Denys, heartbroken after Gerard’s death, returned to Burgundy. A meager pension sustained him until an inheritance fell like an unlooked-for bounty into his lap. There he lived out his days, a grizzled soldier, spinning war tales when the mood struck, but more often cursing the faithlessness of women.

Jerome, repelled by northern laxity, sought refuge in Italy. Connections hoisted him to a mitred abbacy, where he ruled with iron discipline—revered, feared, and unloved. A decade passed before he died alone, the Vulgate on his pillow, the crucifix clenched in his hand. Yet on his lips lingered something rare: a smile. Perhaps, in that final hour, he found a kindness he had never shown. *Requiescat in pace.* The Master he served employs many servants—some gentle, some stern—and even the surliest may yet find grace.

As for the fair-haired boy, Gerard Gerardson, his tale belongs not to fiction but to history. The house of his birth in Brede Kirk Straet bears the inscription: *"Hæc est parva domus natus qua magnus Erasmus,"* and volumes have been writ of him. Yet history, like a dwarf measuring a giant, has failed to grasp the fullness of Erasmus. First scholar of his age, theologian, and—unmatched in his time—a dramatist of divine spark. Scenes in this very book draw from his pen, and where they fall, they light the page. The words of such a mind do not perish. They sleep in dust until new eyes awaken them, and then they rise, they bloom, they seed again, immortal.
